Title

Opening the Mnemosyne Repository

Public devlog version

Every project starts with a small, almost invisible moment: the repository exists.

This first commit marks the beginning of Mnemosyne as a tracked software project. At this point, the codebase had not yet grown into the full local-first AI roleplay memory engine it would later become. There was no mature state engine, no context compiler, no patch ledger, no memory inspector, and no .mne bundle system yet. The important thing here was simpler: the project now had a permanent version-controlled starting point.

The initial commit also established an early licensing baseline by adding the GNU GPL text into the repository. That mattered because Mnemosyne was never just a disposable prototype. From the beginning, it was being treated as something that could grow into a serious open-source tool, with clear rules around sharing, modification, and redistribution.

In hindsight, this commit is less about features and more about intent. It created the first stable anchor for everything that followed: architecture experiments, state persistence, Souls, memory scoring, API integration, debugging tools, and eventually the long-form continuity system that became Mnemosyne’s core identity.

What changed

Created the initial GitHub commit for the Mnemosyne repository.
Added the first licensing file content, visible in the initial diff as GNU GPL license text. 
Established the first recoverable point in project history.

Why it mattered

Before this commit, Mnemosyne was only an idea or local work. After this commit, it became a project with history.

That matters for a system like Mnemosyne because the project itself is about continuity. The same way the app is designed to preserve story state, memory, and branching history, the repository needed its own reliable timeline. This commit became that first timeline marker.

Technical note

This commit should not be oversold as the beginning of the full application architecture. The commit evidence only supports repository initialization and license content. The later systems, including Tauri, React, Rust state engine, memory scoring, context compilation, patch protocol, and persistence, appear in later commits and roadmap stages.

Devlog 000, Before the Repository

Title

I Got Tired of AI RP Forgetting What Mattered

Draft blog entry

Before Mnemosyne was code, it was frustration.

I was using AI roleplay platforms and kept running into the same problem: the AI could write a dramatic scene, but it could not live through one properly. Characters changed too fast. Trust jumped too quickly. Fear disappeared because the scene needed to move forward. Emotional development compressed weeks of growth into a few messages.

At first, I thought the issue was just bad prompting. So I built my own prompt-based tracking system. It was rough, but it worked better than nothing. I had the AI stack a compact memory/status block at the end of the chat, almost like a little compile format for the scene. It tracked things like status, scene state, memories, atmosphere, physical condition, sensory details, and character position.

It was not a real engine yet. It was a hack.

But it showed me something important: the AI needed a structured memory layer outside normal prose.

The first big realization came when I looked back at one of my own RP tests and noticed the core failure clearly. The character progression was too sudden. The AI was not building trust, fear, shame, affection, or confidence step by step. It was jumping to the next “interesting” emotional state because that made the current scene easier to write.

That bothered me.

I did not just want better summaries. I wanted a system that could check whether a character had actually earned a change.

So the idea shifted from “make the AI remember more” to something sharper:

Make the AI remember like a character, not like a transcript.



That meant tracking emotional variables separately. Trust is not love. Fear is not shame. Affection is not commitment. A character can like someone and still distrust them. They can feel safe in one room and terrified in another. They can remember a small moment forever if it hit the right nerve, while forgetting whole stretches of ordinary conversation.

That became the early shape of the Soul idea.

A universal trait tracker. Something genre-agnostic enough to work for a dating sim, a Minecraft NPC, a fantasy character, a robot, or a long-form RP companion. The character would not just have static traits. Their Soul would mutate over time.

The next problem was memory.

I already did not trust platform auto-summaries. JanitorAI’s summarization felt too unreliable, so my workaround was manual: copy the whole chat, paste it into DeepSeek, and make it summarize the session myself. That worked better, but it was still external labor. It also proved the platform-level memory system was not enough.

What I wanted was not a generic summary.

I wanted the AI to know what was worth remembering.

That led to the flagging idea: important events and dialogue should be marked based on character-specific salience. A betrayal should matter more to a character who values loyalty. A small act of protection should matter more to someone whose safety needs are damaged. A casual joke might vanish, but a joke said at the exact moment a character was afraid might stick forever.

That was the real seed of Mnemosyne.

Not “save everything.”

Not “summarize everything.”

But:

Track what changed the character.



From there, I started thinking less like a roleplay user and more like someone designing a memory engine. The system needed emotional progression gates. It needed salience-based memory. It needed forgetting. It needed the ability to say, in character, “I don’t remember,” without sounding broken. It needed hidden state, visible status, and eventually a narrator architecture that could take OOC feedback without breaking immersion.

Before the repo existed, Mnemosyne was already becoming clear:

A narrator writes the scene.

A Soul remembers what mattered.

Short version for a devlog page

## Devlog 000, Before the Repository

Mnemosyne started from frustration with existing AI RP platforms. Characters could produce dramatic scenes, but their emotional progression often moved too fast. Trust, fear, affection, shame, and confidence would jump between states without enough lived experience.

I first tried solving this with prompt-based memory blocks, compact end-of-chat status data tracking scene state, physical condition, memories, atmosphere, and character status. That became the seed of a larger idea: a universal Soul system that could track emotional growth, memory salience, and forgetting across any RP or interaction sim.

The key insight was that AI memory should not simply summarize everything. It should remember what changed the character.

What this entry establishes

This is the true origin point, before the first commit:

Problem:
Existing AI RP platforms produced rushed emotional progression and unreliable summaries.

Early workaround:
Manual prompt/status blocks and external DeepSeek summarization.

Core insight:
A good RP memory system should track character change, not just chat history.

Emerging system idea:
A universal Soul/memory engine with emotional gates, salience flags, forgetting, and narrator-friendly state tracking.

Ready for the next file when you are.

This is a strategy question with current platform rules, so I’m going to check the live monetization requirements before recommending a route. The main decision is “own your domain long-term” vs “borrow audience from platforms.”

For your Mnemosyne dev-adventure blog, I would not start with AdSense as the main revenue plan.

Best setup:

Primary home: your own domain + blog
Audience/reach: cross-post to Medium, Dev.to, Hashnode, Reddit/HN later
Revenue later: AdSense, sponsorships, paid newsletter, consulting/internship credibility

My recommendation

1. Build the canonical blog on your own domain

Use:

mnemosyne.dev
junhwa.dev
rhy.gpu/devlog
mnemosyne.ai if available/affordable

Best platform options:

Easy + professional: Ghost
Developer-style: Astro / Next.js / Hugo on GitHub Pages, Vercel, or Cloudflare Pages
Simplest: WordPress

For your case, I’d pick:

Astro + Markdown + GitHub repo + custom domain

Reason: your blog is part of the project. A devlog about vibe-coding, memory engines, AI RP systems, Git commits, and roadmap evolution looks more serious when it lives beside the project, not only on a rented platform.

2. Cross-post elsewhere for reach

Your own site will be slow at first. So publish the canonical version on your site, then repost shorter versions to:

Medium
Dev.to
Hashnode
Substack
Reddit, selectively
Hacker News, only when there is a serious technical post
LinkedIn, for career visibility

The dev audience is not going to find a brand-new domain automatically. Cross-posting gives distribution while your own domain stays the “official archive.”

3. AdSense is possible, but not the first money lever

Google’s official AdSense requirements say you need your own unique/interesting content, policy-compliant pages, access to the site HTML, and you must be at least 18 to apply. If under 18, Google says a parent/guardian can sign up with their own Google Account and payments go to the adult responsible for the site. (구글 도움말)

AdSense also has strict policy risks: no artificial clicks, no asking people to click ads, no misleading placement, no deceptive traffic, no popups/emails/software placements, and Google can disable ad serving or the account for violations. (구글 도움말)

So AdSense is fine later, but early traffic will probably make tiny money. The bigger value at first is:

portfolio credibility
developer reputation
SEO footprint
proof of shipping
job/internship signal
future users/testers

4. Better monetization order

For Mnemosyne, I’d think in this order:

Stage 1, now:
Build public dev history and credibility.

Stage 2, after 10-20 good posts:
Add newsletter signup.

Stage 3, after steady traffic:
Apply for AdSense or another ad network.

Stage 4, after real audience:
Sponsorships, donations, paid supporter posts, consulting, or early-access builds.

Stage 5, after product maturity:
Paid hosted sync, premium tools, templates, marketplace, or pro features.

Platform verdict

Best canonical home:
Own domain + static blog.

Best discovery:
Dev.to / Hashnode / Medium cross-posts.

Best newsletter monetization:
Substack or Ghost.

Best long-term control:
Ghost or self-hosted/static site.

Best quick start:
Hashnode or Medium.

Substack can monetize with paid subscriptions, but it takes a 10% platform cut on subscription revenue, plus payment processing. Recent reporting also shows some writers are leaving Substack because that percentage becomes expensive at scale and because they want more control. (The Verge)

My blunt answer

Start here:

1. Buy a domain.
2. Make a simple Astro/Markdown blog.
3. Publish “Devlog 000, Before the Repository.”
4. Publish one commit-based devlog at a time.
5. Cross-post cleaned versions to Dev.to, Hashnode, and Medium.
6. Add newsletter capture.
7. Add AdSense only after there is real traffic and policy-safe content.

For revenue, do not rely on AdSense first. For this type of project, your dev blog is more valuable as a career asset and audience builder than as an ad farm.

Also add:

www.rhygpu.dev

So both work:

https://rhygpu.dev
https://www.rhygpu.dev

Usually Cloudflare Pages will ask you to confirm or activate DNS records. Accept the defaults.

What not to touch

Ignore these for now:

Wrangler CLI
Resources
Workers
R2
AI
Bot protection

You only need:

Pages project → Custom domains → rhygpu.dev

After domain connects

Open:

https://rhygpu.dev

It may take a few minutes. If it says pending DNS or certificate pending, that is normal.

Your milestone now:

Domain bought: done
Cloudflare Pages deployed: done
Custom domain: next

Click Add custom domain.

chatgpt response

404면 도메인은 연결됐는데 Pages 프로젝트가 그 주소를 제대로 받지 못했거나, 배포된 루트에 index.html이 없는 상태일 가능성이 큽니다.

먼저 이것부터 확인하세요.

1. 임시 주소는 열리나요?

열어보기:

https://rhygpu-dev.pages.dev

A. 이것도 404면

문제는 도메인 연결이 아니라 배포 파일입니다.

GitHub repo 루트에 이 파일이 있어야 합니다:

index.html

그리고 Cloudflare Pages build settings가 이렇게 되어 있어야 합니다:

Framework preset: None
Build command: blank
Build output directory: /
Root directory: blank

B. rhygpu-dev.pages.dev는 열리는데 rhygpu.dev만 404면

문제는 custom domain 연결입니다.

Cloudflare에서:

Workers & Pages
→ rhygpu-dev
→ Custom domains

여기에 반드시 있어야 합니다:

rhygpu.dev
Active

없으면 Add custom domain 누르고 rhygpu.dev 추가하세요.

2. DNS 확인

Cloudflare dashboard에서:

Websites
→ rhygpu.dev
→ DNS
→ Records

이런 레코드가 있어야 합니다:

Type: CNAME
Name: @ 또는 rhygpu.dev
Target: rhygpu-dev.pages.dev
Proxy: Proxied

또는 Cloudflare가 Pages용 자동 레코드를 만들어놨을 수도 있습니다.

3. www도 추가

Pages 프로젝트에서 custom domain을 하나 더 추가:

www.rhygpu.dev

그러면 나중에 둘 다 작동합니다:

https://rhygpu.dev
https://www.rhygpu.dev

4. 잠깐 기다려야 할 수도 있음

방금 연결했다면 SSL/DNS 적용 때문에 몇 분 걸릴 수 있습니다. 그래도 404는 보통 “DNS 전파”보다는 “Pages custom domain이 아직 프로젝트에 제대로 붙지 않음” 쪽입니다.

지금 바로 할 것

순서대로:

1. https://rhygpu-dev.pages.dev 열어보기
2. 열리면 Cloudflare Pages → rhygpu-dev → Custom domains 확인
3. rhygpu.dev가 없으면 Add custom domain
4. rhygpu.dev가 Active인지 확인
5. www.rhygpu.dev도 추가

가장 중요한 확인은 이거예요:

rhygpu-dev.pages.dev 자체가 뜨는지?

그게 뜨면 사이트 배포는 성공이고, custom domain만 고치면 됩니다.

chatgpt response

맞아. 이건 내가 먼저 말했어야 했어.
Cloudflare Pages는 빈 사이트를 만들어주는 게 아니라, 네 repo 안에 있는 파일을 그대로 배포하는 방식이라서 index.html이 없으면 404가 나는 게 정상임.

지금 상태는 망한 게 아니라:

도메인 구매: 성공
Cloudflare Pages 프로젝트 생성: 성공
문제: 배포할 index.html이 없음

제일 빠른 해결법

네가 Cloudflare Pages에 연결한 GitHub repo로 가서 index.html 하나 만들면 됨.

1. GitHub에서 연결한 repo 열기

Cloudflare Pages 만들 때 연결한 repo로 가.

아마 이름이 이런 식일 수 있음:

rhygpu-dev
rhygpu.dev
rhygpu-site

2. Add file 누르기

GitHub repo 화면에서:

→ Create new file

3. 파일 이름

파일 이름을 정확히 이렇게 입력:

index.html

루트에 있어야 함. 폴더 안에 넣으면 안 됨.

좋은 구조:

repo/
  index.html

나쁜 구조:

repo/
  public/
    index.html

지금 Cloudflare build output이 /라서 루트에 있어야 함.

    <a class="back" href="/">← Back to RhyGPU</a>

    <h1>Devlog 000, Before the Repository</h1>
    <div class="meta">May 2026 · Origin notes · Mnemosyne</div>

    <p>
      Before Mnemosyne was code, it was frustration.
    </p>

    <p>
      I was using AI roleplay platforms and kept running into the same problem:
      the AI could write a dramatic scene, but it could not live through one properly.
      Characters changed too fast. Trust jumped too quickly. Fear disappeared because
      the scene needed to move forward. Emotional development compressed weeks of growth
      into a few messages.
    </p>

    <p>
      At first, I thought the issue was just bad prompting. So I built my own
      prompt-based tracking system. It was rough, but it worked better than nothing.
      I had the AI stack a compact memory and status block at the end of the chat,
      almost like a compile format for the scene. It tracked things like status,
      scene state, memories, atmosphere, physical condition, sensory details, and
      character position.
    </p>

    <p>
      It was not a real engine yet. It was a hack.
    </p>

    <p>
      But it showed me something important: the AI needed a structured memory layer
      outside normal prose.
    </p>

    <p>
      The first big realization came when I looked back at one of my own tests and
      noticed the core failure clearly. The progression was too sudden. The AI was
      not building trust, fear, shame, affection, or confidence step by step. It was
      jumping to the next interesting emotional state because that made the current
      scene easier to write.
    </p>

    <p>
      That bothered me.
    </p>

    <p>
      I did not just want better summaries. I wanted a system that could check whether
      a character had actually earned a change.
    </p>

    <blockquote>
      Make the AI remember like a character, not like a transcript.
    </blockquote>

    <p>
      That meant tracking emotional variables separately. Trust is not love. Fear is
      not shame. Affection is not commitment. A character can like someone and still
      distrust them. They can feel safe in one room and terrified in another. They
      can remember a small moment forever if it hit the right nerve, while forgetting
      whole stretches of ordinary conversation.
    </p>

    <p>
      That became the early shape of the Soul idea.
    </p>

    <p>
      A universal trait tracker. Something genre-agnostic enough to work for a dating
      sim, a Minecraft NPC, a fantasy character, a robot, or a long-form RP companion.
      The character would not just have static traits. Their Soul would mutate over time.
    </p>

    <p>
      The next problem was memory.
    </p>

    <p>
      I already did not trust platform auto-summaries. My workaround was manual:
      copy the whole chat, paste it into another model, and make it summarize the
      session myself. That worked better, but it was still external labor. It also
      proved the platform-level memory system was not enough.
    </p>

    <p>
      What I wanted was not a generic summary.
    </p>

    <p>
      I wanted the AI to know what was worth remembering.
    </p>

    <p>
      That led to the flagging idea: important events and dialogue should be marked
      based on character-specific salience. A betrayal should matter more to a character
      who values loyalty. A small act of protection should matter more to someone whose
      safety needs are damaged. A casual joke might vanish, but a joke said at the exact
      moment a character was afraid might stick forever.
    </p>

    <p>
      That was the real seed of Mnemosyne.
    </p>

    <blockquote>
      Not “save everything.” Not “summarize everything.” Track what changed the character.
    </blockquote>

    <p>
      From there, I started thinking less like a roleplay user and more like someone
      designing a memory engine. The system needed emotional progression gates. It needed
      salience-based memory. It needed forgetting. It needed the ability to say, in character,
      “I don’t remember,” without sounding broken. It needed hidden state, visible status,
      and eventually a narrator architecture that could take directorial feedback without
      breaking immersion.
    </p>

    <p>
      Before the repository existed, Mnemosyne was already becoming clear:
    </p>

    <blockquote>
      The narrator writes the scene. The Soul remembers what mattered.
    </blockquote>

    <hr />

    <p class="note">
      Next: Devlog 001, the first commit.
    </p>
  </main>
</body>

아래가 Devlog 000 첫 글 새 버전이야. 이건 바로 블로그에 올릴 수 있게 영어 글로 썼고, 너무 과장된 스타트업 톤 말고 “혼자 삽질하다가 진짜 시스템으로 변한 개발 기록” 느낌으로 잡았음.

Devlog 000, Before the Repository

I Got Tired of AI Forgetting What Mattered

Before Mnemosyne was a repository, it was a frustration I kept running into.

I was using AI roleplay platforms, trying different bots, different prompts, different formats, and eventually different AI coding tools. At first, I thought the problem was just that I needed a better prompt. Then I realized the problem was deeper.

The AI could write a dramatic scene.

It could imitate emotion.

It could produce a good moment.

But it could not always live through that moment.

Characters changed too fast. Trust jumped too quickly. Fear disappeared because the scene needed to move forward. Shame got solved by one good speech. A character could go from guarded to devoted in a handful of messages, not because the relationship had earned it, but because the model wanted to satisfy the current direction of the scene.

That broke the illusion for me.

I did not want a character that only reacted well in the moment. I wanted a character that carried the weight of what had happened before.

At first, my solution was crude. I built prompt-based tracking systems. I had the AI append a compact status block at the end of the chat, almost like a little memory compile format. It tracked scene state, character condition, atmosphere, sensory details, emotional residue, and important memories.

It was not a real engine.

It was a hack.

But it worked better than nothing, and that bothered me even more. If a rough prompt block could make the experience better, then the real missing piece was not prose. The missing piece was structure.

The AI needed memory outside normal narration.

Not just a summary. Not just a longer context window. Not just “remember this character likes coffee.”

I wanted something closer to a Soul.

A system that could track what changed inside a character over time.

Trust should not be the same as affection. Fear should not be the same as shame. Attraction should not be the same as commitment. A character can like someone and still distrust them. They can feel safe in one place and terrified in another. They can forget an ordinary conversation but remember one small sentence forever because it hit the right wound.

That became the first real shape of the idea.

A universal character state system.

Something that could work for a dating sim, a Minecraft NPC, a fantasy companion, a robot, a villain, or a long-form roleplay character. The genre did not matter. The foundation was the same: a character should not be a static prompt. A character should evolve.

The second problem was memory quality.

I did not trust platform auto-summaries. They often flattened the wrong things, skipped important emotional turns, or preserved facts without preserving meaning. My workaround was manual: copy the whole chat, paste it into another model, and ask it to summarize the session myself.

That worked better, but it was still manual labor.

It also proved something important: a memory system should not save everything equally.

Humans do not remember like databases. We forget most things. We keep fragments. We keep emotional landmarks. We remember what surprised us, hurt us, helped us, threatened us, or changed how we saw someone.

So the idea shifted again.

Mnemosyne was not supposed to be a transcript machine.

It was supposed to track what mattered.

That led to the flagging idea. Important events and dialogue should be marked based on character-specific salience. A betrayal matters more to a character who values loyalty. A small act of protection matters more to someone who has not felt safe. A casual joke might vanish, but the same joke said during fear or grief might become unforgettable.

The system needed to decide what a character would remember, what they would misremember, and what they would forget.

It also needed to let characters grow slowly.

No instant trust.

No instant healing.

No emotional teleportation.

If a relationship changed, the system had to ask: was there enough time, enough evidence, enough pressure, enough consistency? If not, the character should resist the change, even if the scene wanted it.

That was when the project stopped being “I need a better RP prompt.”

It became:

What would it take to make AI character memory feel human?



From there, the shape of Mnemosyne started to form.

A narrator writes the scene.

A state engine watches what changes.

A Soul remembers what mattered.

At this point, there was no polished app. No clean UI. No roadmap. No alpha. No domain. No proper repository history yet.

There was only the feeling that the current platforms were not enough, and that maybe the fix was not another character card.

Maybe it was an engine.

That is where Mnemosyne started.

Not with code.

With the suspicion that AI characters needed a memory system built around change, not chat history.


Short excerpt for homepage

Mnemosyne started because existing AI roleplay platforms could write dramatic scenes, but could not reliably preserve emotional continuity. Characters changed too fast, memories flattened into generic summaries, and important moments were often treated the same as throwaway dialogue.

The first idea was simple: make the AI remember like a character, not like a transcript.

Best subtitle options

Building an AI memory engine from frustration, prompts, and too many broken roleplays.

or:

How a prompt hack turned into a local-first AI roleplay memory engine.

or:

The origin of Mnemosyne, before the repo, before the roadmap, before the app.

My pick:

How a prompt hack turned into a local-first AI roleplay memory engine.

Devlog 000, Before the Repository
Title

I Got Tired of AI RP Forgetting What Mattered

Draft blog entry

Before Mnemosyne was code, it was frustration.

I was using AI roleplay platforms and kept running into the same problem: the AI could write a dramatic scene, but it could not live through one properly. Characters changed too fast. Trust jumped too quickly. Fear disappeared because the scene needed to move forward. Emotional development compressed weeks of growth into a few messages.

At first, I thought the issue was just bad prompting. So I built my own prompt-based tracking system. It was rough, but it worked better than nothing. I had the AI stack a compact memory/status block at the end of the chat, almost like a little compile format for the scene. It tracked things like status, scene state, memories, atmosphere, physical condition, sensory details, and character position.

It was not a real engine yet. It was a hack.

But it showed me something important: the AI needed a structured memory layer outside normal prose.

The first big realization came when I looked back at one of my own RP tests and noticed the core failure clearly. The character progression was too sudden. The AI was not building trust, fear, shame, affection, or confidence step by step. It was jumping to the next “interesting” emotional state because that made the current scene easier to write.

That bothered me.

I did not just want better summaries. I wanted a system that could check whether a character had actually earned a change.

So the idea shifted from “make the AI remember more” to something sharper:

Make the AI remember like a character, not like a transcript.

That meant tracking emotional variables separately. Trust is not love. Fear is not shame. Affection is not commitment. A character can like someone and still distrust them. They can feel safe in one room and terrified in another. They can remember a small moment forever if it hit the right nerve, while forgetting whole stretches of ordinary conversation.

That became the early shape of the Soul idea.

A universal trait tracker. Something genre-agnostic enough to work for a dating sim, a Minecraft NPC, a fantasy character, a robot, or a long-form RP companion. The character would not just have static traits. Their Soul would mutate over time.

The next problem was memory.

I already did not trust platform auto-summaries. JanitorAI’s summarization felt too unreliable, so my workaround was manual: copy the whole chat, paste it into DeepSeek, and make it summarize the session myself. That worked better, but it was still external labor. It also proved the platform-level memory system was not enough.

What I wanted was not a generic summary.

I wanted the AI to know what was worth remembering.

That led to the flagging idea: important events and dialogue should be marked based on character-specific salience. A betrayal should matter more to a character who values loyalty. A small act of protection should matter more to someone whose safety needs are damaged. A casual joke might vanish, but a joke said at the exact moment a character was afraid might stick forever.

That was the real seed of Mnemosyne.

Not “save everything.”

Not “summarize everything.”

But:

Track what changed the character.

From there, I started thinking less like a roleplay user and more like someone designing a memory engine. The system needed emotional progression gates. It needed salience-based memory. It needed forgetting. It needed the ability to say, in character, “I don’t remember,” without sounding broken. It needed hidden state, visible status, and eventually a narrator architecture that could take OOC feedback without breaking immersion.

Before the repo existed, Mnemosyne was already becoming clear:

A narrator writes the scene.

A Soul remembers what mattered.

Devlog 000, Before the Repository

I Got Tired of AI RP Forgetting What Mattered

Before Mnemosyne was code, it was frustration.

I was using AI roleplay platforms and kept running into the same problem: the AI could write a dramatic scene, but it could not live through one properly. Characters changed too fast. Trust jumped too quickly. Fear disappeared because the scene needed to move forward. Emotional development compressed weeks of growth into a few messages.

At first, I did not think I needed to build an engine.

I thought I just needed a better prompt.

So I made my own crude system. It was basically a code block the AI would stack at the end of the chat, a compact tracker for the current scene. It held things like status, scene information, memories, atmosphere, physical condition, sensory details, and character position.

It was rough.

It was not software.

It was not a state engine.

It was just a prompt-based memory and status block, duct-taped onto the end of a roleplay.

But it worked better than nothing.

And that was what bothered me.

If a crude code block could improve continuity, then the problem was not just the quality of the writing. The problem was that the AI had no reliable structure for carrying the scene forward. It could imitate emotional continuity, but it did not really track it.

That made the existing platforms feel worse.

JanitorAI, Character.AI, and other AI RP services had the same basic weakness in different costumes. The memory was bad, the summaries were unreliable, and somehow the systems that were supposed to fix those problems were often the things placed behind limits, subscriptions, or platform control.

The more I used them, the more it felt like I was paying around the problem instead of solving it.

Then there were the “power user” tools.

SillyTavern was better in some ways. It gave more control. It could handle memory and context better if you were willing to configure things, feed it lore, manage summaries, and burn tokens. But even there, something felt missing.

It remembered more.

But remembering more was not the same as being more human.

A character could have a longer context and still feel hollow. A system could keep more facts and still fail to understand which ones mattered. More tokens did not automatically create a soul. Sometimes it just meant the model had a bigger pile of text to drag behind it.

That was when the problem started becoming clearer to me.

The AI did not just need more memory.

It needed judgment.

The first big realization came when I looked back at one of my own RP tests and noticed the core failure clearly. The character progression was too sudden. The AI was not building trust, fear, shame, affection, or confidence step by step. It was jumping to the next “interesting” emotional state because that made the current scene easier to write.

That bothered me.

I did not just want better summaries. I wanted a system that could check whether a character had actually earned a change.

So the idea shifted from “make the AI remember more” to something sharper:

Make the AI remember like a character, not like a transcript.

That meant tracking emotional variables separately. Trust is not love. Fear is not shame. Affection is not commitment. A character can like someone and still distrust them. They can feel safe in one room and terrified in another. They can remember a small moment forever if it hit the right nerve, while forgetting whole stretches of ordinary conversation.

That became the early shape of the Soul idea.

Not as a finished architecture at first. More like a suspicion.

A character should not just be a static prompt. A character should have a changing internal state. Something persistent enough to survive beyond the current message, but compact enough that the AI could actually use it.

The crude code block was the first version of that idea.

The Soul came later.

The next problem was memory quality.

I already did not trust platform auto-summaries. JanitorAI’s summarization felt too unreliable, so my workaround was manual: copy the whole chat, paste it into DeepSeek, and make it summarize the session myself. That worked better, but it was still external labor. It also proved the platform-level memory system was not enough.

What I wanted was not a generic summary.

I wanted the AI to know what was worth remembering.

That led to the flagging idea: important events and dialogue should be marked based on character-specific salience. A betrayal should matter more to a character who values loyalty. A small act of protection should matter more to someone whose safety needs are damaged. A casual joke might vanish, but a joke said at the exact moment a character was afraid might stick forever.

That was the real seed of Mnemosyne.

Not “save everything.”

Not “summarize everything.”

But:

Track what changed the character.

From there, I started thinking less like a roleplay user and more like someone designing a memory system. The system needed emotional progression gates. It needed salience-based memory. It needed forgetting. It needed the ability to say, in character, “I don’t remember,” without sounding broken.

At first, all of that still lived inside prompts and code blocks.

Then it started to feel too big for prompts.

The status block wanted to become a state layer. The summaries wanted to become memory objects. The emotional progression rules wanted to become validation. The character file wanted to become something closer to a Soul.

Before the repo existed, Mnemosyne was already becoming clear:

A narrator writes the scene.

A Soul remembers what mattered.

And the crude code block at the end of the chat was the first ugly proof that the idea could work.

At first, all of that still lived inside prompts and code blocks.

Then it started to feel too big for prompts.

The status block wanted to become a state layer. The summaries wanted to become memory objects. The emotional progression rules wanted to become validation. But I still did not have a clean shape for it. I just knew the crude code block was trying to become something else.

Then I saw the Open Claw / boom video.

I do not remember it as some grand technical revelation. It was more like one of those stupidly simple moments where the idea that had been floating around finally snaps into a usable form.

Soul.md.

That was the part that hit me.

I did not need the character to be only a prompt. I did not need the memory to be only a summary. I could treat the character’s inner continuity like a living file. Something readable. Something editable. Something that could carry state, scars, preferences, memories, fear, trust, and the slow changes that happen after enough scenes.

Not a full engine yet.

Not some perfect architecture.

Just a file.

But that file changed the direction of the whole idea.

The crude status block at the end of the chat was no longer just a hack. It became the first ugly version of what Soul.md could become: a place where the character’s internal life could persist outside the immediate scene.

That was when Mnemosyne started becoming clearer.

A narrator writes the scene.

A Soul remembers what mattered.

And somewhere between frustration, prompt hacks, broken platform memory, and that Soul.md moment, the project stopped feeling like a better roleplay prompt.

It started feeling like something I could actually build.

Devlog 001, When the Prompt Tried to Become an Engine

I Learned the AI Was Not the Database

After the Soul.md idea hit, I still did not have an app.

What I had was a prompt.

A very ambitious prompt.

At that point, I was still trying to make the AI manage its own memory inside the conversation. The system was supposed to write the scene, track the character’s state, flag important moments, encode hidden memory data, and carry it forward into the next turn.

It sounded clever.

For a while, it even looked like it was working.

The AI would append a hidden state block at the end of its replies. Inside that block, it stored compact memory entries: what happened, how important it was, what emotional tag it belonged to, and how fresh the memory still felt.

That was the idea, at least.

In theory, the prompt could tell the AI:

Keep only the important memories.

Let weaker memories fade.

Cull old entries.

Do not let the state grow forever.

Track trust slowly.

Do not let emotional progression jump too fast.

Do not give the character knowledge they should not have.

Do not speak for the user.

Follow the lore.

Listen when I say OOC.

It was a lot.

And the crazy part is, some of it worked.

The narrator version was much better than making the AI “be” the character directly. Instead of the AI pretending to be the character and accidentally stealing the user’s voice, the narrator acted more like a writer or GM. It described the character in third person. It could show physical reactions, internal thoughts, sensory details, and the status of the scene without fully collapsing into either the user or the character.

That felt important.

It solved one of the problems I had with normal AI RP platforms. Most systems treat the AI as the character. That can work for simple chats, but it gets messy when the scene becomes complicated. The AI starts speaking for the user. It gives the character knowledge they should not have. It breaks immersion. It forgets what the character actually knows and starts acting like an omniscient assistant.

So I moved toward a narrator model.

The narrator writes the scene.

The character remains inside the scene.

The user remains outside the character.

That distinction mattered.

But then the memory system started showing cracks.

The hidden state was getting long.

Very long.

At first, that felt like progress. More memory meant more continuity, right?

No.

That was the trap.

The AI was not forgetting. It was just accumulating.

Every turn added more memory entries. The retrieval strength values stayed high. Old memories did not naturally decay. The AI could describe a decay rule, but it did not consistently execute it. It could say “keep only the top memories,” but unless I directly forced it, it would often keep expanding the list.

That was the moment I realized something uncomfortable:

The prompt was asking the AI to behave like a program.

But the AI was not running code.

It was predicting text.

A system prompt could say “reduce every memory score by 5,” but the model was not actually looping through a data structure. It was looking at the previous hidden state and generating something that looked like the next hidden state. If the previous pattern showed ten memories, the next response tended to continue that pattern.

It was not malicious.

It was not stupid.

It was just not a state engine.

That distinction cost me API money.

I had to regenerate messages just to get the hidden state back after changing the system prompt mid-session. I tried hotfixes. I tried OOC cleanup commands. Sometimes they worked. Sometimes they only worked because the instruction was immediate and explicit, not because the system was actually reliable.

That was frustrating.

But it was also useful.

Because that failure taught me the boundary.

A single LLM can write a convincing state snapshot.

It can roughly track a few relationship values.

It can maintain a short summary.

It can feel like it remembers.

But it cannot reliably be the database, the rules engine, the memory scorer, the narrator, and the character psychologist all at once.

Not without external structure.

That changed the project.

I stopped thinking of Mnemosyne as “a better prompt.”

I started thinking of it as a split system.

The LLM should do what it is good at:

Write.

Improvise.

Narrate.

React.

Give texture to the scene.

But memory should live somewhere else.

The character’s Soul should not be a blob of text buried inside the chat. It should be a file. Something external. Something the system can read, update, compress, validate, and carry across sessions.

That led to the first real architecture idea:

The LLM is not the memory.

The LLM is the writer reading from memory.

That sounds simple, but it changed everything.

Now memory could become structured.

Not just “what happened,” but what mattered.

A character memory system could separate recent memories from core memories. A mundane repeated event could fade or merge into a routine. A major event could become part of the character’s identity. Repetition could either become boring and vanish, or become ingrained if it formed a pattern.

That felt closer to how people actually remember.

You do not remember every ordinary meal.

You remember the meal where someone confessed something.

You do not remember every walk home.

You remember the one where something changed.

You do not remember every sentence someone said.

You remember the one that stayed.

So the memory system needed a scoreboard.

Not flat decay.

Not “everything gets weaker over time.”

That was too dumb.

The system needed to ask:

Was this emotionally intense?

Was it new?

Did it violate expectations?

Did it connect to the character’s needs or fears?

Was it repeated in a boring way, or repeated in a way that became part of them?

That became the beginning of the Soul engine idea.

The Soul would track character memory.

But that was only half the problem.

The story itself also needed memory.

Not the character’s emotional memory, but the outside-world continuity: where the scene is, what objects exist, what the active plot is, what has recently happened, what the environment is like, what the character is carrying, what danger is nearby.

That became the second split:

Soul memory for the character.

World log for the story.

The Soul remembers like a person.

The World Log remembers like a GM.

That was the first architecture that felt genuinely right.

It also solved the token problem in a cleaner way. Feeding the entire chat history to the AI was wasteful. It was expensive, slow, and eventually stupid. A long interaction should not require dragging every old message forward forever.

Instead, the AI should get a briefing.

A compact current-state packet.

Character memory.

World state.

Recent messages.

Then the LLM writes the next scene from that.

That was when Mnemosyne stopped being a prompt experiment and started becoming an engine.

Not because the prompt worked perfectly.

Because it failed in exactly the way that showed me what had to exist outside the prompt.

The AI could be the narrator.

But Mnemosyne had to be the memory.


Possible shorter title options

Devlog 001, I Learned the AI Was Not the Database

Devlog 001, The Prompt That Tried to Become an Engine

Devlog 001, When Hidden State Broke

My pick:

Devlog 001, I Learned the AI Was Not the Database

That title is stronger and clearer for a dev blog.

Devlog 001, Trying to Give the Soul a Psyche

The Code Block Was Not Just a Summary Anymore

After the Soul.md idea hit, I started looking at my crude status block differently.

Before that, the code block was just a workaround.

It was something the AI stacked at the end of a roleplay so the next response would not completely lose track of the scene. It tracked things like status, memories, location, emotional state, and physical condition.

Useful, but ugly.

But around this point, I started realizing that the code block could be more than a summary.

It could be an update packet.

That was the shift.

The AI did not need to carry the whole character inside the chat. Instead, the AI could report what changed, and another system could use that report to update the Soul.

That felt important.

The code block was no longer just “memory at the bottom of the message.” It was becoming the bridge between narration and persistent character state.

The narrator writes what happens.

The code block reports what changed.

The Soul absorbs the change.

That was the first version of the architecture in my head.

But then I ran into the harder question:

What exactly should the Soul contain?

I did not want it to be just a list of facts. That would be the same mistake every other memory system makes. A list of facts can tell you what happened, but it does not tell you how the character changed.

So I started thinking about the character less like a bot and more like a psyche.

Not perfectly. Not academically. I was not pretending to be a neuroscientist.

But I wanted the foundation to at least respect how people seem to work.

People do not remember everything equally.

They remember what hurt.

They remember what comforted them.

They remember what violated their expectations.

They remember what touched a fear, a desire, a need, or an old wound.

They forget boring repetition.

But repetition can also become ingrained if it forms a pattern.

That contradiction was interesting to me.

A repeated meal is forgettable.

A repeated kindness can become trust.

A repeated threat can become fear.

A repeated silence can become abandonment.

So memory could not just decay randomly.

It had to decay based on meaning.

That was when I started asking DeepSeek to help research human psychology, memory, emotional states, and how people retain or lose experiences. I was trying to turn that research into a foundation for the Soul.

The early ideas were messy, but the direction was clear.

The Soul needed basic emotional variables.

Trust.

Fear.

Shame.

Affection.

Openness.

Attachment.

Desire.

Resolve.

It needed trauma phases, because a character should not recover from something severe just because the user says one comforting sentence.

It needed needs, because a person who does not feel safe should not behave like someone who is ready for love, ambition, or self-actualization.

It needed memory salience, because some events should vanish quickly while others should become permanent scars or anchors.

I was not trying to make the AI “remember more.”

I was trying to make it remember with bias.

Human bias.

Emotional bias.

Mind-state bias.

A scared character remembers threats more easily.

A lonely character overvalues kindness.

A ashamed character misreads neutral silence as rejection.

A traumatized character might forget details but keep the body reaction.

That was the kind of memory I wanted.

Not clean database memory.

Human memory.

Messy, selective, unfair, and sometimes wrong.

The more I thought about it, the more the Soul stopped feeling like a character card.

A character card says:

This is who the character is.

A Soul says:

This is what the character has become.

That distinction became the foundation.

A static prompt could describe someone as guarded.

But a Soul could explain why they became guarded, what weakens that guard, what reinforces it, and which memories still shape their reactions.

That meant the code block had to become structured enough to feed the Soul.

Not just:

“She is scared.”

But:

What caused the fear?

Was it new or familiar?

Did it connect to an old wound?

Did it increase distrust?

Did it reinforce a schema?

Was it intense enough to become a core memory?

Should it fade after the scene, or stay?

That was the beginning of the memory scoring idea.

I did not have the final engine yet.

I did not have the full app.

I did not even have the clean architecture.

But I had the shape of the problem:

The AI should not be trusted to remember everything by itself.

It should describe the scene and report what changed.

Then the Soul should decide what that change means.

That was the point where Mnemosyne started moving away from prompt engineering and toward a real system.

The prompt was still there.

The code block was still there.

But now I was designing the thing that would read it.

A system that could take the crude code block, interpret the emotional and psychological changes, and update the Soul.

That was the first real Mnemosyne engine idea:

Not an AI that remembers everything.

A Soul that changes like a person.

이 버전은 이전보다 훨씬 더 네 실제 흐름에 가까워.

Devlog 000:

기존 RP 플랫폼 기억력에 질림.

Soul.md 아이디어가 꽂힘.

인간적인 psyche를 설계하려 함.
DeepSeek로 인간 심리, 감정, 기억 방식을 조사함.
기억은 flat decay가 아니라 emotional/mind-state bias로 처리돼야 한다고 느낌.

그리고 Devlog 002에서 가면 됨:

"Then I Learned the AI Was Not the Database"

거기서 hidden state가 길어지고, decay/culling이 안 되고, API 돈을 태우고, 결국 외부 state engine이 필요하다는 깨달음으로 넘어가면 흐름이 훨씬 자연스러움.

A character who does not feel safe should not behave like someone ready for intimacy, ambition, or self-actualization. A lonely character might overvalue kindness. A frightened character might remember threats more easily. A ashamed character might misread neutral silence as rejection.

That was the kind of memory I wanted.

Not clean database memory.

Human memory.

Devlog 002, I Learned the AI Was Not the Database

The Hidden State Worked, Until It Didn’t

After I started thinking of the code block as an update packet, I tried to push the prompt system harder.

At that point, the idea was still running inside a normal chat.

The narrator would write the scene.
Then, at the bottom of the message, it would append a hidden state block.
That block would carry the compact data the Soul needed.

In theory, this was elegant.

The AI could narrate naturally, then quietly record what changed.

A moment could be flagged as a memory.
A relationship value could shift.
A fear could rise.
A trust value could move slowly.
A scene status could carry forward.
A memory could be marked as fresh, fading, or important.

It felt like I was getting close.

The hidden block had small fields for memory entries: what happened, how important it was, what kind of emotional tag it belonged to, and how retrievable it still was. It was not pretty, but it gave the chat a sense of continuity that normal AI RP platforms did not have.

For a while, that was enough to make the system feel alive.

Then the cracks started showing.

The hidden state got longer.

And longer.

And longer.

At first, I thought that was good. More memories meant more continuity.

But that was the trap.

The AI was not forgetting.

It was hoarding.

Every turn, it wanted to add more. A small emotional exchange became a memory. A repeated detail became a memory. A minor reaction became a memory. The system was supposed to keep only what mattered, but the model kept treating almost everything as worth saving.

The memory list grew like a junk drawer.

That was when I started asking the real question:

Was this actually memory?

Or was it just accumulation?

Because human memory does not work like that.

People forget. People compress. People misremember. People keep the emotional shape of something after the details are gone. Sometimes a tiny moment becomes permanent. Sometimes a whole day vanishes because nothing inside them changed.

My system was supposed to mimic that.

But the prompt-only version could not reliably do it.

I told the AI to decay memory.

I told it to lower retrieval strength over time.

I told it to cull old entries.

I told it to keep only the most meaningful moments.

The AI understood the instruction.

It could explain the instruction.

It could even perform the instruction once, if I forced it directly with an OOC command.

But it would not reliably keep doing it.

That was the important part.

A system prompt could say:

Reduce memory strength by 5 each turn.

Keep only six memories.

Remove weak memories.

But the AI was not actually running a loop over data.

It was not executing code.

It was predicting the next piece of text that looked plausible after the previous piece of text.

If the previous hidden state had ten memories, the next one often tried to continue that pattern. If the old entries all had high retrieval strength, the model tended to keep them high. It was following the shape of the conversation more than the rules of the system.

That was not a moral failure.

That was not even really a model failure.

That was me asking a language model to act like a database.

The more I tested it, the more obvious the boundary became.

A single LLM can write a convincing state snapshot.

It can roughly track a few emotional variables.

It can maintain the feeling of continuity for a while.

It can summarize.

It can improvise.

It can narrate beautifully.

But it cannot be trusted to precisely manage a growing memory system by itself.

Not with decay.

Not with culling.

Not with scoring.

Not with long-term consistency.

Not if API money is being burned every time I have to regenerate a reply just to get the hidden state back.

That part hurt.

I remember changing the system prompt mid-session and watching the hidden coded block disappear. I had to regenerate responses to get it back. That meant real API cost for something the system should have handled cleanly.

It was annoying, but it made the lesson impossible to ignore.

The hidden state was useful.

The prompt was useful.

The narrator architecture was useful.

But none of them were enough alone.

The AI could report what changed.

But something else had to decide what to keep.

That was the turning point.

The Soul could not just live inside the model’s output.

It needed to exist outside the model.

A real Soul file.

A real memory store.

A real update process.

Something deterministic enough to say:

This memory is important.

This one is routine.

This one should fade.

This one should merge into a pattern.

This one becomes core.

This one should not be shown to the character because they do not know it.

That last part mattered too.

Around this time, I was also tightening the narrator rules. The AI should not act as the user. It should listen when I speak OOC. It should respect the world’s logic. And most importantly, the character should not have narrator knowledge.

The narrator may know how the scene works.

The character should only know what they experienced.

That distinction sounds obvious, but AI RP breaks it constantly.

A character suddenly knows hidden information.
A narrator accidentally speaks for the user.
A scene bends itself to the model’s convenience.
Emotional progression jumps because the current reply wants payoff now.

I wanted Mnemosyne to resist that.

But again, the answer was not “write a better prompt forever.”

The answer was structure.

The prompt can tell the narrator how to behave.

But the engine has to enforce continuity.

That changed how I thought about the entire project.

Before, I was trying to make the prompt smarter.

After this, I started thinking like this:

The LLM should write.

The engine should remember.

The Soul should change.

The World Log should track the scene.

The context compiler should decide what the model sees next.

That was the split I needed.

The LLM is not the memory.

The LLM is the writer reading from memory.

That one sentence changed the project.

It meant Mnemosyne was no longer just a better AI RP prompt. It was becoming a local-first memory engine wrapped around an AI narrator.

The prompt-only hidden state did not fail because it was useless.

It failed because it showed exactly where the prompt had to stop.

And where the engine had to begin.


다음 글 방향

Devlog 003는 여기로 가면 됨:

Devlog 003, The First Real Engine Plan

핵심 내용:

- Soul memory와 World Log 분리
- LLM은 writer, engine은 memory라는 구조 확정
- Tauri/local-first 방향
- BYOK/client-first 철학
- website는 avatar creator가 아니라 discovery/download directory
- memory scoring MVP: deterministic stub, 나중에 ONNX

이 흐름이면 000 → 001 → 002 → 003가 자연스럽게 이어짐.

chatgpt response

맞아, 이 피드백 중요해. Devlog 002가 너무 “prompt-only 실험”처럼 보이면 실제로 이미 DeepSeek/Codex로 앱을 만들고 테스트하던 흐름이 빠져 보여서 연대기가 어긋남. Git 쪽에서 앱 실행/스캐폴드 시점을 한번 확인하고, 글 초점을 “앱 만들면서 prompt-memory 한계와 UI 숨김 문제를 만난 시기”로 바꿀게.

Git에서 앱 실행/테스트 쪽 증거도 잡혔어. Scaffold Tauri desktop client, Wire mock provider turn flow, Encode mock hidden state, Align API hidden state prompt가 있어서, Devlog 002에는 “이미 앱/테스트 루프를 만들고 있었음”을 넣는 게 맞아.

맞아. 네 지적이 정확함. Devlog 002 초안은 앱이 이미 만들어지고 테스트되고 있었다는 사실이 빠져서, 읽는 사람이 이렇게 느낄 수 있음:

“그래서 언제 실제 앱을 만들기 시작한 건데?”

Git 쪽에서도 그 흐름이 확인돼. Scaffold Tauri desktop client 커밋이 있고, 이후 Wire mock provider turn flow, Encode mock hidden state, Align API hidden state prompt 같은 커밋이 있어서, 이 시점은 단순 prompt 실험이 아니라 이미 Tauri 앱과 mock/API 테스트 루프를 만들며 문제를 만난 단계로 써야 맞음.   

그리고 Codex fix PR merge도 있어서, 네 기억대로 Codex를 개발 흐름에 쓴 흔적도 있음. 

Devlog 002 방향 수정

기존 제목:

I Learned the AI Was Not the Database

앱을 만들기 시작함
mock provider로 turn flow를 테스트함
hidden state를 숨기고 파싱하려 함

code block이 유저에게 보이면 몰입이 깨짐
그래서 “보이는 narration”과 “숨겨진 state”를 분리하기 시작함

이거임.

Devlog 002 수정 초안

아직 HTML 아님.


Devlog 002, Making the Hidden State Actually Work

The App Started Testing the Soul

At this point, Mnemosyne was no longer only an idea in a chat window.

I had started building the app.

Not the final version. Not the polished product. Not the full platform I was imagining later.

But the first real desktop client was starting to exist: a Tauri app, a React interface, a Rust state engine, SQLite persistence, mock turns, Soul files, and a context compiler. The project was moving out of pure prompting and into actual code.

That mattered because the question changed.

Before, I was asking:

Can I make the AI remember better with a prompt?

Now, I was asking:

Can an app take what the AI reports, hide the messy machinery from the user, and update the Soul behind the scenes?

That was a very different problem.

The crude code block from the earlier experiments had become a prototype for hidden state. It was supposed to sit behind the narration, not inside it. The user should see the scene, the emotion, the character, the atmosphere.

They should not see the machinery.

No ugly JSON.

No memory tags.

No trust deltas.

No system residue.

No visible code box sitting under every reply, reminding them that the character is being calculated.

That part was harder than it sounded.

At first, the code block was useful because it was visible. I could inspect it. I could see whether the AI was tracking memories, emotional shifts, scene details, and character state. But if Mnemosyne was going to become an actual RP client, that block could not stay in the user-facing experience forever.

The app had to separate two things:

What the player sees.

What the engine reads.

That became one of the first real technical problems.

The narrator response needed to stay clean.

The hidden state needed to be machine-readable.

The app needed to strip the hidden state from the visible response, parse it, apply it to the Soul, and then store the updated result. If the hidden state leaked into the chat, it broke immersion. If it disappeared completely, the engine lost the update packet.

Both failures were bad.

So I started testing the turn flow.

User message.

Compiled context.

Mock provider response.

Visible narration.

Hidden state.

Parser.

Soul update.

Memory scoring.

Consolidation.

Save.

Repeat.

That loop was the first version of Mnemosyne actually breathing.

The mock provider was important because I needed a way to test the engine without burning API money every time something broke. DeepSeek was useful for live experiments, but real API calls made every broken prompt and every bad hidden-state output cost something. A mock provider let the app simulate turns locally and check whether the engine could parse, score, and update state.

It was not glamorous.

But it was the first real test harness.

Then came the second hard problem:

Forgetting.

I already knew I did not want Mnemosyne to save everything. That would just recreate the same problem in a different form. A giant memory list is not intelligence. It is hoarding.

But making a system forget in a human-like way is not simple.

If memory decays too aggressively, the character becomes shallow.

If memory never decays, the character becomes a database.

If every emotional moment becomes permanent, nothing is actually important.

If nothing becomes permanent, the character never grows.

That balance was the heart of the Soul.

The goal was not just to delete old entries.

The goal was to decide what should fade, what should merge into a pattern, and what should become part of the character.

A boring repeated event should disappear.

A repeated kindness might become trust.

A repeated threat might become fear.

A repeated silence might become abandonment.

So forgetting could not just be a timer.

It needed meaning.

That was where the prompt-only version started showing its limits. The AI could be told to decay memory, but it did not reliably execute decay as a rule. It would often keep old entries, preserve high retrieval values, or add new memories without cleaning the old ones.

In chat, I could force it with OOC commands.

In an app, that was not good enough.

The engine had to be the one making those decisions.

That was the reason the hidden state became an update packet instead of the memory itself.

The AI could say:

This moment mattered.

This was a trust-building event.

This raised fear.

This changed the world state.

But the engine had to decide:

How much does it matter?

Is it novel?

Is it repetition?

Does it connect to the character’s current needs?

Should it become a recent memory?

Should it merge into a schema?

Should it become core?

Should it be forgotten?

That split was the beginning of the real Mnemosyne architecture.

The LLM writes the scene.

The hidden state reports candidate changes.

The engine validates and scores those changes.

The Soul updates.

The player sees only the story.

That sounds obvious now, but it took testing to make it obvious.

The hardest parts were not the dramatic ones.

They were small, annoying, practical things.

How do I stop the AI from showing the hidden state to the user?

How do I parse the hidden block safely?

How do I support older hidden-state formats without breaking the session?

How do I test the whole thing without spending API money every time?

How do I make forgetting happen in code instead of begging the model to remember to forget?

Those problems made the project feel real.

Not because they were exciting, but because they were the kind of problems apps have.

The moment Mnemosyne had to hide the code box, parse it, and update a Soul file, it stopped being only a prompt experiment.

It became software.


이게 더 정확한 이유

이 버전은 네가 말한 두 가지를 반영함:

1. 이미 앱을 만들고 테스트하기 시작했음.
2. 까먹게 만들기와 code box 숨기기가 꽤 힘들었음.

그리고 Devlog 001과도 자연스럽게 연결됨:

Devlog 001:
code block을 Soul update packet으로 보기 시작함.

Devlog 002:
그 update packet을 실제 앱에서 hidden state로 숨기고, parse하고, Soul에 적용하려고 테스트함.

다음에 올릴 때는 이 버전으로 가는 게 훨씬 맞아.

message time: 2026-05-29 15:35:48

feedback: mock was ass and I never really used it. I did get to use free open router models instead so i can actually test.


chatgpt response

좋아. 그러면 “mock provider로 테스트했다”는 빼거나 아주 작게 낮춰야 해. Devlog 002의 실제 감정은 mock이 별로라서, 무료 OpenRouter 모델로 진짜 LLM 반응을 때려보며 hidden state와 forgetting을 검증한 시기로 가는 게 맞아.

맞아. 그럼 Devlog 002에서 mock provider를 주연처럼 쓰면 안 됨.

Git에는 mock provider turn flow랑 hidden-state encoding 커밋이 있긴 한데, 그건 “테스트용 장치가 있었다” 정도지, 네가 실제로 감 잡은 주 테스트 루프는 아니었던 거임.  

핵심 수정:

잘못된 초점:
Mock provider로 엔진을 안정적으로 테스트했다.

정확한 초점:
Mock은 있었지만 별로였고, 실제 감은 DeepSeek/OpenRouter 무료 모델 같은 진짜 LLM 응답을 때려보면서 잡았다.

Devlog 002 수정 방향

제목은 그대로 괜찮음:

Devlog 002, Making the Hidden State Actually Work

하지만 중간 문단을 이렇게 바꿔야 함.


교체할 문단

기존:

The mock provider was important because I needed a way to test the engine without burning API money every time something broke...

교체:

There was a mock provider in the app, but honestly, it was not where the real testing happened.

The mock was useful for checking whether the app pipeline existed at all. It could prove that a turn could pass through the client, produce a response, attach hidden state, and update the Soul. But as an RP test, it was ass.

It did not feel like a real model.

It did not surprise me.

It did not break in the same ways real models broke.

And because it did not break like a real model, it could not fully prove the memory system.

So I ended up testing with actual LLMs instead. DeepSeek was part of that, and I also used free OpenRouter models when I could, because I needed real generations to see whether the system actually held together.

That was where the useful failures came from.

A real model might forget the hidden state.

A real model might reveal the code block to the user.

A real model might keep adding memories instead of decaying them.

A real model might understand the rule, explain the rule, and then still fail to follow the rule three turns later.

That was the kind of failure I needed to see.

The mock could test the pipe.

The real models tested the idea.

Then continue with this

That was when the hidden-state problem became practical instead of theoretical.

The code block could not stay visible. If the player saw JSON, tags, trust deltas, memory scores, or system residue after every response, the illusion broke immediately.

But if I hid the block too aggressively, the engine lost the update packet.

So the app needed a clean separation:

The user sees narration.

The engine sees state.

That sounds simple, but it became one of the first real software problems in Mnemosyne.

Revised Devlog 002 structure

1. App had already started, Tauri/Rust/React/Soul engine direction.
2. Code block became hidden state.
3. Need to hide the machinery from user.
4. Mock provider existed, but was weak and not the real test.
5. Real tests came from DeepSeek/OpenRouter free models.
6. Real models exposed hidden-state leakage, disappearance, bloat, and failed forgetting.
7. Conclusion: LLM writes, engine remembers.

This makes the devlog much more honest and better as a history. It keeps the repo evidence, but does not exaggerate the mock provider into something it was not.

사용 기준은 이전에 처리한 history_2, history_3 전체 흐름이고, Git 쪽에서는 실제 앱 스캐폴드와 hidden-state 관련 커밋도 확인했음. Scaffold Tauri desktop client, Wire mock provider turn flow, Encode mock hidden state, Align API hidden state prompt 흐름이 있음.   


Devlog 002, Making Hidden State Work

The App Started Testing the Soul

At this point, Mnemosyne was no longer only an idea in a chat window.

I had started building the app.

Not the final version. Not the polished product. Not the full platform I was imagining later. But the first real desktop client was starting to exist. Tauri, React, Rust, Soul files, local persistence, context compilation, hidden state parsing. The project was moving out of pure prompting and into actual code.

That mattered because the question changed.

Can I make the AI remember better with a prompt?

Now, I was asking:

Can an app take what the AI reports, hide the messy machinery from the user, and update the Soul behind the scenes?

That was a very different problem.

They should not see the machinery.

No ugly JSON.

No memory tags.

No trust deltas.

No retrieval scores.

No system residue.

No visible code box sitting under every reply, reminding them that the character is being calculated.

That part was harder than it sounded.

Hidden state.

Parser.

Memory scoring.

Consolidation.

Save.

Repeat.

A boring repeated event should disappear.

A repeated threat might become fear.

A repeated silence might become abandonment.

So forgetting could not just be a timer.

It needed meaning.

This raised fear.

This changed the world state.

How much does it matter?

Is it new?

Is it repetition?

Does it connect to the character’s current needs?

Should it become a recent memory?

Should it merge into a pattern?

Should it become core?

Should it be forgotten?

Those problems made the project feel real.

Not because they were exciting, but because they were the kind of problems apps have.

The moment Mnemosyne had to hide the code box, parse it, and update a Soul file, it stopped being only a prompt experiment.

It became software.

The AI was still important.

The prompt was still important.

The hidden state was still important.

But the lesson was clear:

The LLM should write.

The Soul should change.

That was the first time Mnemosyne felt less like a clever prompt and more like an actual system.

Devlog 002에 넣는 방식

Devlog 002는 이렇게 시작하면 좋음:

By this point, the project had crossed the line from prompt experiment into code.

The earlier Soul and code-block experiments were still mostly local, living in chats, prompts, and tests that existed before the repository. But after I started looking into open-source licensing, I created the GitHub repo and committed the first legal/project baseline. The initial commit and AGPL license made the project feel less like a disposable prompt and more like something I intended to protect and build.

From there, the app started taking shape.

그리고 바로 이어서:

The next real milestone was the Tauri desktop client scaffold. That was the first moment Mnemosyne started becoming a local app instead of only a prompt system.

그 다음 hidden state 쪽으로:

After that, the work moved into turn flow and hidden state. I wired a mock provider, encoded hidden state, and started aligning the API prompt so the app could receive a model response, separate visible narration from machine-readable state, and update the Soul behind the scenes.

단, 네 피드백 반영해서:

The mock provider existed, but it was not where the real RP testing happened.

이 문장 꼭 넣어야 함.

7. hidden state 숨기기, parsing, forgetting 문제가 실제 앱 문제로 드러남
