Devlog 002, Making Hidden State Work

The App Started Testing the Soul

Before the repository existed, Mnemosyne was still mostly living inside chats, prompts, and messy experiments.

The crude code block existed.

The Soul idea existed.

The psychology and memory foundation was starting to exist.

But none of that was really a project yet. It was still pre-repo work, the kind of thing that lives in conversations, notes, and local experiments until you finally decide it is worth protecting.

That changed after I started looking into open-source licensing.

I did not want to build something that could just be swallowed, closed off, or copied without giving anything back. The license question was part of the moment where Mnemosyne stopped feeling like a disposable prompt trick and started feeling like something I actually intended to build.

So the repo began.

First came the basic repository state. Then the AGPL license. Then the desktop client scaffold.

That was the line.

Before that, Mnemosyne was an idea with experiments behind it.

After that, it was software.

Not finished software.

Not good software.

But software.

The first real shape was a local desktop client: Tauri, React, Rust, Soul files, local persistence, context compilation, and hidden state parsing. The project was moving out of pure prompting and into an app that could actually hold state outside the model.

That mattered because the question changed.

Before, I was asking:

Can I make the AI remember better with a prompt?

Now, I was asking:

Can an app take what the AI reports, hide the messy machinery from the user, and update the Soul behind the scenes?

That was a completely different problem.

The crude code block from the earlier experiments had become a prototype for hidden state. It was supposed to sit behind the narration, not inside it. The user should see the scene, the emotion, the character, and the atmosphere.

They should not see the machinery.

No ugly JSON.

No memory tags.

No trust deltas.

No retrieval scores.

No system residue.

No visible code box sitting under every reply, reminding them that the character is being calculated.

That part was harder than it sounded.

At first, the code block was useful because it was visible. I could inspect it. I could see whether the AI was tracking memories, emotional shifts, scene details, and character state. But if Mnemosyne was going to become an actual RP client, that block could not stay in the user-facing experience forever.

The app had to separate two things:

What the player sees.

What the engine reads.

That became one of the first real technical problems.

The narrator response needed to stay clean.

The hidden state needed to be machine-readable.

The app needed to strip the hidden state from the visible response, parse it, apply it to the Soul, and then store the updated result. If the hidden state leaked into the chat, it broke immersion. If it disappeared completely, the engine lost the update packet.

Both failures were bad.

So the turn flow started becoming real.

User message.

Compiled context.

Model response.

Visible narration.

Hidden state.

Parser.

Soul update.

Memory scoring.

Consolidation.

Save.

Repeat.

That loop was the first version of Mnemosyne actually breathing.

There was a mock provider in the code, but I do not want to oversell it.

The mock was not where the real RP testing happened.

It was basically an early placeholder for the chat interface and turn flow. It could return a fixed kind of answer, attach a predictable hidden state block, and make the UI look like it was doing something. That was useful for shaping the app before the real provider path was ready.

But as a test of roleplay, memory, or narration, it was useless.

It did not feel like a real model.

It did not surprise me.

It did not fail creatively.

It did not forget in the same way real models forget.

It did not expose the actual problems.

So the real testing came from actual LLMs.

DeepSeek was part of that. I also used free OpenRouter models when I could, because I needed real generations to see whether the system actually held together. A fake response could prove that the button worked. A real model could prove whether the idea survived contact with chaos.

That was where the useful failures came from.

A real model might forget to include the hidden state.

A real model might reveal the code block to the user.

A real model might wrap the hidden state in the wrong format.

A real model might summarize instead of updating.

A real model might keep adding memories instead of decaying them.

A real model might understand the rule, explain the rule, and then fail to follow the rule three turns later.

That was the kind of failure I needed to see.

The mock could help the app have a shape.

The real models tested the system.

Then came the second hard problem:

Forgetting.

I already knew I did not want Mnemosyne to save everything. That would just recreate the same problem in a different form. A giant memory list is not intelligence. It is hoarding.

But making a system forget in a human-like way is not simple.

If memory decays too aggressively, the character becomes shallow.

If memory never decays, the character becomes a database.

If every emotional moment becomes permanent, nothing is actually important.

If nothing becomes permanent, the character never grows.

That balance was the heart of the Soul.

The goal was not just to delete old entries.

The goal was to decide what should fade, what should merge into a pattern, and what should become part of the character.

A boring repeated event should disappear.

A repeated kindness might become trust.

A repeated threat might become fear.

A repeated silence might become abandonment.

So forgetting could not just be a timer.

It needed meaning.

That was where the prompt-only version started showing its limits. The AI could be told to decay memory, but it did not reliably execute decay as a rule. It would often keep old entries, preserve high retrieval values, or add new memories without cleaning the old ones.

In chat, I could sometimes force it with OOC commands.

In an app, that was not good enough.

The engine had to be the one making those decisions.

That was the reason hidden state became an update packet instead of the memory itself.

The AI could say:

This moment mattered.

This was a trust-building event.

This raised fear.

This changed the world state.

But the engine had to decide:

How much does it matter?

Is it new?

Is it repetition?

Does it connect to the character’s current needs?

Should it become a recent memory?

Should it merge into a pattern?

Should it become core?

Should it be forgotten?

That split was the beginning of the real Mnemosyne architecture.

The LLM writes the scene.

The hidden state reports candidate changes.

The engine validates and scores those changes.

The Soul updates.

The player sees only the story.

That sounds obvious now, but it took testing to make it obvious.

The hardest parts were not the dramatic ones.

They were small, annoying, practical things.

How do I stop the AI from showing the hidden state to the user?

How do I parse the hidden block safely?

How do I make the app tolerate weird model output?

How do I support older hidden-state formats without breaking the session?

How do I test the interface before the real model path is stable?

How do I make forgetting happen in code instead of begging the model to remember to forget?

Those problems made the project feel real.

Not because they were exciting, but because they were the kind of problems apps have.

The moment Mnemosyne had to hide the code box, parse it, and update a Soul file, it stopped being only a prompt experiment.

It became software.

The AI was still important.

The prompt was still important.

The hidden state was still important.

But the lesson was clear:

The LLM should write.

The engine should remember.

The Soul should change.

That was the first time Mnemosyne felt less like a clever prompt and more like an actual system.


you asked

message time: 2026-05-29 15:57:46

Devlog 002, Making Hidden State Work
The App Started Testing the Soul

Before the repository existed, Mnemosyne was still mostly living inside chats, prompts, and messy experiments.

The crude code block existed.

The Soul idea existed.

The psychology and memory foundation was starting to exist.

But none of that was really a project yet. It was still pre-repo work, the kind of thing that lives in conversations, notes, and local experiments until you finally decide it is worth protecting.

That changed after I started looking into open-source licensing.

I did not want to build something that could just be swallowed, closed off, or copied without giving anything back. The license question was part of the moment where Mnemosyne stopped feeling like a disposable prompt trick and started feeling like something I actually intended to build.

So the repo began.

First came the basic repository state. Then the AGPL license. Then the desktop client scaffold.

That was the line.

Before that, Mnemosyne was an idea with experiments behind it.

After that, it was software.

Not finished software.

Not good software.

But software.

The first real shape was a local desktop client: Tauri, React, Rust, Soul files, local persistence, context compilation, and hidden state parsing. The project was moving out of pure prompting and into an app that could actually hold state outside the model.

That mattered because the question changed.

Before, I was asking:

Can I make the AI remember better with a prompt?

Now, I was asking:

Can an app take what the AI reports, hide the messy machinery from the user, and update the Soul behind the scenes?

That was a completely different problem.

The crude code block from the earlier experiments had become a prototype for hidden state. It was supposed to sit behind the narration, not inside it. The user should see the scene, the emotion, the character, and the atmosphere.

They should not see the machinery.

No ugly JSON.

No memory tags.

No trust deltas.

No retrieval scores.

No system residue.

No visible code box sitting under every reply, reminding them that the character is being calculated.

That part was harder than it sounded.

At first, the code block was useful because it was visible. I could inspect it. I could see whether the AI was tracking memories, emotional shifts, scene details, and character state. But if Mnemosyne was going to become an actual RP client, that block could not stay in the user-facing experience forever.

맞아, 그럼 결론 문장이 너무 미래형/현재형으로 섞여 있었어. 지금 시점의 Devlog 002는 “이미 code decay 완성”이 아니라, LLM에게 decay를 시키려 했고, 그게 불안정해서 code로 옮겨야겠다고 느끼는 단계로 써야 정확해.

맞아. 네 기억이 맞을 가능성이 큼.

현재 글에서 이 문장들은 너무 이후 관점이 섞여 있음:

How do I move memory decay out of the LLM and into code?

또는:

The engine had to be the one making those decisions.

지금 코드 상태를 보면, 현재 엔진은 memory patch를 받고, score/salience 기준으로 recent memory를 정렬하고 truncate하고, consolidation에서 promote/discard/merge를 처리함. 그런데 “매 turn마다 retrieval_strength를 시간 기반으로 직접 decay 계산한다”는 구조는 명확히 보이지 않음. 예를 들어 consolidation은 retrieval_strength <= 30.0인 memory를 약하게 보거나 schema 후보로 돌리고, score에 따라 core/promote/discard/retain을 처리함.  

그리고 memory patch 쪽에서는 LLM이 보낸 salience나 retrieval_strength가 있으면 그 값을 받아서 memory에 반영하는 구조도 있음. 즉, 이 단계에서는 LLM이 decay/retrieval_strength 판단에 아직 많이 관여하고 있고, 엔진은 그것을 받아 검증, 정렬, 제한, consolidation하는 역할에 가까움.  

그래서 Devlog 002에서는 이렇게 쓰는 게 더 정확함:

바꿔야 하는 핵심 표현

기존 느낌

The engine had to decide everything.

더 정확한 느낌

At first, the LLM was still doing too much of the decay and retrieval-strength reasoning. The engine was starting to take shape around it, parsing the hidden state, scoring memories, limiting the list, and consolidating entries, but the boundary was not clean yet.

교체용 문단

아래 문단으로 바꾸면 정확함:

That was where the prompt-only version started showing its limits. The AI could be told to decay memory, lower retrieval strength, and clean up old entries. Sometimes it even did it.

But it was not reliable.

At this stage, the LLM was still doing too much of the memory judgment. It was the one proposing what mattered, what faded, and what should stay fresh. The app could parse that hidden state, score memories, limit the recent list, and consolidate entries, but the boundary was not clean yet.

That was the uncomfortable part.

I was building an engine, but I was still asking the model to behave like part of the engine.

그리고 질문 목록 쪽은 이렇게 고쳐야 함:

How do I stop the AI from showing the hidden state to the user?

How do I parse the hidden block safely?

How do I test the interface before the real model path is stable?

How much memory judgment should the LLM be allowed to propose?

Where should the engine stop trusting the model and start enforcing rules itself?

How do I make forgetting less dependent on the model remembering to forget?

끝부분도 이렇게 살짝 바꾸는 게 맞음:

The lesson was not that the engine already solved memory.

It was that the boundary had to move.

The LLM could write the scene and propose what changed.

The engine had to become the thing that checked, scored, compressed, and eventually enforced memory.

The Soul should not simply be whatever the model remembered to write.

The Soul should change through a system.

이게 네 실제 단계에 더 충실함. Devlog 002는 “engine 완성”이 아니라 LLM이 아직 decay를 직접 하던 상태에서, 그게 위험하다는 걸 보며 engine boundary를 잡아가던 시기로 쓰는 게 맞아.

1. 먼저 인간 기억/forgetting curve 개념을 prompt/code block에 넣음.
2. 이때는 LLM이 hidden state 안에서 retrieval strength / decay를 직접 계산하게 하려는 발상.
3. 실제로 써보다가 “어? 이거 안 까먹네? 계속 쌓이네?”를 발견.
4. 그때 “LLM이 하는 일이 너무 많아서 decay/culling을 안정적으로 못 한다”는 결론으로 감.
5. 그래서 나중에 code/engine 쪽으로 넘기는 방향이 생김.

즉 Devlog 002 시점은 “engine이 decay를 해결했다”가 아니라 “LLM에게 decay를 맡기는 방식이 불안정하다는 걸 보기 시작했다”가 맞아.

Git 기준으로도 repo 시작, AGPL, Tauri scaffold, hidden-state 관련 커밋은 확인되지만, 이걸 곧바로 “engine decay 완성”으로 쓰면 과장임. 커밋 흐름은 Initial commit, Add AGPL-3.0 license, Scaffold Tauri desktop client, Encode mock hidden state, Align API hidden state prompt 정도로 잡히고 있음.    

현재 코드에는 나중에 engine 쪽 consolidation이 들어가 있음. 예를 들어 consolidate_soul은 recent memory를 가져와서 retrieval strength, score, tag 기준으로 promote/discard/merge/truncate를 처리함.
하지만 patch 적용 쪽을 보면 LLM이 보낸 salience와 retrieval_strength를 받아 memory에 반영할 수도 있음. 그래서 “초기에는 LLM 판단을 받아오고, engine이 점점 검증/정리하는 방향으로 가고 있었다”가 더 정확함. 

Devlog 002에서 고쳐야 할 핵심

기존 느낌:

The engine had to decide forgetting.

수정된 느낌:

At this stage, I was still trying to make the LLM handle too much of forgetting.

그리고 결론은 이렇게 가야 함:

The engine was not fully solving forgetting yet.

But the problem was becoming clear:
the LLM could propose what changed,
but it could not be trusted to reliably decay, cull, and preserve memories by itself.

교체용 문단

아래 부분을 기존 “forgetting” 구간에 넣는 게 제일 정확해.

That was where the prompt-only version started showing its limits.

At first, I was still trying to make the LLM handle forgetting inside the hidden state. The model would output memory entries, retrieval strength, emotional tags, and sometimes its own idea of what should fade or stay.

In theory, that sounded fine.

The LLM had the scene. It knew the emotional context. It could judge what mattered.

But in practice, it was doing too much.

It had to write the scene, stay in character, follow lore, avoid speaking for the user, hide the code block, update relationship values, flag memories, and also remember to decay old ones correctly.

That was too many jobs for one generation.

Sometimes it followed the rule.

Sometimes it explained the rule.

Sometimes it even performed the cleanup when I directly forced it with an OOC command.

But it was not reliable.

That was the moment the question changed again.

Not:

Can the LLM remember?

But:

How much memory judgment should the LLM be allowed to propose before the engine takes over?

그리고 이 질문 목록은 이렇게 바꾸면 됨:

How do I stop the AI from showing the hidden state to the user?

How do I parse the hidden block safely?

How do I test the interface before the real model path is stable?

How much memory judgment should the LLM be allowed to propose?

Where should the engine stop trusting the model and start enforcing rules?

How do I make forgetting less dependent on the model remembering to forget?

끝부분도 이렇게 바꾸면 연대기 맞음:

The lesson was not that the engine had already solved memory.

It had not.

The lesson was that the boundary had to move.

The LLM could write the scene and propose what changed.

The app could hide, parse, and store that proposal.

But the Soul could not simply be whatever the model remembered to write.

Eventually, the engine would need to check, score, compress, and enforce memory itself.

That was the first time Mnemosyne felt less like a clever prompt and more like an actual system.

이게 네 기억이랑 Git/로그 흐름 둘 다에 제일 안전하게 맞음.

Devlog 002, Making Hidden State Work

The App Started Testing the Soul

Before the repository existed, Mnemosyne was still mostly living inside chats, prompts, and messy experiments.

The crude code block existed.

The Soul idea existed.

The psychology and memory foundation was starting to exist.

But none of that was really a project yet. It was still pre-repo work, the kind of thing that lives in conversations, notes, and local experiments until you finally decide it is worth protecting.

That changed after I started looking into open-source licensing.

I did not want to build something that could just be swallowed, closed off, or copied without giving anything back. The license question was part of the moment where Mnemosyne stopped feeling like a disposable prompt trick and started feeling like something I actually intended to build.

So the repo began.

First came the basic repository state. Then the AGPL license. Then the desktop client scaffold.

That was the line.

Before that, Mnemosyne was an idea with experiments behind it.

After that, it was software.

Not finished software.

Not good software.

But software.

The first real shape was a local desktop client: Tauri, React, Rust, Soul files, local persistence, context compilation, and hidden state parsing. The project was moving out of pure prompting and into an app that could actually hold state outside the model.

That mattered because the question changed.

Before, I was asking:

Can I make the AI remember better with a prompt?

Now, I was asking:

Can an app take what the AI reports, hide the messy machinery from the user, and update the Soul behind the scenes?

That was a completely different problem.

The crude code block from the earlier experiments had become a prototype for hidden state. It was supposed to sit behind the narration, not inside it. The user should see the scene, the emotion, the character, and the atmosphere.

They should not see the machinery.

No ugly JSON.

No memory tags.

No trust deltas.

No retrieval scores.

No system residue.

No visible code box sitting under every reply, reminding them that the character is being calculated.

That part was harder than it sounded.

At first, the code block was useful because it was visible. I could inspect it. I could see whether the AI was tracking memories, emotional shifts, scene details, and character state. But if Mnemosyne was going to become an actual RP client, that block could not stay in the user-facing experience forever.

The app had to separate two things:

What the player sees.

What the engine reads.

That became one of the first real technical problems.

The narrator response needed to stay clean.

The hidden state needed to be machine-readable.

The app needed to strip the hidden state from the visible response, parse it, apply it to the Soul, and then store the updated result. If the hidden state leaked into the chat, it broke immersion. If it disappeared completely, the engine lost the update packet.

Both failures were bad.

So the turn flow started becoming real.

User message.

Compiled context.

Model response.

Visible narration.

Hidden state.

Parser.

Soul update.

Memory scoring.

Consolidation.

Save.

Repeat.

That loop was the first version of Mnemosyne actually breathing.

There was a mock provider in the code, but I do not want to oversell it.

The mock was not where the real RP testing happened.

It was basically an early placeholder for the chat interface and turn flow. It could return a fixed kind of answer, attach a predictable hidden state block, and make the UI look like it was doing something. That was useful for shaping the app before the real provider path was ready.

But as a test of roleplay, memory, or narration, it was useless.

It did practically nothing.

The real testing came from actual LLMs.

I was already a heavy AI RP experimenter, and I had been using OpenRouter to try different models. So when I needed real generations for testing, free OpenRouter models became the obvious choice.

I needed to see whether the system actually held together when a real model answered. A fake response could prove that the button worked. A real model could prove whether the idea survived contact with chaos.

That was where the useful failures came from.

A real model might forget to include the hidden state.

A real model might reveal the code block to the user.

A real model might wrap the hidden state in the wrong format.

A real model might summarize instead of updating.

A real model might keep adding memories instead of decaying them.

A real model might understand the rule, explain the rule, and then fail to follow the rule three turns later.

That was the kind of failure I needed to see.

The mock could help the app have a shape.

The real models tested the system.

Then came the second hard problem:

Forgetting.

I already knew I did not want Mnemosyne to save everything. That would just recreate the same problem in a different form. A giant memory list is not intelligence. It is hoarding.

But making a system forget in a human-like way is not simple.

If memory decays too aggressively, the character becomes shallow.

If memory never decays, the character becomes a database.

If every emotional moment becomes permanent, nothing is actually important.

If nothing becomes permanent, the character never grows.

That balance was the heart of the Soul.

The goal was not just to delete old entries.

The goal was to decide what should fade, what should merge into a pattern, and what should become part of the character.

A boring repeated event should disappear.

A repeated kindness might become trust.

A repeated threat might become fear.

A repeated silence might become abandonment.

So forgetting could not just be a timer.

It needed meaning.

At first, I was still trying to make the LLM handle that inside the hidden state.

The model had the scene. It knew the emotional context. It could judge what mattered. So it felt natural to let it propose memory entries, emotional tags, retrieval strength, and what should fade or stay fresh.

In theory, that sounded fine.

In practice, it was asking one generation to do too much.

It had to write the scene, stay in character, follow lore, avoid speaking for the user, hide the code block, update relationship values, flag memories, and also remember to decay old ones correctly.

Sometimes it followed the rule.

Sometimes it explained the rule.

Sometimes it even performed cleanup when I directly forced it with an OOC command.

But it was not reliable.

That was the uncomfortable part.

I was building an engine, but I was still asking the model to behave like part of the engine.

At first, I did not fully see the problem. I was more focused on getting the hidden state to appear, disappear from the user-facing chat, and update the Soul at all.

Then the pattern started to show.

The character was not really forgetting.

The memory list kept growing.

Old entries stayed too strong.

The system could talk about decay, but it did not consistently decay.

That was when the question changed again.

Not:

Can the LLM remember?

But:

How much memory judgment should the LLM be allowed to propose before the engine takes over?

That split was the beginning of the real Mnemosyne architecture.

The LLM writes the scene.

The hidden state reports candidate changes.

The app hides and parses the machinery.

The Soul updates from that proposal.

The player sees only the story.

That sounds obvious now, but it took testing to make it obvious.

The hardest parts were not the dramatic ones.

They were small, annoying, practical things.

How do I stop the AI from showing the hidden state to the user?

How do I parse the hidden block safely?

How do I test the interface before the real model path is stable?

How much memory judgment should the LLM be allowed to propose?

Where should the engine stop trusting the model and start enforcing rules?

How do I make forgetting less dependent on the model remembering to forget?

Those problems made the project feel real.

Not because they were exciting, but because they were the kind of problems apps have.

The moment Mnemosyne had to hide the code box, parse it, and update a Soul file, it stopped being only a prompt experiment.

It became software.

The AI was still important.

The prompt was still important.

The hidden state was still important.

But the lesson was becoming clear:

The LLM should write.

The engine should remember.

The Soul should change.

The lesson was not that the engine had already solved memory.

It had not.

The lesson was that the boundary had to move.

The LLM could write the scene and propose what changed.

The app could hide, parse, and store that proposal.

But the Soul could not simply be whatever the model remembered to write.

Eventually, the engine would need to check, score, compress, and enforce memory itself.

That was the first time Mnemosyne felt less like a clever prompt and more like an actual system.

가능함. 방식은 “각 devlog 끝에 commit list 덤프”가 아니라, 본문 안에 commit milestone을 자연스럽게 박고, 따로 Covered commits 섹션을 짧게 붙이는 게 제일 좋음. 먼저 repo commit 순서를 더 뽑아서 Devlog 002가 어디까지 먹어야 하는지 경계부터 잡을게.

지금 확인해보니 GitHub connector가 “전체 커밋 리스트”를 한 번에 뽑는 함수는 없고, 검색 기반으로 커밋을 찾는 방식이라 누락 위험이 있어. 그래도 Devlog 002에 들어갈 확정 커밋들은 이미 잡혔고, 전체 커버리지는 별도 commit coverage map으로 관리하는 게 안전함.

가능. 그리고 이 방식으로 가야 함:

각 Devlog = 특정 commit range를 커버
본문 = 중요한 커밋을 자연스럽게 서사화
끝부분 = Covered commits 섹션으로 순서 기록
전체 프로젝트 = commit coverage map으로 “누락 커밋 0개” 관리

So the repo began.

이렇게 바꾸면 됨:

So the repo began.

The first commits were not glamorous. They were foundation work: setting up the repository, adding the AGPL license, declaring that license in the package metadata, and merging the initial GitHub repository state. It was not “the app” yet, but it was the moment Mnemosyne stopped being only a pile of prompts and became something with a protected project boundary.

Then came the desktop client scaffold.

That was the line.

이 부분은 Add AGPL-3.0 license, Scaffold Tauri desktop client, Initial commit, Declare AGPL package license, Merge GitHub initial repository state를 커버함. AGPL package metadata commit은 실제로 package.json과 package-lock.json에 AGPL-3.0-or-later를 추가한 커밋임.     

그리고 이 문단:

There was a mock provider in the code, but I do not want to oversell it.

앞에 이렇게 넣으면 커밋 흐름이 붙음:

The first turn-flow commits were mostly about making the app behave like an app. The chat interface needed to send something, receive something, and prove that a hidden-state block could exist inside the pipeline. That led to the early mock provider wiring and later mock acceptance coverage.

But I do not want to oversell that mock.

이건 Wire mock provider turn flow, Add mock turn acceptance coverage 커버.  

그리고 hidden-state 부분에는 이렇게:

Once the pipe existed, the next problem was making the state less visible and more machine-readable. That led to encoded hidden state, API hidden-state prompt alignment, and eventually debug tools for inspecting the turn cycle without exposing the raw machinery to the player.

이건 Encode mock hidden state, Align API hidden state prompt, Surface memory cycle diagnostics, Add turn debug panel을 커버함.

한동안 싱글 패슬로 개고생하다 이 앱 개발중 첫번쨰 혁신: 듀얼패스. AI 2개 사용. 그거한걸로 기억함, 마자?


chatgpt response

맞는지 확인하려면 기록 두 축을 같이 봐야 해: 업로드한 history 로그에서 “dual pass / AI 2개 / single pass 고생”이 언제 나오는지, 그리고 Git 커밋에서 그 구조가 실제로 언제 코드화됐는지. 지금 바로 둘 다 대조해서 Devlog 003 범위까지 잡아볼게.

업로드된 2, 3, 4번 기록 파일은 전부 열람 가능했고, 각각 약 153,558자/1,967줄, 92,085자/1,967줄, 116,491자/1,967줄이야. 정식 목차형 헤더 문서는 아니고 긴 대화/개발 기록 형태라, 섹션은 “prompt-only memory 문제 → middleware/state engine → dual-system memory → narrator/provider/test flow → hidden-state/data-flow” 순서로 기능적으로 읽히는 구조임.

맞아, 큰 방향은 네 기억이 맞음.
다만 정확히 말하면 이렇게 구분해야 해.

문서 처리 확인

파일 길이 구조
mnemosyne start of a history_2.txt 153,558자, 1,967줄 prompt-only memory 한계, middleware/state engine 발상, Soul + overall summary/world tracker 구상
mnemosyne start of a history_3.txt 92,085자, 1,967줄 플랫폼/클라이언트/상태 엔진/브라우저 자동화/Context Compiler 청사진
mnemosyne start of a history_4.txt 116,491자, 1,967줄 mock provider, hidden-state encoding, narrator architecture 문제, middleware data flow, later architecture feedback

정식 목차형 헤더는 없고, 긴 대화/개발 기록임. 세 파일 전체를 확인했음.

결론

“한동안 single-pass로 개고생하다가, 앱 개발 중 첫 번째 큰 혁신이 dual-pass, AI 2개 사용이었다”는 말은 거의 맞음.

하지만 Devlog에 쓸 때는 이렇게 쓰는 게 더 정확해:

첫 번째 conceptual 혁신:
Soul memory + World Log / system memory로 기억을 2계층 분리한 것.

첫 번째 app-level architecture 혁신:
Narrator pass + Evaluator pass로 LLM 호출을 2단계로 나눈 것.

즉, dual-system이 먼저 개념으로 있었고, 그 다음에 dual-pass가 실제 앱 구조로 나타난 흐름임.

네가 기억하는 흐름과 기록이 맞는 부분

초기에는 single-pass 구조였음.

User message
→ LLM writes narration
→ same LLM also tries to output hidden state / memory / decay / relationship changes
→ app parses it
→ Soul updates

이 구조에서 문제가 생겼지.

LLM이 한 번의 generation 안에서 너무 많은 일을 해야 했음:

1. 장면 쓰기
2. 캐릭터처럼 자연스럽게 반응하기
3. narrator/reader/god 모드 지키기
4. user 대사/행동 훔치지 않기
5. lore consistency 지키기
6. hidden state 숨기기
7. 기억 후보 만들기
8. salience/retrieval strength 판단하기
9. decay/culling까지 하기

그래서 기록에서도 “single LLM은 database/rules engine처럼 안정적으로 행동하지 못한다”는 결론이 나옴. 특히 hidden state와 memory decay를 모델에게 맡기는 방식이 흔들렸고, 그걸 middleware/state engine으로 넘겨야 한다는 흐름이 나옴. 

그리고 나중에 네가 직접 정리한 구조도 이거였음:

System 1:
character memory, Soul, personality change, forgetting, detailed lived memory

System 2:
overall story/world tracker, environment/gear/status summary, compressed long-term continuity

이건 업로드된 history_4 쪽에도 “GM/narrator + 2 side systems, overall memory and character memory”로 남아 있음. 

Git 기록상 dual-pass는 실제로 존재함

커밋 검색에서도 명확하게 나옴.

Evaluator V1 skeleton 커밋이 있고, 여기서 build_state_updater_prompt가 build_evaluator_prompt로 바뀌고, EvaluatorOutputV1, evaluator_output_to_engine_patch, build_evaluator_user_message 같은 구조가 들어감. 이건 사실상 narrator와 evaluator를 분리하는 첫 골격임.  

그리고 더 노골적으로:

Dual Pass Eval Fix/ Agressive regen fix

라는 커밋이 있음. 이 커밋은 provider profile에 narrator_timeout_ms, evaluator_timeout_ms, wait_for_evaluator_before_next_turn, evaluator_background_enabled, anti_replay_forced_retry_enabled 같은 필드를 추가하고, evaluator_background_jobs 테이블도 만듦. 즉, dual-pass가 그냥 아이디어가 아니라 앱 설정/DB/job 구조까지 들어간 상태였음.  

정확한 표현

Devlog에 쓸 때는 이렇게 쓰면 좋음:

For a while, I was trying to make one model do everything.

The narrator had to write the scene, maintain character voice, obey lore, avoid stealing the user's actions, hide machine-readable state, judge memory importance, and update the Soul. It worked just enough to prove the idea, but it was fragile.

The first real architectural breakthrough was dual pass.

One pass would write.

The other pass would evaluate.

The narrator pass produced the story the player saw. The evaluator pass read the user turn, the narrator response, and the current compiled context, then returned structured state changes for the engine to apply.

That changed the shape of Mnemosyne. The LLM was no longer one overloaded actor pretending to be writer, psychologist, memory database, and rules engine at the same time.

중요한 nuance

“AI 2개 사용”이라고 말해도 되는데, 더 정확히는:

AI 2개라기보다, LLM call 2개 / role 2개.

같은 모델을 두 번 부를 수도 있고, 다른 모델을 쓸 수도 있음.

Pass 1: Narrator model
- writes visible story
- cares about prose, tone, mode, immersion

Pass 2: Evaluator model
- reads what happened
- judges memory candidates
- outputs structured patch
- does not write story

그래서 Devlog 제목 후보는 이게 좋음:

Devlog 003, The First Real Breakthrough: Dual Pass

또는 더 정확하게:

Devlog 003, Splitting the Writer from the Evaluator

내 픽은 두 번째임.
“Splitting the Writer from the Evaluator”가 기술적으로 더 정확하고, 네 프로젝트의 차별점도 더 잘 보임.

1. Soul Memory
- 캐릭터가 주관적으로 기억하는 것
- 감정적으로 왜곡됨
- 잊어버릴 수 있음
- trust, fear, shame, affection, openness, trauma, attachment 등에 영향

2. World Log / System Memory
- GM/narrator가 객관적으로 알아야 하는 것
- 장소, 시간, 물건, 사건, active plot, scene continuity
- 캐릭터가 반드시 아는 것은 아님

Devlog 002, Splitting Memory in Two

The Soul Remembers Like a Person. The World Log Remembers Like a GM.

After I started thinking of the code block as something that could update the Soul, the next problem became obvious.

Not all memory is the same kind of memory.

At first, I was still thinking too simply. The AI needed to remember what happened, so I needed a better memory system. That sounded reasonable, but it was still too broad. “Memory” was doing too much work as one word.

A character remembering something is not the same as the story remembering something.

That was the next shift.

The Soul should not carry everything.

The Soul should carry what changed the character.

That meant emotional memory. Subjective memory. Biased memory. The kind of memory that affects trust, fear, shame, affection, openness, desire, and resolve. The kind of memory that might grow stronger because it hurt, or fade because it never meant anything.

But the world needed a different kind of continuity.

Where are we?

What objects are in the room?

What just happened in the plot?

What is the active danger?

What information has been established?

What does the narrator need to know, even if the character does not?

That did not belong entirely inside the Soul.

If I put everything into the Soul, the character would become weirdly omniscient. They would “remember” facts they should not personally know. They would carry scene logistics as if those logistics were emotional memories. A location change, an object on a table, or a background plot thread would sit beside trauma, trust, and fear like they were the same kind of thing.

They are not.

That was when the memory system split in my head.

The Soul remembers like a person.

The World Log remembers like a GM.

That distinction mattered a lot.

The Soul is subjective.

The World Log is operational.

The Soul can be wrong, biased, incomplete, or emotionally distorted.

The World Log should be stable enough to keep the scene coherent.

A character might remember the sound of rain because it made them feel safe. The World Log remembers that the scene is taking place at a station, it is raining, the last train is delayed, and a suspicious courier package exists in the scene.

A character might forget the exact words someone said, but remember that the tone felt threatening. The World Log remembers the actual exchange happened, where it happened, and what plot thread it touched.

A character might not know there is someone watching from across the platform. The World Log can know that, if the narrative mode allows it.

That was the reason one memory bucket was not enough.

The project needed at least two layers.

The first layer was the Soul.

That layer tracked the character’s inner continuity: emotional variables, relationship changes, trauma state, personal memories, core memories, schemas, and slow personality drift. This was the “what the character has become” layer.

The second layer was the World Log.

That layer tracked story continuity: setting, active scene, recent objective events, important objects, plot threads, time passage, and what the narrator needed to keep consistent.

Once I saw that split, a lot of earlier problems became easier to name.

Before the split, I kept asking:

How do I make the AI remember everything?

After the split, the question became sharper:

What kind of memory is this?

Is it something the character personally experienced?

Is it something that emotionally changed them?

Is it a world fact the narrator needs?

Is it information the character should not know yet?

Is it just recent chat context?

Is it a long-term plot detail?

Those categories changed the architecture.

A betrayal belongs in the Soul.

A room layout belongs in the World Log.

A repeated kindness might become a relationship schema in the Soul.

A missing keycard belongs in the World Log.

A traumatic memory might become distorted inside the Soul.

A plot event should remain stable in the World Log.

That was the first time Mnemosyne started feeling less like “memory for AI RP” and more like an actual simulation layer.

It was not enough to summarize the chat.

A summary says what happened.

A Soul says what it did to the character.

A World Log says what remains true about the scene.

Those are different jobs.

This also solved part of the narrator problem.

I did not want the AI to simply be the character. That made the model too likely to speak as the character, steal the user’s actions, and collapse the difference between what the narrator knows and what the character knows.

The narrator needed to work more like a GM.

The narrator could see the scene.

The character could only know what they experienced.

The Soul could remember subjectively.

The World Log could preserve continuity objectively.

That meant Mnemosyne needed a narrator architecture, not just a character chatbot.

The character was not supposed to carry the whole universe inside their head.

The narrator and the system had to carry the universe around them.

That was also where the idea of different narrative modes started making more sense.

Realistic mode could restrict the narration to external behavior: body language, voice, movement, visible reactions.

Reader mode could allow access to the character’s inner thoughts, but only what the character themself knows or feels.

God mode could allow the narrator to reveal wider world information, foreshadowing, or details outside the character’s awareness.

Those modes depended on the same split.

Without separating Soul memory from World Log memory, God mode would poison the character. The character might start acting on information they should not have. Reader mode could become too omniscient. Realistic mode could accidentally expose internal state.

So the memory split also became a knowledge-boundary split.

What does the narrator know?

What does the character know?

What does the player see?

What does the engine store?

Those questions became part of the system.

At this point, I still did not have the final app architecture.

I did not have dual pass yet.

I did not have a clean evaluator.

I did not have the whole state engine solved.

But I had something important:

The character’s memory and the world’s memory could not be the same thing.

That was the first conceptual breakthrough after the Soul idea.

The Soul would carry the character’s lived inner history.

The World Log would carry the scene’s objective continuity.

The narrator would read from both.

And the player would only see the story.

That became the next foundation of Mnemosyne.

Not just:

Make the AI remember.

But:

Make the right layer remember the right thing.

you asked

message time: 2026-05-29 17:49:22

Update it.
Devlog 002, Splitting Memory in Two
The Soul Remembers Like a Person. The World Log Remembers Like a GM.

After I started thinking of the code block as something that could update the Soul, the next problem became obvious.

Not all memory is the same kind of memory.

At first, I was still thinking too simply. The AI needed to remember what happened, so I needed a better memory system. That sounded reasonable, but it was still too broad. “Memory” was doing too much work as one word.

A character remembering something is not the same as the story remembering something.

That was the next shift.

The Soul should not carry everything.

The Soul should carry what changed the character.

That meant emotional memory. Subjective memory. Biased memory. The kind of memory that affects trust, fear, shame, affection, openness, desire, and resolve. The kind of memory that might grow stronger because it hurt, or fade because it never meant anything.

But the world needed a different kind of continuity.

Where are we?

What objects are in the room?

What just happened in the plot?

What is the active danger?

What information has been established?

What does the narrator need to know, even if the character does not?

That did not belong entirely inside the Soul.

If I put everything into the Soul, the character would become weirdly omniscient. They would “remember” facts they should not personally know. They would carry scene logistics as if those logistics were emotional memories. A location change, an object on a table, or a background plot thread would sit beside trauma, trust, and fear like they were the same kind of thing.

They are not.

That was when the memory system split in my head.

The Soul remembers like a person.

The World Log remembers like a GM.

That distinction mattered a lot.

The Soul is subjective.

The World Log is operational.

The Soul can be wrong, biased, incomplete, or emotionally distorted.

The World Log should be stable enough to keep the scene coherent.

A character might remember the sound of rain because it made them feel safe. The World Log remembers that the scene is taking place at a station, it is raining, the last train is delayed, and a suspicious courier package exists in the scene.

A character might forget the exact words someone said, but remember that the tone felt threatening. The World Log remembers the actual exchange happened, where it happened, and what plot thread it touched.

A character might not know there is someone watching from across the platform. The World Log can know that, if the narrative mode allows it.

That was the reason one memory bucket was not enough.

The project needed at least two layers.

The first layer was the Soul.

That layer tracked the character’s inner continuity: emotional variables, relationship changes, trauma state, personal memories, core memories, schemas, and slow personality drift. This was the “what the character has become” layer.

The second layer was the World Log.

That layer tracked story continuity: setting, active scene, recent objective events, important objects, plot threads, time passage, and what the narrator needed to keep consistent.

Once I saw that split, a lot of earlier problems became easier to name.

Before the split, I kept asking:

How do I make the AI remember everything?

After the split, the question became sharper:

What kind of memory is this?

Is it something the character personally experienced?

Is it something that emotionally changed them?

Is it a world fact the narrator needs?

Is it information the character should not know yet?

Is it just recent chat context?

Is it a long-term plot detail?

Those categories changed the architecture.

A betrayal belongs in the Soul.

A room layout belongs in the World Log.

A repeated kindness might become a relationship schema in the Soul.

A missing keycard belongs in the World Log.

A traumatic memory might become distorted inside the Soul.

A plot event should remain stable in the World Log.

That was the first time Mnemosyne started feeling less like “memory for AI RP” and more like an actual simulation layer.

It was not enough to summarize the chat.

A summary says what happened.

A Soul says what it did to the character.

A World Log says what remains true about the scene.

Those are different jobs.

This also solved part of the narrator problem.

I did not want the AI to simply be the character. That made the model too likely to speak as the character, steal the user’s actions, and collapse the difference between what the narrator knows and what the character knows.

The narrator needed to work more like a GM.

The narrator could see the scene.

The character could only know what they experienced.

The Soul could remember subjectively.

The World Log could preserve continuity objectively.

That meant Mnemosyne needed a narrator architecture, not just a character chatbot.

The character was not supposed to carry the whole universe inside their head.

The narrator and the system had to carry the universe around them.

That was also where the idea of different narrative modes started making more sense.

Realistic mode could restrict the narration to external behavior: body language, voice, movement, visible reactions.

Reader mode could allow access to the character’s inner thoughts, but only what the character themself knows or feels.

God mode could allow the narrator to reveal wider world information, foreshadowing, or details outside the character’s awareness.

Those modes depended on the same split.

Without separating Soul memory from World Log memory, God mode would poison the character. The character might start acting on information they should not have. Reader mode could become too omniscient. Realistic mode could accidentally expose internal state.

So the memory split also became a knowledge-boundary split.

What does the narrator know?

What does the character know?

What does the player see?

What does the engine store?

Those questions became part of the system.

The character’s memory and the world’s memory could not be the same thing.

That was the first conceptual breakthrough after the Soul idea.

The Soul would carry the character’s lived inner history.

The World Log would carry the scene’s objective continuity.

The narrator would read from both.

And the player would only see the story.

That became the next foundation of Mnemosyne.

Not just:

Make the AI remember.

But:

Make the right layer remember the right thing.

Devlog 003

Making Hidden State Work

현재 Devlog 002에 올라간 내용 대부분은 여기로 밀리는 게 더 자연스러움.

chatgpt response

좋아. 아래는 Devlog 003 스코프 고친 버전이야. 아직 HTML/commit 아님, 읽기용 텍스트.

이 버전은 Devlog 002에서 만든 개념, Soul Memory + World Log 분리, 다음 단계로 자연스럽게 넘어감.
초점은 “앱이 실제로 hidden state를 숨기고, 파싱하고, Soul에 먹이기 시작한 시점”임.


Devlog 003, Making Hidden State Work

The App Started Testing the Soul

After I split memory into two layers, the next problem was no longer just conceptual.

The Soul would remember like a person.

The World Log would remember like a GM.

That sounded clean in theory.

But now I had to make the app actually do something with it.

Before this point, a lot of Mnemosyne still lived in prompts, notes, and thought experiments. I had the idea of a Soul. I had the idea of a World Log. I had the crude code block. I had the idea that the AI should report what changed instead of carrying the whole system inside the chat.

But an idea does not become real until something can run.

That was where the Tauri app started to matter.

The early app was not pretty. It was not a finished roleplay client. It was barely even the shape of the thing I wanted. But it gave Mnemosyne a body: a desktop window, a chat interface, Rust behind it, React in front of it, local state, Soul files, provider settings, and the beginning of a pipeline.

That was the shift.

Mnemosyne was no longer only a better prompt.

It was becoming a machine that could sit between the user and the model.

The first job was simple to say and annoying to implement:

The player should see the story.

The engine should see the state.

That was the whole problem.

The old crude code block was useful because I could see it. It let me inspect what the AI thought had changed. Trust moved. Fear moved. A memory was flagged. The scene changed. Something became important.

But if the user had to see that code block after every response, the experience was dead.

No one wants an emotional scene followed by ugly machinery.

No visible JSON.

No raw memory tags.

No trust deltas under the dialogue.

No retrieval scores.

No system residue.

No code box reminding the player that the character is being calculated.

So hidden state had to become actually hidden.

Not fake hidden.

Not “scroll past this.”

Not “pretend you did not see it.”

The model could output a machine-readable block, but the app had to catch it, strip it out, parse it, and apply it before the user saw the final message.

If the block leaked, immersion broke.

If the block disappeared, the Soul could not update.

If the block was malformed, the engine had to survive.

That was one of the first moments where Mnemosyne started feeling like real software.

Not because the feature was glamorous.

Because it was annoying in exactly the way real software is annoying.

The turn flow started becoming concrete:

User message.

Compiled context.

Model response.

Visible narration.

Hidden state.

Parser.

Soul update.

World update.

Save.

Repeat.

That loop was the first version of Mnemosyne actually breathing.

There was a mock provider in the code, but I do not want to oversell it.

The mock was not where the real roleplay testing happened.

It was mostly an early placeholder for the chat interface and turn flow. It could return a fixed kind of answer, attach a predictable hidden-state block, and make the UI look like it was doing something.

That was useful for building the app shape.

It proved that a button could send a message.

It proved that the backend could return a response.

It proved that hidden state could exist in the pipeline.

It proved that the UI could update.

But as a test of roleplay, memory, narration, or psychology, it was basically useless.

It did not surprise me.

It did not drift.

It did not forget.

It did not misunderstand the format.

It did not fail like a real model fails.

And because it did not fail like a real model, it could not prove the idea.

The real testing came from actual LLMs.

I was already using OpenRouter heavily to try different models for AI RP, so free OpenRouter models became the obvious way to test without burning too much money. I needed real generations. I needed actual model behavior. I needed the system to survive contact with chaos.

A fake response could prove that the interface worked.

A real model could prove whether the idea held together.

That was where the useful failures started.

A real model might forget to include the hidden state.

A real model might expose the hidden block to the user.

A real model might wrap the hidden state in the wrong format.

A real model might summarize instead of updating.

A real model might produce beautiful narration and then ruin the machine-readable part.

A real model might keep adding memories instead of letting anything fade.

A real model might understand the rule, explain the rule, and then fail to follow it three turns later.

That was exactly the kind of failure I needed to see.

Because the point was not to make a toy demo work.

The point was to find the places where a real model would break the architecture.

One of the first problems was formatting.

At first, hidden state could be plain JSON. That was easy to inspect, easy to test, and easy to understand. But plain JSON was also fragile and ugly. It was too easy for the model to leak it, corrupt it, or blend it into the visible response.

So the hidden state had to become more controlled.

The app needed a recognizable marker.

The payload needed to be compact.

The parser needed to support the new format without destroying older test transcripts.

That was why encoded hidden state became important.

The hidden block moved toward a versioned payload format: something the engine could identify, decode, and parse without treating it like normal prose.

That did not make the system perfect.

But it made the boundary sharper.

The narration was for the player.

The encoded hidden state was for the engine.

That boundary was everything.

Around the same time, I also started seeing why debug tools mattered.

When hidden state is visible, debugging is easy. You just read the ugly block.

When hidden state is actually hidden, debugging becomes harder. If the Soul updates wrong, I need to know why. If the parser fails, I need to know where. If a memory gets added, scored, discarded, or consolidated, I need to see the trace.

So diagnostics became part of the early app.

Not as polish.

As survival.

I needed a way to inspect the memory cycle without exposing the machinery to the player. I needed to see what the model returned, what the parser extracted, what the engine accepted, and what changed inside the Soul.

That was the practical reason for turn debug panels and memory cycle diagnostics.

The player should not see the machinery.

But the developer absolutely needs to.

This was also where the narrator architecture started becoming more urgent.

If the AI was allowed to behave like the character directly, it would blur everything. It might speak in first person. It might steal the user’s actions. It might treat narrator knowledge as character knowledge. It might expose internal state as dialogue.

That was not Mnemosyne.

Mnemosyne needed the model to act as a narrator.

The narrator writes the visible scene.

The hidden state reports candidate changes.

The app strips and parses the hidden state.

The engine updates the Soul and World Log.

The player only sees the story.

That sounds obvious now, but this was the stage where it started becoming obvious through bugs.

Every failure pointed back to the same lesson:

The model should not carry the whole system by itself.

At this stage, I was still asking the LLM to do too much inside one response.

It had to write the scene.

It had to follow the selected narrative mode.

It had to respect the user’s agency.

It had to avoid leaking hidden machinery.

It had to flag memories.

It had to judge emotional importance.

It had to update relationship values.

It had to keep the Soul coherent.

It had to remember to forget.

That was too much for one generation to do perfectly.

But I had not fully escaped that yet.

This was not the dual-pass breakthrough.

That came later.

At this point, the lesson was smaller but important:

The app needed to catch the model’s output and begin enforcing boundaries around it.

The AI could propose.

The engine had to manage.

That was the start of the real boundary.

Not complete.

Not clean.

But visible.

The mock provider gave the interface a shape.

OpenRouter models gave the system real failures.

Encoded hidden state gave the engine something it could parse.

Diagnostics gave me a way to see what was happening behind the curtain.

And the Tauri app gave all of it a place to run.

That was the point where Mnemosyne stopped being only a conceptual memory system.

It became an app trying to keep the magic invisible.


Covered commits

This devlog covers the early implementation period where the app shape, mock turn flow, hidden-state encoding, narrator alignment, diagnostics, and workspace controls started making the Soul update loop testable.

1. b0697fa — Wire mock provider turn flow

3. 9c242ea — Add local delete controls
4. 505f75b — Align prototype with narrator architecture

6. b6fb370 — Add mock turn acceptance coverage
7. 365d105 — Surface memory cycle diagnostics
8. 1f6ed4b — Document future settings architecture
9. 53a12d1 — Separate setting controls from Soul identity
10. c526a87 — Add Setting Soul lifecycle
11. 802e588 — Align API hidden state prompt
12. 21f5efd — Add turn debug panel
13. 6f07c51 — Improve chat workspace controls

Working title:

Devlog 004, Teaching the App What to Send the Model

준 로그보면 감상 있지 않아? 좋다던가 몰입감 늘었다거나...? 아무 피드백도 없어 이단계에?


chatgpt response

맞아. 피드백/감상 있음. 내가 Devlog 003 초안에서 너무 “기술 구현 로그”처럼만 썼고, 실제 로그에 있던 첫 체감, 좋은 느낌, 동시에 불안한 느낌을 빼먹었어.

내가 확인한 로그 기준으로는 이 단계 감상이 대략 이거임:

1. mock provider 쪽은 “앱 모양이 생겼다”, “full loop is breathing”, “working prototype” 느낌이 있었음.
2. encoded hidden state + consolidation 테스트는 꽤 좋아 보였음.
3. 실제 외부 API 테스트에서는 “Seems nice”라는 첫 반응이 있었음.
4. 그런데 동시에 “worriful actions”가 보였고, narrator가 아니라 character처럼 말하는 문제가 드러남.
5. 그래서 이 단계는 순수한 성공기가 아니라, “처음으로 앱이 살아 움직였는데, 바로 구조적 위험도 보인 시기”로 써야 맞음.

즉 Devlog 003에는 이런 감정선이 들어가야 함:

The app finally felt like it was doing something.
But the first real test was not a clean victory.
It felt nice.
Then it immediately showed me what was still wrong.

Devlog 003에 추가해야 할 핵심 문단

아래 문단을 OpenRouter/real model testing 부분 뒤에 넣으면 좋아.

The first real test felt good in a way the mock never could.

For the first time, the app was not just clicking through a fake pipeline. A real model answered. The chat moved. The narration had atmosphere. The scene had texture. It was not the final experience, but it was enough to make the project feel less imaginary.

That mattered.

A working mock can tell you the pipe is connected.

A real model can make you feel the product.

And for a moment, it did feel like something was there.

But that good feeling came with a warning almost immediately.

The output was nice, but it was also wrong in a very important way. The model was still talking too much like the character instead of acting like a narrator. It slipped into first-person character presence. It made the experience feel more like a normal character chatbot than the GM/narrator architecture I wanted.

That worried me.

Because Mnemosyne was not supposed to be another character impersonator.

The whole point was that the narrator should describe the character, while the Soul and World Log carried the continuity underneath. If the model collapsed into “being” the character, then the knowledge boundaries would collapse too. The character could steal narrator knowledge. The narrator could steal the user’s agency. The system could start drifting back into the exact platform behavior I was trying to escape.

So the first real test was encouraging and alarming at the same time.

It proved the app could feel good.

It also proved that feeling good was not enough.

이걸 넣으면 Devlog 003 흐름이 더 정확해짐

Tauri app 시작
mock provider는 placeholder
OpenRouter/free models로 real testing
hidden state 문제
encoded payload
diagnostics

하지만 narrator가 character처럼 말하는 문제 발견
그래서 narrator architecture가 더 중요해짐
hidden state 숨기기/parser/diagnostics로 넘어감

이게 훨씬 blog 같고, 네 실제 개발 경험에 가까움.

The good feeling came with a warning almost immediately.

The output was nice, but it was also wrong in a very important way. The model was still talking too much like the character instead of acting like a narrator. It slipped into first-person character presence. It made the experience feel closer to a normal character chatbot than the GM/narrator architecture I wanted.

That worried me.

Because Mnemosyne was not supposed to be another character impersonator.

The whole point was that the narrator should describe the character, while the Soul and World Log carried continuity underneath. If the model collapsed into “being” the character, then the knowledge boundaries would collapse too.

The character could steal narrator knowledge.

The narrator could steal the user’s agency.

The system could drift back into the exact platform behavior I was trying to escape.

So the first real test was encouraging and alarming at the same time.

It proved the app could feel good.

It also proved that feeling good was not enough.

A real model might forget to include the hidden state.

A real model might expose the hidden block to the user.

A real model might wrap the hidden state in the wrong format.

A real model might summarize instead of updating.

A real model might produce beautiful narration and then ruin the machine-readable part.

A real model might keep adding memories instead of letting anything fade.

A real model might understand the rule, explain the rule, and then fail to follow it three turns later.

That was exactly the kind of failure I needed to see.

Because the point was not to make a toy demo work.

The point was to find the places where a real model would break the architecture.

One of the first problems was formatting.

At first, hidden state could be plain JSON. That was easy to inspect, easy to test, and easy to understand. But plain JSON was also fragile and ugly. It was too easy for the model to leak it, corrupt it, or blend it into the visible response.

So the hidden state had to become more controlled.

The app needed a recognizable marker.

The payload needed to be compact.

The parser needed to support the new format without destroying older test transcripts.

That was why encoded hidden state became important.

The hidden block moved toward a versioned payload format: something the engine could identify, decode, and parse without treating it like normal prose.

That did not make the system perfect.

But it made the boundary sharper.

The narration was for the player.

The encoded hidden state was for the engine.

That boundary was everything.

Around the same time, I also started seeing why debug tools mattered.

When hidden state is visible, debugging is easy. You just read the ugly block.

When hidden state is actually hidden, debugging becomes harder. If the Soul updates wrong, I need to know why. If the parser fails, I need to know where. If a memory gets added, scored, discarded, or consolidated, I need to see the trace.

So diagnostics became part of the early app.

Not as polish.

As survival.

I needed a way to inspect the memory cycle without exposing the machinery to the player. I needed to see what the model returned, what the parser extracted, what the engine accepted, and what changed inside the Soul.

That was the practical reason for turn debug panels and memory cycle diagnostics.

The player should not see the machinery.

But the developer absolutely needs to.

This was also where the narrator architecture became more urgent.

Before the real test, narrator mode was mostly an architecture principle.

After the real test, it became a practical requirement.

If the AI was allowed to behave like the character directly, it would blur everything. It might speak in first person. It might steal the user’s actions. It might treat narrator knowledge as character knowledge. It might expose internal state as dialogue.

That was not Mnemosyne.

Mnemosyne needed the model to act as a narrator.

The narrator writes the visible scene.

The hidden state reports candidate changes.

The app strips and parses the hidden state.

The engine updates the Soul and World Log.

The player only sees the story.

That sounds obvious now, but this was the stage where it started becoming obvious through bugs.

Every failure pointed back to the same lesson:

The model should not carry the whole system by itself.

At this stage, I was still asking the LLM to do too much inside one response.

It had to write the scene.

It had to follow the selected narrative mode.

It had to respect the user’s agency.

It had to avoid leaking hidden machinery.

It had to flag memories.

It had to judge emotional importance.

It had to update relationship values.

It had to keep the Soul coherent.

It had to remember to forget.

That was too much for one generation to do perfectly.

But I had not fully escaped that yet.

This was not the dual-pass breakthrough.

That came later.

At this point, the lesson was smaller but important:

The app needed to catch the model’s output and begin enforcing boundaries around it.

The AI could propose.

The engine had to manage.

That was the start of the real boundary.

Not complete.

Not clean.

But visible.

The mock provider gave the interface a shape.

OpenRouter models gave the system real failures.

The first real test gave me a glimpse of the product.

Encoded hidden state gave the engine something it could parse.

Diagnostics gave me a way to see what was happening behind the curtain.

And the Tauri app gave all of it a place to run.

That was the point where Mnemosyne stopped being only a conceptual memory system.

It became an app trying to keep the magic invisible.

Devlog 004 방향

추천 제목

Devlog 004, Teaching the App What to Send the Model

추천 부제

Context Compiler, SessionWorld, and the First Payload Discipline

이 제목이 좋은 이유는 003과 이어짐.

Devlog 003:
앱이 모델 output을 숨기고 parse하는 법을 배움.

Devlog 004:
앱이 모델 input을 고르는 법을 배우기 시작함.

003이 output discipline이라면, 004는 input discipline임.

핵심 뉘앙스

004는 “와 기능 하나 만들었다!” 느낌보다:

The model is only as smart as the context I give it.

이 깨달음으로 가야 함.

느낌은 대충 이거:

처음에는 hidden state를 숨기고 Soul에 먹이는 게 핵심처럼 보였다.
그런데 실제로 돌려보니, 출력만 통제해서는 부족했다.
모델이 다음 장면을 잘 쓰려면, 애초에 무엇을 기억으로 받아야 하는지 앱이 골라줘야 했다.
전체 로그를 던지는 것도 아니고, 랜덤 요약을 던지는 것도 아니고,
Soul, World Log, recent events, relationship, current state를 조립해서 보내야 했다.

즉 004는 “context compiler가 그냥 요약기가 아니라, narrator의 시야를 만드는 장치였다”로 잡으면 좋음.


Devlog 004 내용 구조

1. 003에서 바로 이어지는 시작

Hidden state made the app listen to the model.

But the next problem was the opposite direction:

What should the app say back to the model before the next turn?

여기 좋음.

003에서는:

model response → hidden state → parser → Soul update

004에서는:

Soul + World Log + recent chat → context compiler → model prompt

이 반대 방향을 다루면 됨.

2. “전체 채팅을 계속 보내면 안 된다”

이건 네 프로젝트의 핵심 차별점임.

Normal AI RP platforms survive by dragging more context forward.
Mnemosyne could not do that forever.

하지만 너무 “token savings”만 강조하면 약해짐. 더 좋은 표현은:

The goal was not only smaller context.

The goal was cleaner context.

Game-Plan 쪽에서도 “token savings are proven enough, now selected memory quality matters more than compression” 흐름이 있음. 

3. Context Compiler의 역할

여기서 technical하게 설명.

Context compiler는 그냥 summary가 아니라:

- 현재 상태
- 캐릭터 기억
- 최근 사건
- 관계 상태
- World Log / SessionWorld snapshot
- 최근 대화 몇 턴

을 조립하는 엔진.

초기 설계에서 context compiler는 [CURRENT STATE], [CHARACTER MEMORY], [RECENT EVENTS], [RELATIONSHIP], recent chat을 출력하고 token budget으로 자르는 구조였음. 

블로그 문장으로는:

The context compiler became the narrator’s briefing room.

이거 좋음.

4. Soul과 World Log 분리가 여기서 진짜 의미를 가짐

002에서 개념적으로 나눴던 게, 004에서 practical해짐.

The Soul could not just exist as a file.

The World Log could not just exist as a file.

They had to become usable context.

즉:

Soul remembers like a person.
World Log remembers like a GM.
Context Compiler decides what the narrator gets to see.

이 3단계로 정리 가능.

5. 감정선: “이게 똑똑해 보였지만, 동시에 무서워짐”

004는 좋은 의미로 더 engineering 느낌이 남.

처음에는:

오, 이제 전체 채팅 안 보내도 되네.
작은 compact packet으로 장면이 이어진다.

근데 바로 다음에:

근데 잘못된 memory가 들어가면?
잘못된 world snapshot이 들어가면?
잘못된 relationship이 들어가면?
그럼 모델이 그걸 진실처럼 믿고 다음 장면을 씀.

이 불안감이 나와야 함.

이건 roadmap 후반의 “compact context가 작아지는 것보다 wrong context가 되는 게 더 위험하다” 흐름이랑 맞음. 

6. Patch protocol은 004 후반 또는 005 초반

Add patch protocol v1 foundation이 있으니까 넣을 수는 있음. 다만 004의 중심을 너무 넓히면 또 산만해짐.

내 추천:

004 후반에 이렇게만 언급:

Once context became a compiled artifact, state changes also needed to become more explicit. That was the seed of the patch protocol: the app should not just mutate memory vaguely. It should record what changed.

그리고 005에서 본격적으로:

Devlog 005, Making State Changes Auditable

로 patch protocol, ledger, regenerate, retcon, branch, rebuild 쪽을 다루는 게 좋음.

7. Narrator guard는 004에 살짝, 005/006에 본격

Add narrator attribution guard와 response integrity guard는 context compiler랑 연결되지만, 주제는 다름. 004에서 너무 깊게 다루지 말고:

As the prompt became more structured, another issue became clearer: the narrator still needed guardrails.

정도로 끝내고, 다음 글 후보로 넘기면 좋음.


Devlog 004의 중심 문장

이 글의 핵심 문장은 이거라고 봄:

The hidden state taught the app how to read the model.

The context compiler taught the app how to brief the model.

그리고 더 강한 결론:

Mnemosyne was no longer asking, “How much chat can I fit?”

It was asking, “What does the narrator actually need to know?”

이게 004의 thesis임.

최종 판단

004는 이렇게 뽑으면 됨:

Title:
Devlog 004, Teaching the App What to Send the Model

Core:
hidden state가 output을 숨기고 읽는 문제였다면,
context compiler는 input을 선별하고 조립하는 문제였다.

Tone:
“이제 앱이 단순히 반응하는 게 아니라, narrator의 시야를 설계하기 시작했다.”
흥분 + 불안.

message time: 2026-05-30 17:53:58

동의한다. 004 만들어봐


chatgpt response

좋아. 아래는 Devlog 004 초안, 아직 HTML/commit 아님. 읽기용 텍스트.

문서 처리 확인

파일 길이/처리 섹션 흐름
Roadmap-update.txt 전체 로드됨 Mnemosyne Updated Roadmap → P0 → 7B.1 → 7B.2 → 7C → 7D → 7E → 7F → 7G → 7H → Phase 8 → Phase 9 → Current immediate order
road-map-ver-1.txt 이전 처리 기준 425 lines P0 safety → scene/OOC validation → ledger/branch → API settings → memory provenance/retrieval → alpha
road-map-ver-2.txt 이전 처리 기준 758 lines current verified state → P0/P1/P2/P3/P4/P5/P6/P7 → immediate order

전체적으로 004는 roadmap의 “compact context, memory slot quality, SessionWorld, payload discipline” 흐름에 맞춤. 특히 roadmap은 full chat 재전송보다 compact state/memory/relationship/world summaries를 보내는 설계를 안전한 claim으로 잡고 있고, memory selection quality가 raw compression보다 중요하다고 정리함.  


Devlog 004, Teaching the App What to Send the Model

The Context Compiler Became the Narrator’s Briefing Room

Hidden state taught Mnemosyne how to read the model.

The next problem was the opposite direction.

What should Mnemosyne send back to the model before the next turn?

That question sounded smaller than it was.

At first, I was mostly worried about output. Could the app hide the machine-readable block? Could it parse the hidden state? Could it update the Soul without showing ugly JSON to the player? Could it keep the magic invisible?

But once that pipeline started existing, the other half of the system became harder to ignore.

The model does not just need to answer.

It needs to be briefed.

If the app sends the wrong context, the model writes the wrong scene.

If the app sends too little, the model forgets.

If the app sends too much, the model drowns.

If the app sends the wrong kind of memory in the wrong place, the narrator starts treating emotional memory like world fact, or world fact like character knowledge.

That was the next architecture problem.

Not:

How do I make the AI remember?

But:

What does the narrator actually need to know right now?

That became the job of the Context Compiler.

Before this, I was still close to the normal AI RP mindset: keep the chat alive by dragging more context forward. More conversation. More summary. More memory. More tokens. Hope the model figures out what matters.

But that approach was not Mnemosyne.

Mnemosyne was supposed to avoid turning every long session into a giant bag of text.

The goal was not only smaller context.

The goal was cleaner context.

That distinction mattered.

A compact context packet is useless if it is wrong.

A short memory summary is dangerous if it mixes up who knows what.

A world snapshot is harmful if it preserves stale plot threads as if they are still active.

A relationship memory is bad if it leaks into the location slot.

A recent event is bad if it repeats the same setup every turn and convinces the model the scene keeps restarting.

That was the moment context stopped feeling like a prompt convenience and started feeling like infrastructure.

The Context Compiler became the narrator’s briefing room.

It had to gather the pieces:

Current state.

Soul memory.

World Log.

Recent events.

Relationship status.

Current plot pressure.

Recent chat.

And then it had to decide what the model actually needed for the next response.

Not everything.

The right things.

That connected directly back to the memory split.

The Soul remembers like a person.

The World Log remembers like a GM.

The Context Compiler decides what the narrator gets to see.

Those three ideas started locking together.

The Soul by itself was not enough.

The World Log by itself was not enough.

They had to become usable context.

A memory sitting in a file does not help the narrator unless it is selected, shaped, and placed into the prompt at the right time.

A world fact sitting in storage does not keep continuity unless the narrator sees it before writing the next scene.

The app needed to stop treating context as a pile and start treating it as a briefing.

That was where the sections started to matter.

A relationship memory should go into relationship memory.

A location fact should go into world or location memory.

A current unresolved problem should go into current plot or unresolved tension.

A short-term emotional reaction should go into recent emotional state, not permanent identity.

A few clean recent messages should remain as conversation texture, but they should not replace structured state.

The model needed a readable packet.

Something like:

Here is where the scene is.

Here is who the character is right now.

Here is what the character personally remembers.

Here is what the world says is objectively true.

Here is what the current relationship pressure is.

Here are the last few clean messages.

Now write the next moment.

That was the feeling.

The model should not be forced to dig through a transcript like an archaeologist.

The app should hand it the right briefing.

This was also where token savings became less interesting by itself.

It was nice if Mnemosyne used fewer tokens than resending the full chat. That was part of the design. But the deeper value was not just compression.

Compression can destroy meaning.

A bad summary can be worse than no summary.

The real goal was selection.

Which memory matters now?

Which world fact is still active?

Which relationship detail should affect this line of dialogue?

Which old event should stay buried?

Which tension is unresolved enough to remain in the narrator’s field of view?

That was the hard part.

The roadmap later put this into clearer terms: memory retrieval quality matters more than raw compression, and the next intelligence jump comes from structured memory routing, not simply adding more memory. 

At the time, I was feeling that problem through the app.

A compact context packet could make the model feel smarter.

But a wrong compact context could poison the scene.

That made the Context Compiler exciting and scary at the same time.

Exciting because it meant the app could guide the model instead of begging the model to remember everything.

Scary because the app was now responsible for what became “truth” in the next prompt.

If the Context Compiler included a bad memory, the narrator might treat it as real.

If it omitted a critical emotional memory, the character might feel shallow.

If it kept a resolved conflict in current plot, the model might keep reopening it.

If it sent narrator-only information as character memory, the character might become omniscient.

That was the burden of the briefing room.

The compiler was not just saving tokens.

It was controlling the narrator’s attention.

This is where the project started to feel less like a chat wrapper and more like a state machine around storytelling.

The LLM was still the writer.

But the app was starting to decide what the writer walked into the room knowing.

That is a dangerous kind of power.

And it needed rules.

The early shape was simple enough:

Keep the current state compact.

Keep recent chat clean.

Include the strongest relevant memories.

Include the active world snapshot.

Do not flood the prompt with every old fact.

Do not let stale events act like current events.

Do not confuse character knowledge with narrator knowledge.

Do not let one memory category dominate the whole context.

That last part became more important over time.

If relationship memories flood the prompt, every scene becomes about trust pressure.

If world memories flood the prompt, the character becomes emotionally flat.

If recent events flood the prompt, the model loops the same scene.

If identity memories flood the prompt, the character becomes rigid and never reacts to the present.

The Context Compiler had to balance categories.

Not perfectly yet.

But the need was visible.

The app was beginning to learn that “memory” was not one slot.

It was routing.

Relationship memory.

Current plot memory.

Character identity memory.

Unresolved tension.

World and location memory.

Recent emotional state.

Each kind of memory had a different job.

Each had to earn its place in the prompt.

The roadmap later formalized those exact slots and added stricter rules: no emotional facts in world/location memory, no generic body language as durable memory, unresolved tension should detect pressure and ambivalence, stale plots should decay, and resolved plots should move out of current context. 

That was the direction the Context Compiler was pointing toward.

It was not enough to have a Soul.

It was not enough to have a World Log.

The app had to decide how those stores became a prompt.

That also made the API payload more important.

Before, an API call felt like “send the chat to the model.”

Now it felt like a constructed artifact.

A payload had shape.

A payload had sections.

A payload had budget.

A payload had risks.

What goes into the system prompt?

What goes into the current-state block?

How many recent messages are enough?

How much memory is too much?

Which memories belong in a compact slot?

What should be hidden from the user but visible to the narrator?

What should be hidden from the character but visible to God mode?

What should never be sent at all?

Those questions made payload discipline part of the architecture.

This was also where SessionWorld started to matter more.

A world snapshot is not just flavor.

It is how the app prevents the model from losing the room.

The character can be confused.

The player can be surprised.

The narrator can choose what to reveal.

But the app should know where the scene is, what objects exist, what state those objects are in, what plot pressure is active, and what recent world events matter.

That is not the same job as the Soul.

The Soul says what the character became.

SessionWorld says what the scene still is.

The Context Compiler has to bring both into the prompt without letting them contaminate each other.

That was one of the big practical lessons of this phase.

Separate memory is not enough.

Separated memory has to stay separated when compiled.

Otherwise the split collapses at the exact moment the model receives the prompt.

This is also why the early guardrails started to feel necessary.

If the narrator receives a clean briefing but still steals the user’s actions, the scene breaks.

If it treats OOC as in-world dialogue, the state gets polluted.

If it repeats old setup, the scene loops.

If it pulls mock-template prose into the API path, the real model starts inheriting fake scaffolding.

If it treats a premature user turn as a real world event, the memory layer gets dirty.

So the Context Compiler phase naturally led toward narrator guardrails and response integrity checks.

But that was not the full breakthrough yet.

This was not the dual-pass architecture.

This was not the full evaluator era.

This was the point where Mnemosyne learned that input is architecture.

The model’s output mattered.

The hidden state mattered.

But the prompt that created the output mattered just as much.

The app had to stop sending “whatever fits.”

It had to send a deliberate briefing.

That changed how I thought about the whole system.

The context window was not just space.

It was attention.

Every token sent to the model told it what to care about.

Every memory included pushed the scene in a direction.

Every stale event left in context became a ghost.

Every missing relationship detail made the character feel less continuous.

So Devlog 004 is not about a flashy feature.

It is about the app learning to curate attention.

Hidden state taught the app how to read the model.

The Context Compiler taught the app how to brief the model.

And once that clicked, Mnemosyne was no longer asking:

How much chat can I fit?

It was asking:

What does the narrator actually need to know?
