수정된 004 opening 느낌

**Document Title: **

Devlog 004, The Whole Chat, But Compressed

Context Injection, Recent Messages, and the First Attempt at Carrying a Session Forward

After hidden state started working, I was not thinking, “What is the perfect context architecture?”

I was thinking something much more practical.

How do I keep feeding the model the whole session without literally feeding it the whole chat?

That was the idea.

Mnemosyne was supposed to carry the session forward through compressed data that kept updating over time. Not raw transcript. Not a giant wall of every message. Not platform auto-summary garbage. A living packet.

Current state.

Character memory.

Recent events.

Relationship status.

World information.

The last few clean messages.

That was the rough shape.

The model still needed recent chat because prose has rhythm. A summary alone cannot carry tone, pacing, or the exact feel of the last exchange. But the model also needed structured memory because recent chat alone cannot carry a long session.

So the early Context Compiler was not some genius relevance engine yet.

It was my first serious attempt to give the model a compressed version of the whole situation.

Enough to keep the scene alive.

Small enough to fit.

Structured enough that the model did not have to dig through the entire chat like an idiot.

At this point, I was still thinking in terms of “carry the whole session forward, but compressed.”

The more advanced question came later:

Which parts are actually relevant right now?

That distinction matters.

Because at first, compression itself felt like the win. If I could keep Soul state, world state, recent events, relationship pressure, and a few recent messages moving forward, then the model would have more continuity than normal AI RP platforms.

And honestly, that was already a big deal.

But as the project kept going, the weakness became clearer.

A compact context can still be wrong.

A short memory packet can still include the wrong memory.

A world summary can still keep stale plot alive.

A relationship note can still crowd out the current scene.

So Devlog 004 is not the moment where I magically solved relevance.

It is the moment where the app started carrying the chat as structured compressed context, and I began running into the next problem hiding underneath that:

compressed is not the same as relevant.

이렇게 바꾸면 네가 덜 멍청해 보임

이 버전은 “갑자기 와 Context Compiler란 뭘 보내야 하지?”가 아니라, 네가 이미 꽤 똑똑한 방향으로 생각하고 있었다는 걸 보여줌:

raw full chat은 안 됨
하지만 전체 session continuity는 필요함
그래서 compressed living context를 계속 업데이트해서 먹임
그 다음 문제로 relevance/slot quality가 생김

그리고 말투도 좀 더 네 쪽에 맞춰야 함. 너무 “The narrator’s briefing room” 같은 문장만 반복하면 예쁘긴 한데, 네 개발 블로그가 아니라 내가 정리한 백서처럼 보임. 004는 좀 더 이렇게 써야 맞음:

This was not elegant yet.
It was me trying to keep the damn session alive.

다음 단계로는 이 톤으로 Devlog 004 전체 초안 다시 뽑으면 됨.

chatgpt response

ㅇㅋ. 이번 버전은 “나중에 정리된 똑똑한 백서” 느낌 빼고, 지금 막 Context Compiler 쪽 밀고 있는 개발자 devlog 느낌으로 다시 잡을게. 핵심은 “full chat raw로 던지는 게 아니라, 계속 업데이트되는 compressed session packet을 먹이려 했다”로 고정함.

맞아. 이번에는 톤을 바꿨어.
이 버전은 “아키텍처 해설자” 말투가 아니라, 지금 막 context compiler 쪽 커밋하고 devlog 쓰는 사람 느낌으로 잡았어. 로그 기준으로도 context는 처음부터 [CURRENT STATE], [CHARACTER MEMORY], [RECENT EVENTS], [RELATIONSHIP], recent chat 같은 압축 패킷을 LLM 호출 전에 붙이는 구조였고, roadmap도 full chat 재전송 대신 compact state/memory/relationship/world summaries를 보내는 방향을 안전한 claim으로 잡고 있음.  

**Document Title: **

Devlog 004, The Whole Chat, But Compressed

Context Compiler V2 and the First Real Session Packet

After hidden state started working, I ran into the other side of the problem.

The app could now read something from the model.

But the model still needed something useful before it answered.

My original idea was not to feed the model the entire raw chat forever. That would be stupidly expensive, slow, and eventually impossible. But I also did not want the model to lose the session just because older messages fell out of context.

So the idea was simple:

Feed it the whole session, but compressed.

Not literally every message.

Not a giant transcript.

Not an auto-summary that eats the soul out of the scene.

A structured packet that keeps updating over time.

Current state.

Character memory.

Recent events.

Relationship pressure.

World information.

A few recent clean messages.

That was the shape of it.

The model still needs recent chat because writing has rhythm. If I only send summaries, the model loses the exact tone of the last exchange. It forgets how the scene is moving. It forgets the texture.

But recent chat alone is not enough either.

Recent chat can carry pacing.

It cannot carry a long campaign.

That was where the Context Compiler started becoming important.

The Context Compiler was not some perfect relevance engine yet. It was my first serious attempt to keep the whole session alive without dragging the whole chat behind every turn.

That distinction matters.

I was not trying to make the prompt tiny just for the sake of being tiny.

I was trying to make the model start each turn with the right compressed situation.

The app needed to say:

Here is the current scene.

Here is what the character remembers.

Here is what the world says is true.

Here is the current relationship state.

Here is what just happened recently.

Here are the last few messages so the prose still has continuity.

Now continue.

That was the job.

In Devlog 002, I split memory into Soul and World Log.

In Devlog 003, I started making hidden state work, so the app could take model output and update the Soul behind the scenes.

Now, in Devlog 004, the problem is input.

The updated Soul and World Log are useless if the model never sees them.

So Context Compiler V2 is about taking that stored state and turning it into something the narrator can actually use.

That is the key difference between memory existing and memory being useful.

A Soul file sitting in storage does nothing by itself.

A World Log sitting in storage does nothing by itself.

They have to become prompt context.

And not just dumped in randomly. They need structure.

That is why I started thinking in sections.

Current state should not be mixed with relationship memory.

Relationship memory should not be mixed with room layout.

World facts should not be treated like the character’s personal emotional memory.

Recent chat should not replace structured state.

Each piece has a job.

The Soul carries what happened inside the character.

The World Log carries what remains true about the scene.

Recent chat carries the immediate rhythm.

The Context Compiler puts those pieces together before the model writes.

This is where the earlier memory split started paying off.

If everything is just one summary, the model has to guess what kind of information it is reading.

Is this something the character knows?

Is this something only the narrator knows?

Is this an emotional scar?

Is this a physical object in the room?

Is this still active, or is it old plot noise?

That guessing is dangerous.

It is how character memory and world continuity start bleeding into each other.

So the compiler needed to preserve the separation.

Soul information should stay Soul-shaped.

World information should stay World-shaped.

Recent events should stay recent.

Relationships should stay relationship-specific.

That is the difference between “summary” and “state.”

A summary is just a compressed version of text.

State is the current condition of the session.

Mnemosyne needs state.

That was the bigger realization behind this part.

Normal AI RP often survives by stretching context. More tokens. More chat. More lore. More summary. More everything.

Mnemosyne is trying to do something else.

The app should carry the state, then brief the model.

That does not mean less context is always better.

Bad compact context is still bad.

If the packet is missing the important emotional memory, the character feels shallow.

If the packet keeps stale plot active, the scene loops.

If the packet sends narrator-only knowledge as character memory, the character starts acting omniscient.

If the packet only sends relationship tension, every scene becomes relationship tension.

So compression alone is not the win.

The win is carrying the session forward in a way the model can actually use.

That is why I care about sections, slots, and budgets.

Not because I want the architecture to look fancy.

Because without that structure, the model gets a messy pile and does model things with it.

Sometimes that works.

Sometimes it confidently continues the wrong version of the scene.

The Context Compiler is me trying to stop that before it happens.

The early version is still rough, but the direction is there.

The compiler builds a packet out of the active Soul, the world state, recent events, relationship info, and clean recent messages. Then the API call uses that packet instead of blindly shoving the whole chat into the model.

That already changes the shape of the app.

The model is no longer the only thing responsible for continuity.

The app is carrying continuity.

The model is writing from it.

That is the difference I wanted.

This also makes debugging more serious.

If the model says something wrong, it might not be only a model problem.

Maybe the compiled context was wrong.

Maybe the memory slot was wrong.

Maybe the world snapshot was stale.

Maybe recent chat was too short.

Maybe the wrong relationship memory got pulled forward.

Maybe the prompt gave the narrator knowledge the character should not have.

That is annoying, but it is also useful.

Because now the failure has a place to live.

Before, if the AI forgot something, the answer was just “AI memory bad.”

Now I can ask a better question:

Did the app give the model the right state?

That is progress.

This is also why payloads started mattering more.

An API request is not just “send message.”

It is a constructed object.

System prompt.

Mode.

Compiled context.

Recent messages.

Provider settings.

Model choice.

State snapshot.

If that payload is messy, the output will be messy.

If that payload is clean, the model at least has a fighting chance.

This is where the project started feeling more like a real engine and less like a prompt experiment.

The hidden state path taught the app how to receive structured information from the model.

The Context Compiler path teaches the app how to send structured information to the model.

Both directions matter.

The app has to listen.

The app has to brief.

The app has to keep the state moving.

That is the whole point.

I am not at the final “relevant info engine” yet.

That comes later.

Right now, this is the first real version of the compressed session packet.

The whole chat, but not as raw chat.

The session, carried forward as state.

That is what Context Compiler V2 is for.

This devlog covers the first serious context/payload phase after hidden state became testable.

**Document Title: **

004, The Whole Chat, But Compressed

Context Compiler V2 and the First Real Session Packet

After hidden state started working, the next thing I pushed on was context.

The app could now take model output, strip the hidden block, and update state behind the scenes. That was good. But the next turn still had to start somewhere.

The model needed the session back.

Not the full raw chat. That would get expensive, slow, and eventually useless. But also not some lazy summary that cuts out the actual feel of the scene.

What I wanted was closer to a compressed living packet.

The whole session, but not as a transcript.

Current state.

Character memory.

Recent events.

Relationship pressure.

World information.

A few recent clean messages.

That was the shape I was chasing.

Recent chat still matters because writing has rhythm. If the model only gets summaries, it loses the tone of the last exchange. It forgets how the conversation feels. It can technically know what happened, but the scene starts sounding detached.

Structured memory matters because recent chat alone cannot carry a long session. It can keep the last few turns alive, but it cannot carry a character’s slow emotional changes, old tension, object state, world continuity, or relationship drift.

So the Context Compiler became the piece that tries to hold both.

Enough recent chat for flow.

Enough structured state for continuity.

Small enough to fit.

Clear enough for the model to use.

This is where the earlier Soul and World Log split started becoming useful in the app instead of just sounding good on paper.

The Soul carries the character’s subjective history.

The World Log carries the scene’s objective continuity.

The compiler turns both into something the model can read before writing the next response.

That is the important part.

Memory sitting in storage does not help anything by itself. A Soul file can be perfectly designed, but if the model never sees the right part of it, the character still acts like nothing happened. A World Log can track the room, objects, and active plot, but if that snapshot is not passed forward, the scene still drifts.

State has to be compiled.

That was the job.

The early version was rough, but the goal was clear: stop relying on the model to dig through chat history and start giving it a prepared session packet.

Not a giant lore dump.

Not the entire conversation.

Not a generic summary.

A structured packet that says, in effect:

Here is where the scene is.

Here is what still matters.

Here is what the character carries.

Here is what the world currently says is true.

Here is the recent dialogue texture.

Now continue.

This also changed how I thought about API payloads.

Before, a model request felt like sending a user message and waiting for a reply.

Now the request itself was becoming an object I had to care about.

System prompt.

Narration mode.

Compiled context.

Recent messages.

Provider settings.

Model choice.

State snapshot.

The quality of the output depended on what I put into that payload.

That sounds obvious, but it matters a lot when the whole project is about continuity. If the payload is messy, the model writes from a messy version of the session. If the payload is clean, the model at least has a fair shot.

Context Compiler V2 was the first real push toward that.

It improved the memory and world snapshots, and it made the input side of the loop feel more intentional. Hidden state handled what came back from the model. Context compilation handled what went into the model.

That made the system feel more complete.

There was now a loop:

Compile the current session.

Send the model a structured packet.

Get narration back.

Extract hidden state.

Update Soul and World.

Compile again.

That loop is the actual engine shape.

The model is still doing the writing, but the app is starting to carry the session instead of begging the model to remember everything. That is the difference.

I also started seeing why categories matter.

If relationship state, world state, recent events, and character memory all get mashed together, the model has to guess what each line means. That is where weird bleed happens. The character starts knowing things only the narrator should know. World facts start acting like emotional memories. Old events stay too sticky. Relationship pressure leaks into scenes where it should only be background tension.

So the compiler needs structure.

Relationship memory should stay relationship-shaped.

World facts should stay world-shaped.

Recent events should stay recent.

Character memory should stay tied to the Soul.

The point is not to make the prompt look organized. The point is to stop the model from confusing different kinds of truth.

A character feeling betrayed is not the same as a door being locked.

A phone being silenced is not the same as someone feeling ignored.

A plot thread being active is not the same as a memory becoming part of identity.

If the system mixes those together, the model will mix them together too.

This is also why I care about recent messages staying in the packet. Pure state is not enough. If I only pass structured memory, the model gets continuity but loses the sound of the scene. If I only pass recent chat, the model gets tone but loses long-term continuity.

The compiler is where those two meet.

That was the main work here.

Making the app carry the session as state, then feed the model enough of that state to keep going.

This is not flashy compared to visible UI features. But it is one of those parts that decides whether the whole thing actually works. A long-form RP memory engine cannot just save memories. It has to use them at the right time.

Otherwise it is just a storage system pretending to be intelligence.

The next issue is already obvious from this work: once the app is compiling state, state changes need to be more explicit too. If memory and world state are going to move every turn, I need to know what changed, where it came from, and whether it should survive regeneration or correction.

That is where the patch protocol starts to matter.

For now, Context Compiler V2 is the first proper step toward making the model write from a compressed session instead of a raw chat pile.

The whole chat, but compressed into state.

That is the direction.

187763a — Commit and push Context Compiler V2

Next

Patch protocol, explicit state changes, and making memory/world updates easier to trace instead of treating state mutation like magic.

# Voice Rules for Your Devlog

Use this as the writing filter before publishing.

```txt
1. First 3-5 lines must contain a real artifact:
   feature name, commit, UI, file, bug, test result, parser, payload, screenshot.

2. No “this devlog is not...” sentences.

3. No repeated sentence skeletons.
   If two lines start the same way, the third one must change structure.

4. Do not make yourself look like you discovered obvious things late.
   Frame it as: I built X, testing exposed Y, so I changed Z.

5. Keep philosophy after evidence.
   Concrete thing first, interpretation second.

6. One section = one job.
   Do not mix what changed, why, how, and next steps in the same block.

7. End with the next engineering constraint.
   Not a motivational quote.

Best Title Format

Use this format:

# 004, [Concrete change]

**Document Title: **

# 004, The Whole Chat, But Compressed

## Context Compiler V2, input payloads, and state injection

This pass was about the input side.

Hidden state was already starting to give the app a way to read model output. Now I needed the other half of the loop: what the model gets before it writes the next response.

The core change is `Context Compiler V2`.

The goal was not to keep feeding the model the raw chat forever. That path gets expensive, bloated, and eventually useless. But I also did not want to throw a generic summary at the model and hope continuity survived.

I wanted a session packet.

The whole chat, but compressed into usable state.

### What changed

Context Compiler V2 started turning the active session into a more deliberate payload.

Instead of relying on recent messages alone, the app now pushes a structured packet toward the model:

- current state
- Soul memory
- World Log
- recent events
- relationship state
- recent clean messages

This is the point where the saved state starts becoming actual model input, not just data sitting somewhere in the app.

That matters because a memory system that never reaches the prompt is just storage.

### Why I touched this

Recent chat is good at keeping tone.

It remembers the exact rhythm of the last few messages. It keeps the conversation from sounding like a cold summary. It preserves the last emotional beat, the wording, and the way the scene is moving.

But recent chat cannot carry a long session.

A few messages do not hold slow trust changes, old tension, physical object state, background world facts, or the character’s longer emotional history.

Structured state solves that side of the problem.

The Soul carries what the character has become.

The World Log carries what the scene still is.

Recent events keep the last actions from vanishing.

Relationship state keeps the pressure from resetting.

The compiler’s job is to put those pieces together before the model writes.

Not as a lore dump.

Not as raw transcript.

As a packet the model can actually use.

### How the packet works

The current loop looks like this:

```txt
Soul
World Log
Recent Events
Relationship State
Recent Messages
        ↓
Context Compiler V2
        ↓
Provider Payload
        ↓
Model Response
        ↓
Visible Narration + Hidden State
        ↓
Strip / Parse
        ↓
Soul / World Patch
        ↓
Updated Session State

That loop is the app starting to act like an engine.

The model still writes the scene, but the app is carrying the session between turns. That is the difference I care about.

I am not asking the model to remember the entire campaign by vibes. I am giving it the current session state, then making it write from that.

What got better

The model no longer has to start from a loose chat pile.

It gets a clearer setup before the next turn:

where the scene is, what happened recently, what the character carries emotionally, what the world says is still true, and what relationship pressure is active.

That makes the output easier to debug too.

If the next response goes weird, I can check the compiled packet. Maybe the model failed. Maybe the packet missed something. Maybe stale state got carried forward. Either way, the failure has a place to live now.

That is much better than just saying “AI memory bad” and guessing.

What is still rough

The packet now has power over the next scene.

If the wrong thing enters the compiled state, the model may treat it as truth. If relationship pressure is missing, the character feels flatter. If old plot stays active, the scene can loop. If world information gets mixed into character memory, the character starts knowing things they should not know.

So the compiler cannot just compress. It has to preserve the type of information.

Soul memory stays Soul-shaped.

World state stays world-shaped.

Recent events stay recent.

Relationship state stays relationship-specific.

That separation is the whole reason the previous memory split mattered.

Why patch protocol entered the same range

Once the app starts compiling state into the next payload, state changes need a trail.

It is not enough for memory and world state to just mutate somewhere in the database. If the app is going to support regenerate, correction, restore, and branch logic later, then changes need to be explicit.

That is why patch protocol starts showing up in this range.

Context Compiler V2 handles what gets sent forward.

Patch protocol starts handling how state changes are recorded.

Those two belong near each other because they are two sides of the same loop.

Covered commits

af5f3ae — Add patch protocol v1 foundation  
b4ad92a — Merge pull request #2 from RhyGPU/feat/patch-protocol-v1  
187763a — Commit and push Context Compiler V2

Next

Next is making state changes easier to trace.

If the app is going to compile Soul and World state into every turn, then every important change needs a source. Memory cannot just appear. World state cannot just drift. Corrections and regenerations need to know what changed, where it came from, and whether it still belongs on the active branch.

This version is much closer to the template: concrete opening, sections with jobs, commit scope tied to the work, no “this entry is not...” framing, and less repeated rhythm.

Your intent was not “what should the model know right now?” as a polished abstract question.
It was more like:

Your design was not “state only.” It was compressed state + recent clean narration. The logs say the context injection gets prepended to the system prompt and the last 3-5 messages before the LLM call, with a target under 2,000 tokens.

The context compiler was supposed to detect active characters, include present characters, separate character memories, recent events, and relationships, then narrate all relevant characters in third person. 


Later roadmap thinking sharpens it into category routing, but that is later than 004.
The roadmap says memory selection needs category slots, like relationship memory, current plot memory, identity memory, unresolved tension, world/location memory, and recent emotional state. That is the next intelligence jump, but not necessarily the emotional center of 004.

Revised 004 skeleton

# 004, Feeding the Model a Session Packet

## Context Compiler V2, clean recent chat, and state injection

### Opening
Context Compiler V2 is where the app starts building the next model input instead of just waiting for the model to answer.

The previous work made hidden state readable: model output could contain machine state, the app could strip it, parse it, and update Soul/World. This pass is the other half. Before the next response, the app needs to give the model the session back.

Not raw chat.
Not just a summary.
A compressed session packet.

### What changed
- Context Compiler V2 builds a structured input packet.
- The packet includes current state, Soul memory, World Log, recent events, relationship state, and recent clean messages.
- Hidden state stays middleware-only.
- The LLM sees processed state plus clean recent narration, not raw machine JSON.
- Patch protocol starts entering the same range because state changes now need a trace.

### Why I touched this
The external API tests made the problem obvious. Without the right system prompt and context shape, the model drifts into normal character-chat behavior. It talks as the character, leaks narrator confusion, or loses the distinction between user, narrator, character, and engine.

The app cannot just trust the model to remember the session.

The app has to carry the session.

### How the loop works
Soul / World / Recent Events / Relationship / Recent Clean Messages
→ Context Compiler
→ Provider Payload
→ Model Response
→ Visible Narration + Hidden State
→ Strip / Parse
→ Soul / World Patch
→ Updated State
→ Compile again

### What got better
The model starts from a constructed session state instead of a loose chat pile.

That means the app can preserve:
- the long-term character arc through Soul
- objective scene continuity through World Log
- immediate rhythm through recent clean messages

The hidden-state block is not fed back raw. It goes through middleware first.

### What is still rough
The model can still drift if the prompt or payload is wrong.

The test logs already showed the danger:
- no system prompt means character-chat behavior
- bad hidden-state formatting breaks continuity
- memory can bloat if the model is asked to manage decay by itself
- OOC and narrator identity need stronger boundaries

So this pass proves the input loop, but it also points directly toward stricter state changes, patch protocol, and later memory slot routing.

### Covered commits
1. `af5f3ae` — Add patch protocol v1 foundation
2. `b4ad92a` — Merge pull request #2 from RhyGPU/feat/patch-protocol-v1
3. `187763a` — Commit and push Context Compiler V2

### Next
State changes need a trail.

If compiled context is going to become the source of the next response, then Soul and World updates cannot be vague. The app needs to know what changed, what turn caused it, and whether that change still belongs on the active branch.

That is the direction I should use for the actual rewrite. It is less “invented thesis,” more faithful to the logs: you were wiring the model input side after proving the output side, while trying to keep session continuity without raw-chat bloat.

chatgpt response

좋아. 이번엔 “설계 설명”으로 안 감. 004는 Context Compiler V2를 막 넣은 개발 로그, 그리고 핵심은 “raw chat 대신 session packet을 먹이는 쪽으로 배선했다”로 잡겠음.

Processed basis for this rewrite: the current uploaded logs show that Context Compiler was already imagined as a context_injection containing [CURRENT STATE], [CHARACTER MEMORY], [RECENT EVENTS], and [RELATIONSHIP], prepended with the system prompt and last 3-5 messages, targeting under 2,000 tokens.  The hidden-state flow is also explicit: hidden state goes to the Rust middleware, gets decoded/parsed into Soul/World, and the LLM only receives processed summary plus clean recent messages next turn, not raw hidden JSON.  The devlog research says the entry should open with the concrete artifact and avoid meta-explaining itself. 

**Document Title: **

004, Feeding the Model a Session Packet

Context Compiler V2, clean recent chat, and state injection

Context Compiler V2 is where the input side started becoming real.

The app already had the output path taking shape. The model could return narration with a hidden-state block, the app could strip the block, decode it, and start updating Soul and World behind the curtain.

Now the next turn needed the reverse path.

The model had to get the session back.

Not as raw chat history forever. That was the exact kind of bloat I was trying to avoid. But also not as one dead summary that cuts out the rhythm of the scene.

I needed a packet.

Current state, character memory, recent events, relationship pressure, world information, and a few recent clean messages.

The whole session, but carried forward as state.

What changed

Context Compiler V2 started building the next provider payload from actual session pieces instead of treating the prompt as a loose pile.

The packet centered on:

CURRENT STATE
CHARACTER MEMORY
RECENT EVENTS
RELATIONSHIP
WORLD
clean recent messages

That last part matters.

The recent messages stay clean. No hidden block. No machine JSON. No encoded state being fed back into the model raw.

The hidden state is for the engine. The visible narration is for the chat history. The compiled packet is what the model gets next.

That separation is the whole point.

Why I touched this

The external API tests made the problem obvious fast.

Without the right input shape, the model drifts back into normal RP chatbot behavior. It talks as the character. It forgets that it is supposed to be a narrator. It treats prompt instructions like vibes instead of rules. It can also start mixing character knowledge, narrator knowledge, and user action if the payload is sloppy.

That was worrying because Mnemosyne is not supposed to be another character impersonator.

The app has to hold the session together.

The model writes from the state, but the model should not be trusted to carry the whole state by itself.

That was the real pressure behind this work.

How the packet works

The turn loop now starts to look like this:

Soul
World Log
Recent Events
Relationship State
Clean Recent Messages
        ↓
Context Compiler V2
        ↓
Provider Payload
        ↓
Model Response
        ↓
Visible Narration + Hidden State
        ↓
Strip / Parse
        ↓
Soul / World Patch
        ↓
Updated Session State
        ↓
Compile again

That is the engine shape I wanted.

The model still does the writing. The app carries the session.

The clean recent messages keep the prose alive. They preserve the last exchange, the tone, and the immediate rhythm.

The compiled state carries the long-term part: what the character remembers, where the scene is, what recently happened, and what the current relationship pressure looks like.

One side keeps the writing from going cold.

The other side keeps the session from going empty.

What got better

The model is no longer starting from only a few recent chat turns.

It gets a compact version of the session before writing: current scene, character memory, world state, recent events, relationship pressure, and the last clean messages.

That makes the whole thing easier to reason about.

If the next response goes wrong, I can check the payload.

Maybe the model ignored the instructions.

Maybe the compiled packet missed something important.

Maybe the recent chat window was too short.

Maybe the world state was stale.

That is still annoying, but it is better than the old version of the problem where the answer was just “the AI forgot.”

Now there is a place to look.

Why the categories matter

The Soul and World Log split only works if the compiler preserves it.

A character memory is not the same kind of thing as a room fact.

Relationship pressure is not the same thing as active plot.

Recent dialogue is not the same thing as long-term identity.

If those get mashed together, the model will mash them together too. That is how a character starts knowing narrator-only information, or how old emotional tension keeps bleeding into every scene.

So the packet needs sections.

Soul stays Soul-shaped.

World stays World-shaped.

Relationship stays relationship-shaped.

Recent messages stay as actual chat texture.

That sounds simple, but this is where the design starts paying rent. The split from earlier finally becomes something the app can feed into a model call.

Patch protocol entering the same range

Patch protocol started showing up here because compiled state creates a new problem.

If the app sends state forward every turn, then state changes need to be traceable.

A memory should not just appear.

A world event should not just mutate.

A relationship shift should not become permanent without knowing which turn caused it.

Regenerate, correction, restore, and branch logic all need that trail later.

So the input side and the patch foundation belong near each other. One decides what the next model call sees. The other starts making state changes explicit enough to survive edits.

Covered commits

af5f3ae — Add patch protocol v1 foundation  
b4ad92a — Merge pull request #2 from RhyGPU/feat/patch-protocol-v1  
187763a — Commit and push Context Compiler V2

Next

State changes need a source trail.

Now that the app can compile Soul and World into the next payload, the next pressure is making every meaningful update traceable: what changed, which turn caused it, what branch it belongs to, and whether it should still be active after regenerate or correction.

Actual 005 angle

Best fit: 005, Keeping the Narrator Honest

Subtitle:

Attribution guard, immediate continuity, and response integrity.

That sounds like a real devlog title, not a whitepaper.

Writing direction

Start with artifact, not philosophy:

After Context Compiler V2, the next problem was not more memory.

It was ownership.

The model had the session packet. It had recent chat. It had Soul and World context. But it still had to answer one basic question correctly:

Who actually did what?

Then go concrete fast:

The narrator cannot treat its own character dialogue like the user said it.
It cannot invent user actions to make the scene smoother.
It cannot replay the last exchange because the context packet repeated it.
It cannot let mock-provider lines leak into the real API path.

This is grounded in the attribution guard commit, which adds speaker ownership rules and explicitly says not to invent user actions, thoughts, motives, or dialogue. 

Recommended structure

# 005, Keeping the Narrator Honest

## Attribution guard, immediate continuity, and response integrity

Opening:
After Context Compiler V2, the app could feed the model a better session packet.
That made the next failure more obvious: the narrator could still misuse the packet.

Section 1: What changed
- Attribution and agency rules added to the narrator prompt
- Immediate continuity section added
- Recent chat budget adjusted to protect last user/assistant facts
- API response trace metadata added
- Response integrity guard rejects mock-template prose on API path
- Premature user-turn recent_events cleaned before they become state

Section 2: Why I touched this
- Real model tests showed narrator/persona confusion
- Model could act like the character instead of narrator
- Model could treat character lines as user statements
- Model could invent user action or repeat prior beats
- Clean context is useless if narrator abuses it

Section 3: Attribution guard
Explain:
- user words = actual user messages
- narrator/character words are not user dialogue
- character jokes are character jokes
- narrator must check what the user actually said before writing

Section 4: Immediate continuity
Explain:
- last narrator action
- last user action
- do not replay unless rewind/retcon requested
- this protects scene momentum

Section 5: Response integrity
Explain:
- API path should not return mock-template prose
- provider request/response metadata matters
- premature user-turn events should not become world state before the turn is actually resolved

Section 6: What got better
- fewer narrator ownership mistakes
- less replay
- better inspection of API turn origin
- cleaner separation between mock, API, opening messages
- stronger bridge into state/branch validation

Covered commits:
1. 70ed66a — Add narrator attribution guard
2. 38018a — Add narrator response integrity guard V1

Next:
SessionWorld routing and .mne portability validation.

Tone to use

Keep it direct and slightly sharp:

The model was not “forgetting” here.

It was cheating.

Not maliciously. Just in the usual LLM way:
smooth over the gap, invent the missing action, treat nearby text as if it had the same owner, and keep the scene moving.

That fits your style and the actual bug class.

Avoid:

- “This devlog is about...”
- “This was not the final...”
- repeated “A ___ can still ___” chains
- grand architecture summary

005 thesis

The core thesis should be:

A better context packet is not enough.

The narrator also has to respect ownership:
who spoke,
who acted,
what already happened,
and what the user actually did.

That is the right next entry. Then 006 can cleanly move to:

006, Making the World Portable
SessionWorld routing, .mne bundles, and export/import validation.

The key source points are: [IMMEDIATE CONTINUITY] was introduced because [RECENT CHAT] was being treated as background instead of a hard continuity constraint, with the distinction “Recent Chat = dialogue/prose continuity” and “Immediate Continuity = action-state continuity.”  Then the logs show the attribution bug: the narrator treated its own “second date” joke as if the user said it, which led to a targeted narrator attribution guard.  After that, the same scenario still failed, pushing the design from “more guard text” into a ranked Context Priority Stack V1. 

Here’s the draft.

**Document Title: **

005, Keeping the Scene From Replaying

Immediate continuity, attribution guard, and the first context priority stack

Context Compiler V2 gave the model a session packet.

That helped, but it exposed the next problem fast: the model could still continue from the wrong part of the scene.

The packet had Soul, World, relationship state, recent events, and recent chat. On paper, that sounded like enough. In practice, the model could still grab the wrong anchor. It could treat old dramatic prose as current physical state. It could replay a completed beat because that beat was emotionally loud. It could treat its own character dialogue like the user said it.

The issue was not just memory anymore.

It was priority.

The model needed to know which part of the context was legally binding.

This pass started with continuity guards and ended with a stronger context priority structure.

The first fix was [IMMEDIATE CONTINUITY].

Recent chat was useful, but it was not strict enough. It preserved what was said, but the model could still treat it like background. I needed a section that told the model where the scene currently was.

Not just:

Here is what was said.

But:

Here is where the scene is now. Continue from here. Do not replay.

That created a cleaner split:

Recent Chat = dialogue and prose continuity
Immediate Continuity = action-state continuity
World Log = scene and plot continuity
Soul Memory = character and emotional continuity

That was the first useful improvement.

Then the attribution issue showed up.

The model wrote a character joke, then later acted like the user had said the joke. That is not long-term memory failure. That is local speaker ownership failure.

So I added the narrator attribution guard.

The rule was simple: user facts come from user messages. Character dialogue is not user dialogue. Narrator prose is not user action. The narrator cannot invent user thoughts, motives, actions, or dialogue just to smooth the scene forward.

After that, the continuity problem still was not fully solved.

The model got better, but it could still resurrect stale physical facts. It could bring an old object back into the character’s hand because the old prose was more vivid than the current state. It could invent unsupported elapsed time because that made the scene flow better.

That is when the priority problem became obvious.

A guard says:

Please do not mess this up.

A priority stack says:

This section outranks that section.

That is the stronger fix.

The test case was simple.

A scene had already moved past a phone reveal and into the kitchen. The user accepted food and added a new emotional beat. The next response should continue from the kitchen, react to the new user input, and leave the completed reveal behind.

Instead, the model pulled the phone back into focus.

That told me the context packet was not authoritative enough.

It had the information, but the model did not know how to rank it. Recent chat, status prose, world events, character emotion, and the latest user message were all competing inside the same prompt. The model followed the most dramatic thing, not necessarily the most current thing.

That is bad for RP continuity.

A long scene cannot keep replaying whatever detail was most intense two turns ago. If a character already saw something, reacted, set it down, and moved to the kitchen, that state needs to be current fact. It should not be re-discovered because the prose was vivid.

So the goal became narrower:

Feed less clutter.

Rank the state harder.

Make recent chat support the scene, not override it.

[IMMEDIATE CONTINUITY] was the first version of that fix.

The idea was to promote the latest action state above recent chat.

Recent chat still matters. It keeps tone, rhythm, and dialogue texture alive. But it is not always a safe source of physical continuity. Prose can be long. It can contain metaphors, old actions, sensory detail, and status text. The model may latch onto the wrong part.

Immediate continuity was supposed to give the model a short checkpoint:

Completed scene facts:
Aurora has already seen and reacted to the phone reveal.
She moved toward the kitchen for food.

Latest user input:
The user accepted food and admitted loneliness/attraction.

Continue forward.
Do not replay completed beats.

That is much better than dumping the full previous assistant response into context again.

It is cheaper.

It is clearer.

It tells the model where the scene is instead of asking it to infer everything from prose.

The attribution bug was a different failure class.

The model did not forget. It confused ownership.

A character made a joke. Later, the same character reacted as if the user had said it. That is small in one response, but dangerous for an RP engine because the same pattern can become worse:

character speech becomes user speech
narrator metaphor becomes world fact
user intent gets invented
the narrator moves the user to keep the scene smooth
the model reacts to its own setup instead of the player’s actual input

So the guard had to be compact.

Not a giant prompt lecture.

Just enough pressure to remind the narrator:

User facts only come from user messages.

Character dialogue is character dialogue.

Narrator prose is not user action.

Do not invent user thoughts, motives, actions, or dialogue.

That was worth doing because it targets a common RP failure without spending many tokens.

The point was not perfection. The point was cheap damage reduction.

The next bug was time.

The model invented a concrete duration that the scene never established.

That is the same kind of issue as attribution drift. It fills a gap because the prose wants to move smoothly. For normal writing, that might be fine. For a continuity engine, it is dangerous.

If the World Log or user did not establish a timestamp or elapsed duration, the narrator should not make one up.

Vague pacing is fine.

Concrete time claims need support.

So the time rule stayed compact too:

Do not invent exact elapsed time, timestamps, or scene transitions unless the user or World Log supports them.

Again, this is not a full time engine. It is a small guard against an expensive class of mistakes.

The bigger fix was the Context Priority Stack.

At this point, adding more guard text was not the right move. The model already had instructions. The problem was that the context did not clearly say what outranked what.

So the compiled context needed a priority order.

The shape became:

P0 Latest user message
P1 Scene State
P2 Do Not Replay
P3 Active World
P4 Active Soul
P5 Relevant Memory
P6 Recent Chat Excerpt

That changed the role of recent chat.

Recent chat became tone support, not the highest authority.

Scene State became the physical continuity anchor.

Do Not Replay marked completed beats.

World and Soul state outranked old prose.

The latest user input stayed highest because the user is the new action.

That is the important difference.

Before this, the model could see a lot of context and still choose the wrong anchor. After this, the prompt at least tells it how to rank the context.

A guard asks the model to behave.

A priority stack gives the model a hierarchy.

Recent chat also needed cleanup.

Full assistant responses are expensive, and for this engine they can be actively harmful. They contain style, sensory detail, status blocks, metaphors, old physical positions, and dramatic phrasing. A lot of that is good for reading, but bad as authority.

So recent chat had to become shorter and lower priority.

The direction was:

strip assistant status blocks
strip hidden state if it appears
cap assistant excerpts
preserve user messages more strongly
keep recent chat for tone, not state authority
avoid breaking UTF-8 while trimming

That matches the project goal better: fewer tokens, better outcome.

Not less context for its own sake.

Less useless context.

The system became less dependent on the model guessing correctly from prose.

That is the main improvement.

Instead of throwing a dense recent scene at the model and hoping it infers the current physical state, the app started pushing a ranked state brief:

latest user input is the new action
scene state is current physical continuity
completed beats should not replay
world and Soul state override older chat
recent chat exists for tone

That is a better shape for long-form RP.

It also gives debugging a cleaner target. If the model repeats something, I can check whether the completed beat was missing from Do Not Replay. If it resurrects an object, I can check whether Scene State had the current object location. If it invents time, I can check whether Time State was blank or vague.

The failure gets a place to live.

The priority stack improved the situation, but it did not fully solve physical continuity.

The same kind of stale-state bug still showed up: the model could continue as if an object was still central after the scene had already moved on. It could also invent unsupported time because it sounded natural.

That was the limit of prompt and context fixes.

The system was still asking the LLM to infer physical state from recent events and excerpts.

That is not reliable enough.

Object position should not be hidden inside prose.

Completed actions should not only exist as text the model has to interpret.

Current physical state should be engine-owned state.

That points directly to Scene State V1.

70ed66a — Add narrator attribution guard  
38018a — Add narrator response integrity guard V1  
TBD — Compact continuity and attribution context / Context Priority Stack V1

The third commit needs a hash check before publishing. The chat log records the work and the reported test results, but I still need to match it to the exact Git commit before this goes live.

Next is Scene State V1.

The current system can rank context better, but it still relies too much on the model reading prose correctly. Scene State V1 should move current physical continuity into the engine:

current_physical_state
completed_beats

chatgpt response

I’ll verify this instead of defending the draft. The question is whether that paragraph is true as implemented code/commit, true as chat-log design intent, or me overpromising based on the plan.

Yes, but not exactly as written.

The core idea is true, but the wording currently overstates how successful/complete it was.

What is true

The priority-stack idea is real. The log explicitly says to “feed it a ranked state brief,” not narrative clutter, and proposes this priority order:

P0 Latest user message
P1 Scene State
P2 Do Not Replay
P3 Active World
P4 Active Soul
P5 Relevant Memory
P6 Recent Chat Excerpt

That source is directly in the chat log. 

It is also true that recent chat was supposed to become lower-priority tone/prose support. The log says recent chat should be “for tone and dialogue continuity only” and lower priority than scene state. 

It is true that the Codex-reported fix replaced [IMMEDIATE CONTINUITY] with [CONTEXT PRIORITY], [SCENE STATE], and [DO NOT REPLAY]. It also reported that [RECENT CHAT] became lower-priority tone context, with assistant messages capped, user messages capped higher, status blocks stripped, hidden state stripped, and UTF-8-safe truncation.  

What is too strong

This line is too confident:

Scene State became the physical continuity anchor.

Better:

Scene State was introduced as the intended physical continuity anchor.

Because the fix still failed afterward. The log says the model still said she was holding the phone and invented “three minutes,” then concludes that prompt/context fixes hit their limit and Scene State V1 needed to become engine-owned. 

This line is also a bit too absolute:

The system became less dependent on the model guessing correctly from prose.

Better:

The system tried to become less dependent on the model guessing correctly from prose.

Or sharper:

The compiler started reducing that dependency, but the test showed it was not enough yet.

What I would change in the draft

Replace this:

That changed the role of recent chat.

Recent chat became tone support, not the highest authority.

Scene State became the physical continuity anchor.

With this:

That changed what recent chat was supposed to do.

Recent chat stopped being treated as the main authority. It became tone and dialogue support.

Scene State became the intended physical-continuity anchor, with Do Not Replay marking completed beats above old prose.

Replace this:

The system became less dependent on the model guessing correctly from prose.

With:

The compiler became less dependent on raw prose, but not enough to solve physical continuity by itself.

Replace this:

That is a better shape for long-form RP.

With:

That was a better shape for long-form RP, but the next test showed the limit immediately.

Final verdict

The paragraph is true as design intent and reported implementation, but not true as a solved result.

005 should keep the priority-stack section, but phrase it as:

This was the first attempt to rank context authority.
It improved the shape of the prompt.
It did not fully solve the bug.
That failure is exactly why Scene State V1 became the next step.

That will be accurate and stronger.


you asked

message time: 2026-06-04 21:51:08

Ok push it to git based on the correct homepage style after comparing with the ver in the git already. dev log 005.

To be fixed ver:# 005, Keeping the Scene From Replaying

Immediate continuity, attribution guard, and the first context priority stack

Context Compiler V2 gave the model a session packet.

That helped, but it exposed the next problem fast: the model could still continue from the wrong part of the scene.

The packet had Soul, World, relationship state, recent events, and recent chat. On paper, that sounded like enough. In practice, the model could still grab the wrong anchor. It could treat old dramatic prose as current physical state. It could replay a completed beat because that beat was emotionally loud. It could treat its own character dialogue like the user said it.

The issue was not just memory anymore.

It was priority.

The model needed to know which part of the context was legally binding.

This pass started with continuity guards and ended with a stronger context priority structure.

The first fix was [IMMEDIATE CONTINUITY].

Recent chat was useful, but it was not strict enough. It preserved what was said, but the model could still treat it like background. I needed a section that told the model where the scene currently was.

Not just:

Here is what was said.

But:

Here is where the scene is now. Continue from here. Do not replay.

That created a cleaner split:

Recent Chat = dialogue and prose continuity
Immediate Continuity = action-state continuity
World Log = scene and plot continuity
Soul Memory = character and emotional continuity

That was the first useful improvement.

Then the attribution issue showed up.

The model wrote a character joke, then later acted like the user had said the joke. That is not long-term memory failure. That is local speaker ownership failure.

So I added the narrator attribution guard.

The rule was simple: user facts come from user messages. Character dialogue is not user dialogue. Narrator prose is not user action. The narrator cannot invent user thoughts, motives, actions, or dialogue just to smooth the scene forward.

After that, the continuity problem still was not fully solved.

The model got better, but it could still resurrect stale physical facts. It could bring an old object back into the character’s hand because the old prose was more vivid than the current state. It could invent unsupported elapsed time because that made the scene flow better.

That is when the priority problem became obvious.

A guard says:

Please do not mess this up.

A priority stack says:

This section outranks that section.

That is the stronger fix.

The test case was simple.

A scene had already moved past a phone reveal and into the kitchen. The user accepted food and added a new emotional beat. The next response should continue from the kitchen, react to the new user input, and leave the completed reveal behind.

Instead, the model pulled the phone back into focus.

That told me the context packet was not authoritative enough.

It had the information, but the model did not know how to rank it. Recent chat, status prose, world events, character emotion, and the latest user message were all competing inside the same prompt. The model followed the most dramatic thing, not necessarily the most current thing.

That is bad for RP continuity.

A long scene cannot keep replaying whatever detail was most intense two turns ago. If a character already saw something, reacted, set it down, and moved to the kitchen, that state needs to be current fact. It should not be re-discovered because the prose was vivid.

So the goal became narrower:

Feed less clutter.

Rank the state harder.

Make recent chat support the scene, not override it.

[IMMEDIATE CONTINUITY] was the first version of that fix.

The idea was to promote the latest action state above recent chat.

Recent chat still matters. It keeps tone, rhythm, and dialogue texture alive. But it is not always a safe source of physical continuity. Prose can be long. It can contain metaphors, old actions, sensory detail, and status text. The model may latch onto the wrong part.

Immediate continuity was supposed to give the model a short checkpoint:

Completed scene facts:
Aurora has already seen and reacted to the phone reveal.
She moved toward the kitchen for food.

Latest user input:
The user accepted food and admitted loneliness/attraction.

Continue forward.
Do not replay completed beats.

It tells the model where the scene is instead of asking it to infer everything from prose.

The attribution bug was a different failure class.

The model did not forget. It confused ownership.

A character made a joke. Later, the same character reacted as if the user had said it. That is small in one response, but dangerous for an RP engine because the same pattern can become worse:

character speech becomes user speech
narrator metaphor becomes world fact
user intent gets invented
the narrator moves the user to keep the scene smooth
the model reacts to its own setup instead of the player’s actual input

So the guard had to be compact.

Not a giant prompt lecture.

Just enough pressure to remind the narrator:

User facts only come from user messages.

Character dialogue is character dialogue.

Narrator prose is not user action.

Do not invent user thoughts, motives, actions, or dialogue.

That was worth doing because it targets a common RP failure without spending many tokens.

The point was not perfection. The point was cheap damage reduction.

The next bug was time.

The model invented a concrete duration that the scene never established.

That is the same kind of issue as attribution drift. It fills a gap because the prose wants to move smoothly. For normal writing, that might be fine. For a continuity engine, it is dangerous.

If the World Log or user did not establish a timestamp or elapsed duration, the narrator should not make one up.

Vague pacing is fine.

Concrete time claims need support.

So the time rule stayed compact too:

Do not invent exact elapsed time, timestamps, or scene transitions unless the user or World Log supports them.

Again, this is not a full time engine. It is a small guard against an expensive class of mistakes.

The bigger fix was the Context Priority Stack.

At this point, adding more guard text was not the right move. The model already had instructions. The problem was that the context did not clearly say what outranked what.

So the compiled context needed a priority order.

The shape became:

P0 Latest user message
P1 Scene State
P2 Do Not Replay
P3 Active World
P4 Active Soul
P5 Relevant Memory
P6 Recent Chat Excerpt

That changed the role of recent chat.

Recent chat became tone support, not the highest authority.

Scene State became the physical continuity anchor.

Do Not Replay marked completed beats.

World and Soul state outranked old prose.

The latest user input stayed highest because the user is the new action.

That is the important difference.

Before this, the model could see a lot of context and still choose the wrong anchor. After this, the prompt at least tells it how to rank the context.

A guard asks the model to behave.

A priority stack gives the model a hierarchy.

Recent chat also needed cleanup.

Full assistant responses are expensive, and for this engine they can be actively harmful. They contain style, sensory detail, status blocks, metaphors, old physical positions, and dramatic phrasing. A lot of that is good for reading, but bad as authority.

So recent chat had to become shorter and lower priority.

The direction was:

strip assistant status blocks
strip hidden state if it appears
cap assistant excerpts
preserve user messages more strongly
keep recent chat for tone, not state authority
avoid breaking UTF-8 while trimming

That matches the project goal better: fewer tokens, better outcome.

Not less context for its own sake.

Less useless context.

The system became less dependent on the model guessing correctly from prose.

That is the main improvement.

Instead of throwing a dense recent scene at the model and hoping it infers the current physical state, the app started pushing a ranked state brief:

latest user input is the new action
scene state is current physical continuity
completed beats should not replay
world and Soul state override older chat
recent chat exists for tone

That is a better shape for long-form RP.

It also gives debugging a cleaner target. If the model repeats something, I can check whether the completed beat was missing from Do Not Replay. If it resurrects an object, I can check whether Scene State had the current object location. If it invents time, I can check whether Time State was blank or vague.

The failure gets a place to live.

The priority stack improved the situation, but it did not fully solve physical continuity.

The same kind of stale-state bug still showed up: the model could continue as if an object was still central after the scene had already moved on. It could also invent unsupported time because it sounded natural.

That was the limit of prompt and context fixes.

The system was still asking the LLM to infer physical state from recent events and excerpts.

That is not reliable enough.

Object position should not be hidden inside prose.

Completed actions should not only exist as text the model has to interpret.

Current physical state should be engine-owned state.

That points directly to Scene State V1.

70ed66a — Add narrator attribution guard
38018a — Add narrator response integrity guard V1
TBD — Compact continuity and attribution context / Context Priority Stack V1

The third commit needs a hash check before publishing. The chat log records the work and the reported test results, but I still need to match it to the exact Git commit before this goes live.

Next is Scene State V1.

The current system can rank context better, but it still relies too much on the model reading prose correctly. Scene State V1 should move current physical continuity into the engine:

current_physical_state
completed_beats
do_not_replay
object_locations
pending_focus
time_state

That is the real next step.

The model should not have to infer that the phone is on the couch, the food is in the kitchen, or a reveal already happened.

The engine should know.

The narrator should continue from that.

chatgpt response

내 판단 기준은 크게 정확성, 시리즈 일관성, 과장 제거, 실제 로그 흐름, 홈페이지 톤이었음.

1. 원래 005의 장점

원래 버전은 글맛은 있었어.

Fighting Prose Gravity, Hierarchy of Truth, live grenade incident 같은 표현은 훅이 강했고, 블로그 제목처럼 눈에 들어오긴 했음. 원래 파일도 제목과 설명부터 “Prose Gravity를 깼다”는 식으로 잡혀 있었고, 그 자체는 마케팅성은 좋았음. 

근데 문제는 좋은 문장 = 좋은 devlog가 아님이라는 거였음.

2. 원래 005의 가장 큰 문제

원래 버전은 너무 승리 선언처럼 들렸음.

예를 들면 원래 글에는:

Aurora finally stopped resurrecting the phone.
She stayed in the kitchen where the state engine said she was.

이런 방향의 결론이 있었음. 그런데 실제 채팅 로그에서는 Context Priority Stack 이후에도 모델이 여전히 “phone like a live grenade”를 다시 꺼냈고, “three minutes” 같은 unsupported time도 만들어냄. 로그에서는 그걸 보고 prompt/context fix의 한계가 확인됐고 Scene State V1이 필요하다고 정리했음. 

그래서 원래 글은 실제 결과보다 더 성공한 것처럼 보이는 문제가 있었음.

내 기준에서는 이건 devlog에서 위험함. 나중에 읽는 사람이 “아, 여기서 해결됐구나”라고 이해하는데, 실제 흐름은 “개선됐지만 실패했고, 그래서 다음 구조로 넘어갔다”였으니까.

3. Context Priority Stack 설명도 원래 버전이 살짝 안 맞았음

원래 글은 Priority Stack을 이렇게 단순화했음:

P0 Latest User Input
P1 Latest Exchange
P2 Scene State
P3 Character / World Snapshot
P4 Recent Chat

근데 실제 채팅 로그에서 나온 설계는 더 구체적으로:

P0 Latest user message
P1 Scene State
P2 Do Not Replay
P3 Active World
P4 Active Soul
P5 Relevant Memory
P6 Recent Chat Excerpt

이 구조였음. 특히 Do Not Replay, Active World, Active Soul, Relevant Memory가 빠지면 005의 핵심이 약해짐. 실제 로그에서도 Recent Chat은 tone/dialogue continuity용이고 Scene State보다 낮은 우선순위라고 명시되어 있었음. 

그래서 나는 원래 버전보다 네가 실제로 설계한 priority hierarchy를 더 충실하게 반영하는 쪽으로 바꿈.

4. “Tail-based excerpt system”은 지금 005에 넣기 애매했음

원래 글에는 LLM Payload Inspector, tail-based excerpt, “앞부분이 아니라 끝부분을 먹여야 한다”는 이야기가 들어가 있었음. 

이건 좋은 소재일 수 있는데, 지금 005의 핵심 범위를 흐림.

005는 흐름상:

004 = session packet
005 = immediate continuity / attribution / context priority
006 = Scene State V1 or payload/debugging 쪽

이어야 깔끔함.

Tail excerpt, Payload Inspector, schema drift 같은 건 006이나 이후 글 소재로 빼는 게 더 좋다고 판단했음. 005에 다 넣으면 “scene replay 문제”에서 “payload inspector 문제”로 중심이 흔들림.

5. 새 버전의 판단 기준

내 기준은 이거였음:

1. 실제 로그에서 확인되는 순서인가?
2. 성공/실패를 정확히 말하는가?
3. 004에서 자연스럽게 이어지는가?
4. 006으로 넘길 문제가 분명한가?
5. 홈페이지 목록에서 000-004와 톤이 맞는가?
6. 과장된 제목보다 기술 흐름이 먼저 보이는가?

그래서 원래 제목인:

Fighting Prose Gravity

보다:

Keeping the Scene From Replaying

을 선택했음.

이게 덜 화려하긴 한데, 005의 실제 기능과 실패 사례를 훨씬 정확하게 설명함.

6. 내가 고친 핵심 문장 방향

원래는 이런 느낌이었음:

드디어 Prose Gravity를 깼다.
Priority Stack으로 현실 계층을 세웠다.
Aurora가 이제 제대로 kitchen에 남았다.

내가 바꾼 방향은:

Session packet만으로는 부족했다.
Immediate Continuity와 Attribution Guard를 넣었다.
그래도 model은 stale physical fact를 되살렸다.
그래서 Context Priority Stack으로 우선순위를 줬다.
하지만 이 역시 완전한 해결은 아니었고, Scene State V1이 필요해졌다.

이게 실제 로그 흐름에 맞음. 실제 로그에서도 Context Priority Stack V1 구현 후 [CONTEXT PRIORITY], [SCENE STATE], [DO NOT REPLAY]로 바뀌었고, Recent Chat은 lower-priority tone context로 정리됐다고 나옴. 

결론

원래 버전은 멋있지만 너무 이긴 것처럼 말함.

새 버전은 덜 화려하지만 더 정확함.

내 판단은:

Devlog는 멋있는 승리담보다 정확한 개발 기록이어야 한다.
특히 이 프로젝트는 “실패 → 진단 → 구조 변경”이 강점이라,
실패를 숨기면 오히려 글의 가치가 떨어진다.

그래서 005는 “우리가 Prose Gravity를 깼다”가 아니라,

우리는 recent chat이 authority가 아니라는 걸 확인했고,
context에 우선순위를 주기 시작했다.
하지만 아직 physical continuity는 engine-owned state가 필요하다.

이 방향으로 잡은 거임.

다음거 006 계획 ㄱㄱ


chatgpt response

006는 005가 끝낸 지점 그대로 이어가야 함: Context Priority Stack으로도 해결 안 된 physical continuity를 engine-owned Scene State로 옮기는 단계. 먼저 로그에서 Scene State V1 구간과 실제 커밋 범위를 확인해서, 006이 너무 넓어지지 않게 경계를 잡겠음.

로그를 보니 006은 내가 방금 첫 문장에서 말한 “Scene State V1”로 바로 가면 안 됨. 네가 그 방향에 반대했고, 실제 다음 흐름은 Latest Exchange를 high-priority로 올리고, 정확히 LLM에 뭐가 들어가는지 Payload Inspector로 확인하는 단계야. 005의 “Scene State V1 next”도 나중에 수정해야 할 가능성이 있음.

006의 진짜 중심이 잡힘: Payload Inspector로 실제 입력을 까보니, Latest Exchange가 “끝 상태”가 아니라 이전 assistant 응답의 앞부분을 먹이고 있었다는 흐름. 그래서 006은 Scene State가 아니라 “Opening the Black Box / Payload Inspector / tail excerpt bug”가 더 맞음.

006 계획 결론

006은 Scene State V1이 아님.

처음엔 005 다음이 Scene State V1처럼 보였는데, 로그를 다시 보니까 네가 바로 반대했음. 네 의도는 “모든 사소한 물체 위치를 추적하자”가 아니라, LLM에게 최신 대화를 제대로 먹이고 있는지부터 확인하자였음. 네가 직접 “latest chat을 제대로 못 먹이는 거 아니냐, feed order가 중요하다”고 밀었고, 그 다음 흐름이 Latest Exchange high-priority + LLM Payload Inspector + tail excerpt bug fix로 감. 

그래서 006 주제는 이게 맞음:

006, Opening the Black Box

Subtitle:

LLM Payload Inspector, latest-exchange ordering, and the tail excerpt bug

또는 조금 더 네 스타일:

006, Seeing What the Model Actually Sees

내 픽은 006, Opening the Black Box.


006이 다룰 정확한 흐름

1. 005에서 바로 이어지는 문제

005는 Context Priority Stack으로 끝났지만, 그것도 완전 해결이 아니었음.

핵심 문제:

The model still repeated stale physical state.

처음에는 Scene State V1로 가자는 말이 나왔지만, 네가 바로 선을 그음:

No, tracking every minor object is too much shit.
The latest chat is probably being fed wrong.
The order of feeding the LLM matters.

이게 006의 진짜 시작점임. 로그에서도 “full Scene State would become bloat”이고, 실제 문제는 latest chat이 너무 일찍 들어가거나, 과하게 압축되거나, summary보다 낮게 취급되거나, 마지막 assistant 응답의 end-state를 잃는 것이라고 정리됨. 

2. Latest Exchange를 high-priority로 올림

네가 제안한 feed order가 006의 핵심 구조임:

1. SYSTEM RULES
2. WORLD / STORY SNAPSHOT
3. CHARACTER SNAPSHOT
4. RELEVANT MEMORIES
5. LOWER-PRIORITY RECENT CHAT
6. LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY
7. CURRENT USER MESSAGE

이 순서가 중요한 이유는, 최신 exchange가 Soul/World/Memory 뒤쪽에 있어야 모델이 “이게 지금 장면의 가장 가까운 anchor다”라고 받아들이기 때문임. 로그에서도 [LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY]는 [RECENT CHAT] 뒤에 오고, 최신 assistant/user pair는 recent chat에서 중복되지 않아야 한다고 정리됨. 

3. Payload Inspector를 붙임

여기서 네 질문이 좋았음:

Is there a way I can get the text or token that our thing feeds the LLM?

이게 006의 중심 장면임.

Context Preview만으로는 부족했고, 실제 API call에 들어가는 최종 payload를 봐야 했음:

system message = narrator prompt + context compiler output
user message = latest user input

그래서 LLM Payload Inspector를 추가하는 방향으로 감. 요구사항은 final system message, current user message, context text, token estimate, provider/model/base URL을 보여주되 API key는 절대 보여주지 않는 것. 

4. Inspector가 진짜 버그를 찾음

Payload Inspector가 나오고 나서, 문제의 본질이 바뀜.

모델이 그냥 멍청하게 무시한 게 아니었음.

우리가 잘못된 anchor를 high-priority로 먹이고 있었음.

로그에서 명확히 나옴:

The problem is not that the LLM ignored the latest exchange.
The problem is that [LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY] is feeding the wrong part of the last assistant response.

즉, “final state를 보라”고 말하면서 실제로는 assistant 응답의 앞부분, phone reaction 시작 부분을 먹이고 있었음. 그래서 모델 입장에서는 high-priority section이 “Aurora already moved on”이 아니라 “Aurora is reacting to the phone”이라고 말하는 꼴이었음. 

5. Head vs Tail bug

006의 기술적 핵심은 이거임:

Latest Exchange was using the head, not the tail.

로그가 아주 명확함. 모델이 봐야 했던 중요한 끝 상태는:

Aurora turns toward the kitchen, tosses the phone onto the couch, and reaches for the takeout.

근데 실제 high-priority excerpt는 시작 부분인 “She doesn't take the phone right away...”를 먹이고 있었음. 그래서 모델이 “phone reveal이 아직 진행 중”이라고 따라간 거임. 

6. 실제 fix

006은 여기까지 다뤄야 함:

assistant excerpt = tail/end of last assistant message
user excerpt = full or capped latest user message
recent chat = older messages only

그리고 fix 내용:

- strip status blocks before taking tail
- strip hidden state blocks before taking tail
- latest assistant tail cap around 1200 chars
- latest user cap around 1000 chars
- avoid duplicating latest assistant/user pair in recent chat
- fix malformed time formatting
- keep Scene State out
- no object tracking

로그의 보고서에 따르면 이후 구현은 context_compiler.rs, commands.rs, tauri.ts를 바꿨고, latest assistant excerpt는 hidden/status 제거 후 tail을 사용하도록 바뀜. 테스트도 npm.cmd run test:rust, typecheck, cargo fmt, cargo test가 통과했다고 기록됨. 

7. 결과

이후 테스트에서 중요한 부분은 성공함.

모델이 phone reveal을 다시 꺼내지 않고, 음식/취약함/“love story” 농담에서 이어갔음. 로그에서 “fix worked in the important way”라고 정리되어 있고, tail/end of previous narrator response가 들어가면서 실제 current state를 포함하게 됐다고 되어 있음. 

다만 아직 남은 문제도 있음:

- debug payload UI가 context를 시각적으로 중복 표시함
- current user input이 [LATEST EXCHANGE]와 actual user message에 중복됨
- lower-priority recent chat은 아직 stale assistant head excerpt를 포함함
- relevant memories가 generic filler로 오염됨

이건 006의 마지막 “남은 문제” 섹션에 넣으면 됨. 


006 커버 범위

Start

005 이후:
Context Priority Stack이 도입됐지만 physical continuity가 여전히 흔들림.

1. Scene State V1로 갈 뻔함

4. LLM Payload Inspector 필요성 발생
5. Inspector가 실제 payload bug를 보여줌
6. Latest Exchange가 tail이 아니라 head를 먹이는 문제 발견

- memory pollution cleanup
- full Scene State V1
- regenerate/fix-last-response UI
- .mne portability

가장 좋은 건:

# 006, Opening the Black Box
## LLM Payload Inspector, latest-exchange ordering, and the tail excerpt bug

이 제목이 좋은 이유는 006의 진짜 변화가 “새 memory system”이 아니라, 우리가 실제로 모델에게 뭘 먹이고 있는지 보기 시작한 것이기 때문임.


006 작성 방향

감정 톤은 이렇게 잡아야 함:

005에서 나는 context priority를 만들었고, 좋아 보였다.
근데 또 실패했다.
처음에는 Scene State V1로 가려 했는데, 그건 너무 무거웠다.
내가 진짜 의심한 건 input order였다.
그래서 payload를 직접 까봤다.
그리고 문제를 찾았다.
모델이 무시한 게 아니라, 내가 wrong anchor를 high-priority로 먹이고 있었다.

이게 좋음. 이유는 네 의도와 제일 맞음:

Less tokens, best outcome.
Do not track every fork and chair.
Feed the latest exchange correctly first.
Inspect before overbuilding.


006 구조 초안

# 006, Opening the Black Box

## LLM Payload Inspector, latest-exchange ordering, and the tail excerpt bug

Opening:
Context Priority Stack looked like the right fix, but the same class of bug came back.
At first, the obvious next move looked like Scene State V1.
I pushed back on that.
Tracking every minor object would bloat the engine. The cheaper suspicion was simpler:
maybe the model was not seeing the latest exchange correctly.

What changed:
- Latest Exchange became high-priority.
- It was moved after World, Soul, Memory, and lower-priority recent chat.
- Payload Inspector was added to show the exact system/user payload.
- API keys stayed hidden.
- Copy LLM Payload made debugging possible.
- The inspector exposed the head-vs-tail bug.

Why I touched this:
The model kept resurrecting the phone.
But before adding more state, I needed to know what the model was actually seeing.

The black box problem:
Context Preview was not enough.
I needed the actual system message, context text, user message, and token estimate.

What the inspector exposed:
[LATEST EXCHANGE] said high priority, but it was feeding the beginning of the previous assistant response.
The beginning had the phone reveal.
The ending had the true current state.
So the model followed the wrong anchor.

The fix:
- assistant latest exchange uses tail/end
- strip status blocks
- strip hidden state
- cap latest assistant around 1200 chars
- cap latest user around 1000 chars
- dedupe latest pair from recent chat
- normalize malformed time text
- keep Scene State out

What got better:
The model stopped resurrecting the phone in the important test.
It continued from food/vulnerability/love-story joke instead of replaying the phone reveal.

What is still rough:
- payload inspector display duplicates context visually
- current user input appears twice
- lower-priority recent chat can still include stale head excerpts
- memory slots can collect generic filler

Covered commits:
1. 7985475 — Clean up LLM payload context and latest exchange
2. a543e1c — Improve chat navigation and payload copy feedback

Next:
Clean up payload display and memory pollution, or move toward Retrieval Layer V1 depending on commit order.


Important note about 005

005 currently ends with Scene State V1 as the next step.

Based on the next log, that is not fully accurate anymore. It was the immediate thought, but you pushed back and redirected the project toward Latest Exchange + Payload Inspector first. So later, I’d slightly adjust 005’s ending from:

Next is Scene State V1.

to:

The obvious next answer looked like Scene State V1, but before adding more engine state, I needed to inspect what the model was actually receiving.

That would make 005 → 006 much cleaner.
