**Document Title: **

006, Opening the Black Box

LLM Payload Inspector, Latest Exchange, and the tail excerpt bug

Context Priority Stack looked like the right direction.

It gave the model a hierarchy. Latest user input mattered more than old chat. Scene state and world state were supposed to outrank stale prose. Recent chat was supposed to help tone, not override continuity.

But the same kind of failure still showed up.

The model could still continue from the wrong physical moment.

At first, the obvious answer looked like Scene State V1. Track current physical state, completed beats, object locations, do-not-replay lists, and time state directly in the engine.

That sounded clean.

It was also too much.

I did not want Mnemosyne to become a giant object tracker. I did not want to track every fork, chair, door, shirt, phone, fridge, and microwave just because the model failed one scene. That would fight the whole point of the project:

Less tokens, better outcome.

So I pushed back.

Before adding more state, I needed to know what the model was actually seeing.

The next fix was not more memory.

It was inspection.

The context order became more intentional:

SYSTEM RULES
WORLD / STORY SNAPSHOT
CHARACTER SNAPSHOT
RELEVANT MEMORIES
LOWER-PRIORITY RECENT CHAT
LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY
CURRENT USER MESSAGE

The important part was LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY.

That section was supposed to sit near the end of the compiled context, right before the current user message. It should tell the model:

Continue from the final state of the last narrator response.

Then respond to the latest user input.

If older context conflicts with this, ignore the older context.

That was the cheaper fix.

Not full Scene State.

Not object tracking.

Just feed the model the most recent anchor correctly.

Then came the second piece: the LLM Payload Inspector.

I needed to see the actual text going into the API call.

Not a fake preview.

Not a simplified context panel.

The real payload:

system message = narrator prompt + compiled context
user message = current user input

Without that, I was guessing.

The phone scene was still the test case.

The previous narrator response ended with the character moving past the phone reveal and turning toward the kitchen. The next user message accepted the food and added a new emotional beat.

The next response should have continued from that.

Instead, the model acted like the phone was still central.

That made the problem look like bad memory, but I was not convinced.

The model may not have been ignoring the latest exchange.

It may have been receiving the wrong latest exchange.

That distinction matters.

If the model is ignoring correct context, that is a model behavior problem.

If the app is sending the wrong anchor, that is my bug.

The only way to know was to open the black box.

The inspector needed to show the exact LLM input.

It had to include:

final system message text
current user message text
compiled context
estimated tokens
provider mode
model name
base URL

It also had one hard rule:

Never show the API key.

This was not a fancy UI feature. It was debugging equipment.

If continuity breaks, I need to inspect the prompt and ask:

Is Latest Exchange actually near the bottom?

Did the final state survive?

Is Recent Chat duplicating old material?

Is the world snapshot too vague?

Is the current user message duplicated?

Is the model being told two different things at once?

Once I can copy the exact payload, the problem stops being mystical.

It becomes inspectable.

The inspector worked.

And it exposed the actual problem.

[LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY] was supposed to give the model the final state of the previous narrator response.

But it was feeding the beginning.

That is a brutal bug.

The beginning of a long narrator response often contains the dramatic setup. In the phone scene, the beginning was the phone reveal. The ending was the actual current state: the character had already reacted, moved on, and turned toward the kitchen.

So the app was saying:

Continue from the final state.

But the text it provided was:

She is still reacting to the phone.

That means the model was not randomly failing.

It was following the wrong anchor.

The high-priority section was high-priority garbage.

The fix was simple once the payload made the bug visible.

For the latest assistant response, use the tail.

Not the head.

The latest assistant excerpt should preserve the ending because the ending usually contains the current physical state.

The rules became:

latest assistant excerpt = tail / end of the last assistant message
latest user input = full if reasonable, otherwise capped
recent chat = older messages only

Before taking the tail, the app should strip machine noise:

status blocks
hidden state blocks
markdown status artifacts

Then cap safely:

latest assistant tail around 1200 characters
latest user input around 1000 characters
UTF-8 safe truncation
prefix tail truncation with ... so it is obvious this is the ending

That small detail matters.

If the excerpt starts with ..., I know I am reading the end of the message, not the beginning.

The same bug existed in a weaker form inside lower-priority recent chat.

Older assistant excerpts were also taking the head of responses. That meant even the lower-priority context could keep reintroducing old physical states.

It did not break the output after the priority fix, but it was still noisy.

The rule became clearer:

User messages should be preserved more directly.

Assistant messages should often preserve the final state.

That does not mean every assistant message needs a huge tail excerpt. But for continuity, the end of the assistant response is often more important than the opening prose.

A narrator response can start with atmosphere and end with movement.

The ending is usually where the scene actually lands.

The payload also showed a smaller bug.

Time formatting was malformed.

A line like Session startLate evening means two time fields got glued together. That kind of malformed state gives the model permission to invent time.

Then it starts saying things like “three minutes later” or “twenty minutes ago” even when nothing established that.

The fix was not a full time engine.

Just clean the formatting.

If exact time is not known, do not invent it. If two time strings get joined, separate them properly or only show one clean value.

Again, small bug.

Expensive consequences.

After the tail fix, the important test improved.

The model stopped resurrecting the phone reveal. It continued from the food, the vulnerability, and the latest user joke. That is what I wanted to see.

This was not a magical memory breakthrough.

It was simpler and more useful:

The model finally saw the right anchor.

The architecture was not wrong. The implementation was pointing the model at the wrong part of the previous response.

That is why the inspector mattered.

Before the inspector, I could only argue with the output.

After the inspector, I could inspect the input.

That changes the whole debugging loop.

The fix worked in the important way, but the logs still showed cleanup work.

The payload inspector visually duplicated context. The actual model probably was not receiving duplicate context, but the debug display made it look confusing.

The current user input also appeared inside LATEST EXCHANGE, then again as the actual user message. That is not fatal, but it wastes tokens and muddies priority.

Lower-priority recent chat could still include stale assistant head excerpts.

Relevant memories were also getting polluted by generic filler. Things like “neutral exchange added texture” are not memories. They are debug-flavored garbage. They cost tokens and teach the model nothing.

So the next cleanup pressure is clear:

make payload display labels clearer
stop duplicating current user input
use better assistant excerpts in recent chat
filter generic memory filler
keep the payload lean

7985475 — Clean up LLM payload context and latest exchange  
a543e1c — Improve chat navigation and payload copy feedback

Next is cleanup, not a new grand architecture.

The payload is now visible.

The latest exchange is closer to correct.

The model can continue from the right anchor when the right anchor is actually fed to it.

Now the problem moves to hygiene:

do not duplicate the current user message
do not let stale assistant heads pollute recent chat
do not store generic debug filler as memory
do not let the debug UI misrepresent the actual API payload

That is the next layer.

Not more magic.

Better plumbing.

Git 기반이라서 안 되는 게 아니라, 지금 사이트가 너무 raw HTML 방식이라 그런 것임. Cloudflare Pages는 Git repo에서 build해서 배포하는 방식이라, Astro, Eleventy, Hugo 같은 static site generator를 쓰면 블로그처럼 템플릿화 가능함. Cloudflare Pages도 프레임워크별 빌드 설정을 지원하고, build command와 output directory를 설정하는 구조임. 

내 추천은 이거:

Option A, 지금 당장:
현 구조 유지 + posts.json 만들기 + JS로 latest/prev/next 자동 렌더링

Option B, 장기 정답:
Astro로 전환
Markdown 파일만 쓰면 자동으로 블로그 페이지 생성
prev/next/latest/topic tags 자동화 가능
Cloudflare Pages에 그대로 배포 가능

내 추천

Astro로 전환이 맞음.

이유:

- devlog를 Markdown으로 작성 가능
- 공통 layout 하나로 모든 글 스타일 통일
- index 자동 생성 가능
- prev/next 자동 생성 가능
- 같은 topic/tag 글 자동 표시 가능
- RSS, sitemap, SEO 확장 쉬움
- Cloudflare Pages와 잘 맞음

장기적으로 구조는 이렇게 가면 됨:

src/
  content/
    devlogs/
      000-before-the-repository.md
      001-soul-update.md
      002-splitting-memory.md
      ...
  layouts/
    BlogPost.astro
  pages/
    index.astro
    devlog/[slug].astro

그러면 글 하나 추가할 때는 Markdown만 쓰면 됨:

---
number: "006"
title: "Opening the Black Box"
subtitle: "LLM Payload Inspector, Latest Exchange, and the tail excerpt bug."
date: 2026-05-29
project: Mnemosyne
tags: ["payload", "context", "debugging"]
---

본문...

그리고 사이트가 자동으로:

- homepage 최신글 목록
- 이전글 / 다음글
- 같은 태그 글
- 전체 devlog archive
- 공통 스타일

이걸 만들어줌.

지금 당장 할 다음 단계

내 기준 최선의 순서:

1. 현재 000-006 HTML은 그대로 백업
2. Astro 프로젝트를 repo 안에 세팅
3. 기존 devlog 000-006을 Markdown content로 변환
4. BlogPost layout 하나 만들기
5. index 자동 생성
6. Cloudflare Pages build 설정 변경

Cloudflare Pages 설정은 대략:

Framework preset: Astro
Build command: npm run build
Build output directory: dist

지금처럼 수동 HTML 계속 쓰면 20개 넘어가는 순간 관리가 지옥 됨.
기본을 튼튼하게 하려면, 여기서 Astro로 갈아타는 게 맞음.

내 장기 비전은 이거야:

rhygpu.dev = Git-based personal devlog / project archive
Raw HTML 사이트가 아니라, Markdown 기반 static publishing system

즉, 네이버 블로그처럼 글만 쓰면 자동으로:

결론

Astro로 갈아엎자.

지금 구조는:

devlog-000.html
devlog-001.html
devlog-002.html
index.html 직접 수정
style.css 직접 맞춤
하단 next link 직접 수정

이건 “웹사이트”라기보다 수동 HTML 묶음임.

장기 구조는:

Markdown 글 + Astro 템플릿 + 자동 생성 페이지

가 맞음.

Astro Content Collections는 블로그 글, 문서, 프로필 같은 구조화된 콘텐츠를 관리하기 위한 기능이고, Markdown/MDX 파일을 모아서 타입 체크, 자동 쿼리, 렌더링을 지원함. 공식 문서도 content collections를 “sets of content” 관리에 쓰는 방식으로 설명하고, 로컬 Markdown/MDX 기반 blog 같은 정적 사이트에 적합하다고 설명함. (docs.astro.build)

Cloudflare Pages 쪽도 Astro 배포는 npm run build, build directory dist 구조로 바로 가능하고, push할 때마다 자동 rebuild/deploy되는 흐름을 지원함. (Cloudflare Docs)

Best long-term architecture

rhygpu.dev/
  public/
    images/
    favicon.svg

  src/
    content/
      devlogs/
        000-before-the-repository.md
        001-soul-update-packet.md
        002-splitting-memory-in-two.md
        003-hiding-the-machine.md
        004-session-packet.md
        005-scene-replay.md
        006-opening-the-black-box.md

      projects/
        mnemosyne.md
        omniplanner.md
        pythagorean-harmony.md

    layouts/
      BaseLayout.astro
      BlogPostLayout.astro
      ProjectLayout.astro

    components/
      Header.astro
      Footer.astro
      PostCard.astro
      PostNav.astro
      RelatedPosts.astro
      TagList.astro
      CommitList.astro

    pages/
      index.astro
      devlog/
        [slug].astro
        index.astro
      projects/
        [slug].astro
      tags/
        [tag].astro
      rss.xml.ts
      sitemap.xml.ts

    styles/
      global.css

  astro.config.mjs
  package.json

이렇게 가면 앞으로 글 하나 추가할 때 HTML 안 만짐.

그냥:

---
number: "007"
title: "Cleaning the Payload"
subtitle: "Deduping user input, fixing stale excerpts, and filtering memory filler."
project: "mnemosyne"

tags: ["mnemosyne", "payload", "debugging", "memory"]

  - hash: "xxxxxxx"

    url: "https://github.com/RhyGPU/mnemosyne/commit/xxxxxxx"
previous: "006-opening-the-black-box"
next: null
status: "published"
---

본문 작성...

이런 식으로 Markdown만 쓰면 됨.

핵심 기능 설계

1. 자동 homepage

현재는 index.html을 매번 직접 바꾸고 있음.

Astro로 바꾸면 homepage는 자동으로 content collection에서 devlog를 읽고 최신순으로 뿌림.

Latest Mnemosyne devlogs
006 Opening the Black Box
005 Keeping the Scene From Replaying
004 Feeding the Model a Session Packet
...

수동 수정 없음.

2. 이전글 / 다음글 자동

각 글의 number/date 기준으로 정렬해서 자동 생성.

하단에:

← 005, Keeping the Scene From Replaying
007, Cleaning the Payload →

이렇게 뜨게 가능.

3. 관련글 자동

frontmatter에 tags 넣고 같은 태그 글을 자동 추출.

예:

Related:
- 004, Feeding the Model a Session Packet
- 005, Keeping the Scene From Replaying
- 006, Opening the Black Box

기준:

same project + shared tags + nearby number

4. 프로젝트별 페이지

/projects/mnemosyne

여기 들어가면:

Mnemosyne
A local-first AI memory engine for RP continuity.

Devlog timeline:
000 → 001 → 002 → ...

나중에 OmniPlanner, Pythagorean Harmony도 같은 시스템으로 들어감.

5. Commit list component

지금 commit section을 매번 HTML로 씀.

나중에는 Markdown frontmatter에:

commits:
  - hash: "7985475"
    title: "Clean up LLM payload context and latest exchange"
    repo: "RhyGPU/mnemosyne"

이렇게만 쓰면, CommitList.astro가 자동 렌더링.

6. draft/published 상태

글 초안은:

status: draft

published만 homepage에 표시.

나중에 초안 여러 개 만들어도 사이트에는 안 뜨게 가능.

7. SEO / share preview

각 글에서 자동 생성:

<title>006, Opening the Black Box | RhyGPU</title>
description
og:title
og:description
canonical url

나중에 Twitter/X, LinkedIn, Discord에 공유할 때 preview가 안정적으로 뜸.

8. RSS / sitemap

블로그처럼 운영하려면 RSS는 장기적으로 좋음.

Astro는 content 기반으로 RSS도 만들기 좋고, sitemap도 공식 integration으로 붙이기 쉬움. Astro 문서도 sitemap을 official integration 목록에 포함하고 있음. (docs.astro.build)

Cloudflare는 그대로 쓰면 됨

Cloudflare 산 거 잘한 거임.

다만 방향은 두 가지:

단기, Cloudflare Pages 유지

Cloudflare Pages
Framework: Astro
Build command: npm run build
Build output: dist

이게 제일 쉬움. Cloudflare Pages는 Git push마다 자동 build/deploy되고 preview deployment도 지원함. (Cloudflare Docs)

장기, Cloudflare Workers 쪽도 고려

Astro 공식 배포 문서 기준으로 Cloudflare는 새 프로젝트에 Workers를 권장한다고 되어 있음. 정적 assets + backend API + on-demand rendering까지 고려하면 Workers 쪽이 장기 확장성이 더 좋음. (docs.astro.build)

근데 지금 네 사이트는 블로그/아카이브니까, 일단 Pages + Astro static이면 충분함. 나중에 로그인, comments, analytics dashboard, API 붙이면 Workers로 확장하면 됨.

내가 권장하는 최종 방향

Phase 1: Astro static blog migration
Phase 2: Markdown content system
Phase 3: Auto index / prev-next / related posts
Phase 4: Project pages + tags
Phase 5: RSS / sitemap / SEO
Phase 6: Optional CMS or editor workflow

CMS는 지금 당장 필요 없음.

너는 GitHub/Codex/Cursor로 작업하니까, Git-based Markdown이 더 맞음.

나중에 필요하면:

- Decap CMS
- Pages CMS
- TinaCMS
- Keystatic

같은 걸 붙일 수 있음. 하지만 지금은 overkill.

Migration plan

Step 1, 새 Astro scaffold

현재 repo 루트에 Astro를 세팅.

package.json
astro.config.mjs
src/
public/

기존 HTML은 바로 삭제하지 말고 legacy/로 옮기거나 Git history에만 남김.

Step 2, 현재 style.css를 global.css로 이식

현재 디자인은 나쁘지 않음.

유지할 것:

- dark theme
- Fraunces title
- DM Mono body
- grid background
- section-label
- post-title
- prose style

바꿀 것:

- HTML별 중복 구조 제거
- layout component로 통합
- footer/nav 자동화

Step 3, 000-006을 Markdown으로 변환

예:

devlog-000.html → 000-before-the-repository.md
devlog-001.html → 001-soul-update-packet.md
...

각 글에 metadata 넣기:

number
title
subtitle
date
project
tags
commits
summary
status

Step 4, route 자동 생성

src/pages/devlog/[slug].astro에서 모든 Markdown 글 자동 렌더.

Step 5, homepage 자동화

src/pages/index.astro에서 latest posts query.

Step 6, prev/next/related 자동화

BlogPostLayout.astro에서 자동 계산.

Step 7, deploy setting 변경

Cloudflare Pages:

Build command: npm run build
Build directory: dist

Long-term content model

내가 추천하는 frontmatter schema:

number: "006"
title: "Opening the Black Box"
subtitle: "LLM Payload Inspector, Latest Exchange, and the tail excerpt bug."
slug: "006-opening-the-black-box"
project: "mnemosyne"
date: 2026-05-29
status: "published"
summary: "The moment I stopped guessing and started inspecting the exact payload sent to the model."
tags:
  - mnemosyne
  - context
  - payload
  - debugging
  - continuity
series:
  name: "Mnemosyne Devlog"
  order: 6
commits:
  - hash: "7985475"
    title: "Clean up LLM payload context and latest exchange"
    repo: "RhyGPU/mnemosyne"
    url: "https://github.com/RhyGPU/mnemosyne/commit/7985475596fcbcb1f7f2e22dbb7e809539745113"
  - hash: "a543e1c"
    title: "Improve chat navigation and payload copy feedback"
    repo: "RhyGPU/mnemosyne"
    url: "https://github.com/RhyGPU/mnemosyne/commit/a543e1c8651dbb9149716081fddfc1c0bc18ca65"

이 schema가 있으면 장기적으로 거의 모든 자동화를 할 수 있음.

중요한 판단

지금 갈아엎는 게 맞음.

이유:

1. 글이 아직 7개라 migration 비용 낮음
2. 앞으로 devlog가 계속 늘어날 예정
3. 수동 HTML은 이미 귀찮아지고 있음
4. 관련글/이전글/태그/프로젝트 페이지는 수동으로 하면 금방 무너짐
5. Git-based Markdown + Astro는 네 작업 방식과 잘 맞음

내 기준 best long-term vision:

RhyGPU becomes your technical archive.
Not just a blog.
A structured public record of projects, commits, design decisions, failures, fixes, and philosophy.

그러려면 지금 해야 할 건 새 글 쓰기가 아니라, publishing engine 만들기임.

Next move는 Astro migration.

chatgpt response

워크플로 파일을 확인해보니 현재 main의 YAML에는 이미 cache: npm이 없어. 그래도 Actions가 이전 커밋의 워크플로를 돌렸을 가능성이 큼. 장기적으로는 package-lock.json도 있는 게 맞아서, 이번엔 lockfile 생성 쪽으로 고정하겠음.

현재 파일엔 cache: npm이 없어서, 실패한 run은 예전 workflow로 돈 것 같아. 그래도 확실하게 새 커밋을 만들어서 Actions가 최신 YAML로 다시 돌게 만들고, 설치 명령도 lockfile 없는 상태에 맞게 명시해둘게.

업데이트 요청이 safety filter에 막혔음. 파일 내용 자체는 정상 GitHub Actions YAML인데, 명령어 문자열이 오탐된 듯해. 지금 repo의 workflow는 이미 cache 없는 상태라서, 해결은 “latest commit의 Actions 재실행”이면 됨.

현재 main의 workflow는 이미 이렇게 되어 있어:

- name: Setup Node
  uses: actions/setup-node@v4
  with:
    node-version: 20

즉, cache: npm이 없음. 네가 붙여준 실패 로그는 setup-node가 npm cache를 켜려고 했던 run이라서, 아마 이전 커밋의 workflow가 돌았거나 GitHub Actions가 최신 push 전 run을 보여준 거야.

지금 할 것

GitHub에서:

Actions → Astro Build Validation → 최신 run 선택

또는 실패한 run 말고, 내가 이후에 push한 최신 커밋 기준 run을 봐.

최신 workflow는 lockfile 없어도 여기서 실패하면 안 됨:

Setup Node
Install dependencies

왜냐면 npm install은 package-lock.json 없어도 실행 가능하고, cache 옵션만 없으면 setup-node가 lockfile을 요구하지 않음.

더 좋은 장기 fix

나중에는 네 로컬에서 한 번:

npm install

하면 package-lock.json이 생김. 그걸 commit하면 이후 workflow를 더 안정적으로 이렇게 바꿀 수 있음:

- name: Setup Node
  uses: actions/setup-node@v4
  with:
    node-version: 20
    cache: npm

- name: Install dependencies
  run: npm ci

근데 지금 단계에서는 lockfile이 없으니까:

npm install

이 맞음.

다음

최신 run에서 새 에러가 뜨면 그 로그 붙여줘. 그때는 진짜 Astro schema/layout/build 에러일 가능성이 높음.

Core thesis

Even with better context, the model will still mess up.

Cover in 007

Proposed 007 structure

# 007, Making Bad Outputs Recoverable

## UX comfort, regenerate/fix response, and the road to real branching

Opening:
After payload cleanup, the backend finally looked stable enough.
The next obvious backend task was Memory Hygiene V2.
But that was not what made the app uncomfortable to use.

What changed:
- Sticky chat header
- Return to Library always visible
- Copy LLM Payload feedback
- Regenerate latest response
- Fix last response with instruction

Why I touched this:
The model will still produce bad continuations.
No context system can prevent every replay, bad tone, time invention, or attribution mistake.
The user needs a fast correction loop.

UX Comfort Pass:
The app should not trap me inside a chat.
The payload copy button should confirm it worked.
Small fixes, but they reduce friction every session.

Regenerate / Fix Last Response:
Regenerate reuses the latest user message.
Fix with instruction adds a temporary correction:
“Continue from the kitchen. Do not replay the phone reveal.”
This instruction is high-priority for the next generation, not a permanent memory.

State safety:
The dangerous part is hidden state.
If the discarded response already changed Soul/World, regeneration can double-apply patches.
V1 needs either snapshot/restore or delayed patch acceptance.
This is where correction stops being just UI.

What got better:
Bad outputs are no longer dead ends.
The user can correct the model without manually editing the whole session.

What is still rough:
Only the latest response is handled.
Changing an older response does not create a true branch.
Later messages still belong to the old timeline.
That is not ChatGPT-style branching yet.

Covered commits:
1. a543e1c — Improve chat navigation and payload copy feedback
2. TBD — Regenerate / Fix Last Response V1

Next:
True branch timelines and debug log export.

Best boundary

007 should end exactly where the logs turn toward branching and logs:

Current response variants are not real branches.
Clothing consistency needs logs before more tracking.
Do logs first, then true branching.

That sets up 008 cleanly:

008, Making the Session Inspectable
Visible chat export, backend payload history, and why logs come before branch timelines.

or:

008, From Variants to Branches
Debug logs, payload history, and the first real branch model.

My recommendation:

007 = Making Bad Outputs Recoverable
008 = Making the Session Inspectable
009 = From Variants to Branches

That order matches your actual intent: make the app usable, then make it inspectable, then make branching safe.


you asked

message time: 2026-06-06 12:16:57

Ok give me a draft


chatgpt response

Based on the logs, 007 should cover the pivot from backend memory work to app usability: after payload cleanup looked stable, the next planned backend step was Memory Hygiene V2 + Retrieval Layer V1, but your TODO showed the real blocker was app comfort and correction workflow.  The logs then move into UX Comfort Pass V1, followed by Regenerate / Fix Last Response V1, and finally the realization that true branching and debug logs are separate later problems. 

**Document Title: **

007, Making Bad Outputs Recoverable

UX comfort, regenerate/fix response, and the road to real branching

After the payload cleanup, the backend finally looked stable enough to breathe.

The latest exchange was being fed from the right end of the previous narrator response. Recent chat was lower priority. The payload inspector was no longer lying to me. The model was finally seeing the right anchor more often.

The obvious next backend task looked like Memory Hygiene V2.

Less duplicate memory. Less irrelevant memory. Better retrieval.

That was all good.

But it was not the thing making the app uncomfortable to use.

The problem was simpler:

The app still made it annoying to recover from bad outputs.

And bad outputs are not going away.

This pass shifted from backend memory to app comfort.

First came the small friction fixes:

keep Return to Library visible while scrolling
make the chat header sticky
show feedback when copying the LLM payload

Small stuff.

But small stuff matters when the app is something I actually use.

If I have to scroll to the top just to leave a chat, that is friction. If I click Copy LLM Payload and get no feedback, I have to wonder whether it worked. If the app feels annoying during testing, I will avoid testing it.

That is bad for the project.

So the next feature was not smarter memory.

It was making the app less irritating.

Then came the bigger comfort feature:

Regenerate / Fix Last Response.

Because even with better context, the narrator can still mess up.

It can repeat a scene beat.

It can misread who said what.

It can invent time.

It can continue from the wrong emotional tone.

It can give an answer that is almost good, but wrong in one specific way.

The app needed a fast correction loop.

The payload fixes made the system more stable, but they did not make the model perfect.

That is the important difference.

A better context compiler can reduce failure.

It cannot eliminate LLM behavior.

So the question changed again.

Not:

How do I prevent every bad response?

But:

What happens when a bad response appears?

Before this, the answer was ugly. I had to manually retry, edit around the problem, or keep pushing the scene forward and hope it recovered.

That is not good enough for long-form RP.

The user needs to be able to say:

Regenerate this.

or:

Fix this response.
Do not replay the phone reveal.
Continue from the kitchen.

without destroying the session.

That is the real product need.

The first comfort pass was intentionally small.

The Return to Library button needed to stay visible. I should not have to scroll back to the top of a long RP session just to leave the chat.

The payload copy button needed feedback. If I copy the LLM payload, the app should confirm it.

Not with a giant modal.

Just:

Copied!

or:

Payload copied.

Then it disappears.

That is not a deep feature. It is just basic usability.

But it made the app feel less like a prototype and more like something I could keep open while debugging.

The rule was simple:

Do not touch the backend.

Do not change hidden state.

Do not change provider behavior.

Just reduce friction.

Regenerate is the first real recovery tool.

The behavior is simple:

The latest user message stays the same.

The app asks the model for a new narrator response.

The old narrator response gets replaced, or at least clearly treated as discarded.

V1 does not need full branching yet.

That matters because full branching is a different system. Regenerate is a practical fix. It gives me a way to recover from a bad continuation without building a whole timeline tree immediately.

The point is speed.

If the model gives a bad answer, I should not have to repair the whole session manually.

Click regenerate.

Get another attempt.

Move on.

Regenerate is useful when the answer is generally bad.

Fix with instruction is useful when the answer is close.

Sometimes the model gets the tone right but repeats an old scene beat. Sometimes it writes well but invents time. Sometimes it understands the relationship but forgets the physical state.

In that case, I do not want a blind reroll.

I want to tell it what went wrong.

Example:

Continue from the kitchen.
Do not replay the phone reveal.
Keep the same emotional beat.

That instruction should be high-priority for the next generation.

But it should not become permanent memory.

That is important.

A correction instruction is not character history. It is not Soul memory. It is not a world event. It is a temporary repair command for the next response.

If the app stores it as memory, the system gets polluted.

So Fix with Instruction has to live in a separate category:

temporary correction context

not:

character memory

This feature is not just UI.

The dangerous part is hidden state.

If the model already produced a response, that response may have also produced a hidden-state patch. It may have updated memory, relationship state, world state, or recent events.

If I regenerate and the app simply applies another hidden-state patch on top, the discarded response still affects the Soul.

That is bad.

It means a response I rejected can still change the character.

So regenerate needs state safety.

The preferred behavior is:

Before generation:
save a snapshot

If regenerating:
restore snapshot
remove or replace old assistant response
generate again
apply only the accepted replacement

That is the real difference between a chat UI gimmick and a memory engine feature.

In a normal chatbot, regenerate just means “give me another answer.”

In Mnemosyne, regenerate also means:

Do not let rejected state become canon.

Bad outputs stopped being dead ends.

That is the real improvement.

Before this, a wrong narrator response created pressure. Either accept it and continue, or manually fight the system.

After this, there is a correction loop.

The model can fail.

The user can push back.

The app can try again.

That makes the whole project more usable.

It also changes how testing feels. I no longer need every response to be perfect. I need the app to make recovery cheap.

That is a healthier expectation for an LLM-based RP engine.

This is still not true branching.

That became obvious immediately.

Regenerate / Fix Last Response works for the latest output. But if I go back to an older assistant response and choose a different version, the later messages still belong to the old timeline.

That is not a branch.

That is just response variant selection.

A real branch means each response choice owns its own future.

Something like:

User 1
  Assistant 1A
    User 2A
      Assistant 2A

User 1
  Assistant 1B
    User 2B
      Assistant 2B

If I choose Assistant 1B, the app should not keep showing the later messages from Assistant 1A’s timeline as if nothing changed.

That needs a branch model.

Not just variants.

The next issue was not only branching.

It was logs.

A clothing consistency issue showed up, and the right move was not to immediately add outfit tracking.

That would be another overbuild.

The better move was to inspect the evidence first.

I need two kinds of logs:

visible chat export

Only what the user sees. No hidden state. No engine junk. Just the readable RP transcript.

And:

backend payload history

The actual LLM inputs over time, not only the current payload.

That matters because consistency bugs can come from many places:

character profile
latest exchange
status block
memory section
world snapshot
model invention

Before adding more tracking, I need to know which layer is causing the contradiction.

That is the same lesson as the payload inspector.

Inspect first.

Then build.

a543e1c — Improve chat navigation and payload copy feedback  
TBD — Regenerate / Fix Last Response V1

The first commit is shared with the end of 006 because payload copy feedback belongs to both the payload-inspection workflow and the UX comfort pass.

The Regenerate / Fix Last Response introducing commit still needs commit archaeology. Search did not surface a clean commit message for it yet.

Next is making the session inspectable.

Regenerate makes bad outputs recoverable.

But branching and consistency debugging need better records.

The next layer should export:

visible chat log
backend payload history

Then the app can answer the question that keeps coming back:

Did the model invent this,
or did we feed it conflicting state?

That has to come before full branch timelines.

Bad outputs are recoverable now.

Next, the session needs to become inspectable.

Why 007 connects well to 006

006 ends with the payload finally becoming inspectable and mostly correct. The log says the high-priority latest exchange was previously feeding the beginning of the last assistant response instead of the ending, which caused the model to follow the wrong anchor. The fix was to use the tail/end of the assistant response, dedupe recent chat, normalize time, and avoid implementing Scene State or object tracking yet. 

Then the next log confirms the fix worked: [LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY] now contains the tail/end of the previous narrator response, the current user message is no longer duplicated inside Latest Exchange, and the output did not replay the phone scene. 

So the end of 006 is basically:

The model is now seeing the right anchor.
Payload debugging works.
Context order is good.
Recent chat is lower priority.

That makes 007 connect because the next question becomes:

Okay, now that the backend is stable enough, what still makes the app bad to use?

And the log directly answers that. The next recommended backend step was Memory Hygiene V2 + Retrieval Layer V1, but you pushed back because your discomfort was app workflow, not memory yet. The log says your TODO was mostly app comfort: sticky return-to-library, payload copy feedback, regenerate/fix response, branching, chat deletion/Soul rewind, character images, initial messages, and multi-character settings. 

That is the bridge.

The real connection

006 is about debugging what the model receives.

007 is about recovering when the model still gives a bad output.

That is a natural progression:

006:

007:
The app needs a correction loop when the model output is still wrong.

This works because 006 does not magically solve LLM behavior. It fixes a major payload bug, but the log still says memory hygiene is only “okay,” retrieval is basic, world snapshot is basic, and relevant memories can still include old phone/event content. 

So 007’s thesis is justified:

A better payload reduces failures.
It does not eliminate bad generations.
Therefore the app needs recovery tools.

- API key 절대 저장/노출 안 함
- export buttons
- “Exported!” feedback

로그에서는 llm_payload_logs 테이블과 list_llm_payload_logs, get_llm_payload_log, export_visible_chat_log, export_llm_payload_history 같은 command가 제안됨. API key를 저장하거나 export하지 말라는 조건도 명확함. 

What the logs revealed

008은 단순히 “export 기능 만들었다”에서 끝나면 약함.

진짜 중요한 건 export를 통해 문제의 위치가 보이기 시작했다는 점임.

로그에서 export 파일을 보고 확인한 문제:

- payload history는 작동함
- visible chat export도 작동함
- 하지만 new test session이 clean하지 않음
- stale Soul relationship과 stale World Snapshot이 넘어옴
- 새 debt collector scene인데 trust/affection이 높고 location이 Aurora's kitchen counter로 남아 있음

이게 다음 devlog의 후반부 핵심임. 로그는 “logging fix worked”라고 판단한 뒤, 진짜 backend quality issue는 fresh session이 stale Soul/World state를 상속한다는 점이라고 정리함. 


008에서 멈출 위치

그건 009가 더 적합함.

008은 여기까지:

1. 로그 export를 만들었다.
2. 이제 app이 보여준 것과 model에게 먹인 것을 비교할 수 있다.
3. 그 결과, 문제는 memory retrieval보다 stale scenario state라는 게 보였다.
4. 다음은 Fresh Session / Reset Scenario State.

The logs worked.

new sessions were not actually new.

로 가면 됨.

너의 상태는:

I don't want to guess anymore.
I want receipts.

Show me what the backend sent.

톤은 이렇게:

Before this, consistency bugs were arguments.
Maybe the model invented it.
Maybe the prompt caused it.
Maybe memory was stale.
Maybe the World Log was wrong.

After this, I could export the visible chat and payload history.
Now the bug had evidence.

이 방향이 좋음.


008 구조 초안

# 008, Making the Session Inspectable

## Visible chat export, payload history, and the first real state diagnosis

Opening:
Regenerate made bad outputs recoverable, but recovery is not the same as diagnosis.
If the character suddenly changes clothes, forgets the scene, or acts too warm in a new scenario, I need evidence.
Not guesses.

What changed:
- added visible chat export
- added LLM payload history export
- stored actual final payloads sent to provider
- added export buttons and feedback
- never store/export API key

Why I touched this:
The app needed two records:
1. what the player saw
2. what the model received

Visible chat export:
- user/narrator transcript only
- no hidden state
- no backend debug junk
- useful for reading the RP as a story

Payload history:
- multiple turns, not just current payload
- system message
- user message
- context
- provider/model/base URL
- token estimates
- no API key

What got better:
- can compare visible output against backend input
- can inspect whether stale clothing/state came from profile, latest exchange, status, memory, world snapshot, or model invention
- bugs become traceable

What the logs revealed:
- export worked
- visible chat was clean
- payload history contained multiple payloads
- new test session was not truly clean
- stale relationship and world snapshot leaked from old scenario

The real diagnosis:
The issue was not Memory Retrieval V1 yet.
The issue was fresh sessions carrying stale Soul/World state.

What not to overbuild:
- do not add full clothing tracking yet
- do not add object tracking yet
- do not jump straight to true branch timelines
- inspect first, then build

Covered commits:
TBD — Debug Log Export V1 commit, exact hash needs commit archaeology

Next:
Fresh Session / Reset Scenario State V1.


Commit coverage

현재 commit search로는 Debug Log Export V1에 해당하는 깨끗한 commit이 안 잡혔음.

확인된 후보:

1a13926 — branch system and fresh chat system being implemented

근데 이건 이름상 branch/fresh chat 쪽이고, 008의 pure logging commit이라고 보기엔 애매함. 

그래서 008에서는 일단 이렇게 처리하는 게 안전함:

1. TBD — Debug Log Export V1, visible chat export and LLM payload history

또는 아예:

거짓 해시 넣는 것보다 훨씬 낫다.


008에서 넣지 말아야 할 것

넣지 말 것:

- Fresh Session V1 구현 결과

008의 final thesis

A correction loop helps me recover from bad output.

But logs tell me why the output went bad.

Visible chat shows what the player experienced.
Payload history shows what the model was actually told.

Once both exist, bugs stop being vibes.

이게 008의 중심문장임.

message time: 2026-06-07 01:21:36

chatgpt response

008, Making the Session Inspectable

Visible chat export, payload history, and the first real state diagnosis

Regenerate made bad outputs recoverable.

That helped, but recovery is not the same as diagnosis.

If the narrator gives a bad response, I can retry it now. I can fix the last response with instructions. I can stop one bad continuation from becoming the end of the test.

But that still leaves the bigger question:

Why did the response go bad in the first place?

Before this, debugging continuity was still too much guessing.

Maybe the model invented the detail.

Maybe the prompt caused it.

Maybe the World Log was stale.

Maybe the Soul carried old relationship state.

Maybe recent chat had the wrong anchor.

Maybe the visible scene looked fine, but the backend was feeding the model garbage.

I needed receipts.

Not vibes.

This pass added exportable logs.

Two kinds.

The first was the visible chat log.

That is the clean transcript: what the user saw, what the narrator said, and nothing else. No hidden state. No backend payload. No debug junk. Just the readable RP conversation.

The second was backend payload history.

Not just the current payload in the inspector.

The actual history of what the app sent to the model over multiple turns.

That matters because a single payload snapshot is useful, but it is not enough. A consistency bug can build over several turns. A stale memory can enter the context once, then echo later. A world snapshot can be wrong from the beginning and keep poisoning the scene quietly.

So the app needed to remember its own model inputs.

Not forever as character memory.

As debugging evidence.

The immediate reason was consistency.

A character could suddenly change clothing. A scene could start in the wrong place. A new test could feel weirdly warm, like the character already trusted the user. The model could act like it was continuing an old emotional relationship inside a supposedly new setup.

Without logs, all of that is hard to prove.

You can argue with the output forever:

The model is dumb.

The prompt is bad.

The memory is stale.

The context is wrong.

The state engine failed.

Maybe all of those are partly true. But without records, it is just guessing.

So the next tool had to answer two questions:

What did the player see?

and:

What did the model receive?

Once both exist, a continuity bug becomes traceable.

The visible chat export is the story-facing record.

It should contain only the conversation as it appeared in the UI:

User:
...

Narrator:
...

No hidden state blocks.

No encoded payload tails.

No memory patches.

No provider metadata.

No token estimates.

No API data.

Just the RP transcript.

That is useful because sometimes I need to read the session like a normal scene. If the visible chat already contradicts itself, then the problem is in the narrative output. If the visible chat is clean but the next model response goes sideways, then the problem may be in the backend context.

That separation matters.

The visible log tells me what happened in the story.

The payload history tells me what the system told the model.

Those are not the same thing.

The payload history is the backend-facing record.

It should capture the actual final input used for generation:

provider
mode
model
base_url
system_message
user_message
context_text
estimated_system_tokens
estimated_user_tokens
estimated_total_tokens
created_at

The rule is hard:

Never store the API key.

Never export the API key.

Model name, provider, mode, and base URL are useful debugging metadata. The key is not.

The export should show multiple payloads, not only the latest one. That lets me check the progression of the session:

Did the stale object appear in Payload 1?

Did the relationship values drift in Payload 2?

Did the World Log start wrong?

Did a retry or fix response create duplicate context?

Did the model receive one version of the scene while the visible chat showed another?

That is the point.

The payload history turns the app from a black box into a record.

The logging fix worked.

Now I could export the visible transcript and the backend payload history, then compare them.

That changed the debugging process immediately.

Before, I would look at a bad output and wonder what happened.

After, I could check:

did the visible chat contain the contradiction?
did the payload contain stale world state?
did the context include old relationship values?
did the model receive conflicting scene information?
did the current user message get duplicated?
did the backend send the right model and provider?
did the app accidentally keep old scenario state?

The answer was no longer hidden.

That is a big shift.

It does not make the model smarter by itself.

It makes the failure inspectable.

The logs did their job.

They showed the real problem.

The new test session was not actually clean.

The visible chat looked like a new scenario, but the backend payload still carried old state.

The character was entering a new setup with stale relationship values and stale world context. A scene that should have started cold could inherit warmth from an older scenario. A new situation could still carry location details from the previous one.

That explains a lot.

If a new scene starts with high trust and affection, the narrator will write the character too warmly.

If the World Snapshot still says the character is at the kitchen counter, the model will keep pulling the scene toward that location.

If old objects remain in key objects, the narrator may resurrect them even when the new scenario never introduced them.

That is not a retrieval problem yet.

That is not a clothing tracker problem yet.

That is not a reason to add a giant object system.

The logs showed something simpler:

Fresh sessions were not fresh enough.

This is exactly why I wanted logs before more architecture.

Without logs, the tempting fix would be to add more tracking.

Track clothes.

Track props.

Track objects.

Track scene state harder.

Track everything.

But that can become bloat fast.

The logs showed that the issue was not necessarily missing tracking. The issue was stale state leaking into a new test.

That is a different fix.

If the session starts dirty, adding more memory only gives the model more dirty context.

So the correct move is not:

Add more tracking.

It is:

Make new sessions actually start clean.

That is a better diagnosis.

And it only became obvious because the logs existed.

The payload history was useful, but still not perfect.

Multiple payloads can appear from retries, variants, and fixes. That is expected, but the export needs stronger metadata to make them easier to map back to the visible chat.

The next useful metadata would be:

message_id
variant_id
source: normal / regenerate / fix
selected: true / false

Without that, I can still inspect the payloads, but it takes effort to know which payload produced the currently visible narrator response.

That is not fatal.

It is just the next layer of debug hygiene.

The visible chat tells me what happened.

The payload history tells me what was sent.

The missing piece is a clean bridge between them.

The logs also prevented another bad instinct.

A clothing issue showed up, but the answer should not be full outfit tracking right away.

Same with props.

Same with physical scene state.

A lightweight appearance pin might make sense later, something like:

[APPEARANCE CONTINUITY]
Current outfit: ...
Do not change outfit unless the user action or narrator event explicitly changes it.

But not before checking the payload.

If the payload already contains contradictory clothing state, fix the context.

If the payload is clean and the model still invents changes, then add a small continuity pin.

Inspect first.

Then build.

TBD — Debug Log Export V1, visible chat export and LLM payload history

The exact introducing commit still needs commit archaeology. The implementation checkpoint is clear from the test logs, but I do not want to invent a hash.

The logs worked.

And because they worked, they exposed the real next problem:

New sessions were not actually new.

The next fix is Fresh Session / Reset Scenario State.

Not full Scene State.

Not object tracking.

Not clothing tracking.

A smaller, cleaner boundary:

same persistent Soul continuity

or:

fresh scenario state

The user should be able to choose whether a new chat continues the existing Soul or starts with clean volatile state.

Relationship warmth, recent scene objects, stale location, active plots, and recent events should not leak into a new scenario unless the user wants continuity.

That is the next layer.

The session is inspectable now.

Next, it needs to start clean.

# 009, Starting Fresh Without Erasing the Soul

## Fresh scenario state, stale world cleanup, and the first reset boundary

Opening:
The logs worked.
That was the problem.
They showed that a new session was not actually new.

  - Start fresh scenario state
- reset volatile scenario state
- lower relationship warmth for fresh tests
- label chat mode clearly

Why I touched this:
A debt collector / random test scene should not inherit trust 130, affection 126, or Aurora’s kitchen counter.
If a scene starts dirty, the model writes dirty.

Persistent Soul vs scenario state:
The Soul can persist.
But location, active plot, recent objects, and relationship temperature may need a reset.

What got better:
- relationship values no longer start insane
- fresh test starts closer to neutral
- payload history and visible log still work
- the app can distinguish continuity mode from fresh scenario mode

What still failed:

- key objects still included wine glass, couch, phone
- active plot still sounded like default first scene
- threat severity was too weak
- time discipline still failed

- device/prop agency rule was too rigid
- it made the narrator too cautious
- character should be allowed to act naturally
- correct rule is not “character can’t touch props”
- correct rule is “do not control the user’s body/actions/decisions”

chatgpt response

# 009, Starting Fresh Without Erasing the Soul

## Fresh scenario state, stale world cleanup, and the first reset boundary

The logs worked.

That was the problem.

Once visible chat export and payload history existed, the bug stopped being a feeling. I could finally compare what the player saw against what the model received.

And the answer was ugly:

New sessions were not actually new.

A fresh test could look new in the chat window, but the backend still carried old scene state. The payload could still remember old relationship warmth, old location, old objects, and old active plots.

So the model was not always being stupid by itself.

Sometimes I was handing it a dirty brief and expecting a clean scene.

<div class="section-label">What changed</div>

This pass added the first real fresh-session boundary.

A new chat needed a choice:

```txt
Continue Soul continuity

or:

Start fresh scenario state

That distinction matters.

The Soul can persist. The character can still be the same person. Their long-term personality, core memories, trauma patterns, preferences, and emotional tendencies can remain.

But a scenario is different.

A new test should not automatically inherit:

old location
old key objects
old active plots
old recent events
old relationship temperature
old scene-specific emotional momentum

A character can be persistent without every new scene starting inside the last room.

That was the boundary I needed.

The test case was obvious.

A new debt collector or random threat scene should not inherit trust over 100, affection over 100, and a kitchen-counter setup from an earlier scenario.

If the payload tells the model:

Location: Aurora's kitchen counter
Key objects: wine glass, couch, phone
Relationship: high trust, high affection

then the model will write from that.

It will make the character warmer than she should be. It will pull the scene back toward the apartment. It will reuse the old phone or wine glass because the context says those objects matter.

That is not a mysterious LLM failure.

That is stale state.

The logs made that clear.

So the fix was not Memory Retrieval V1.

It was not object tracking.

It was not full Scene State.

The fix was simpler:

Make fresh sessions actually fresh.

This became one of those boundaries that seems obvious after the bug exposes it.

The Soul is long-term character continuity.

Scenario state is current session continuity.

They should not be the same reset lever.

If I delete all Soul continuity every time I start a fresh scene, the character becomes shallow again. That defeats the point of Mnemosyne.

But if I preserve every scene detail forever, the character gets haunted by old tests. Every new scene starts with leftover objects, stale relationship warmth, and active plots that no longer belong.

So the split became:

Persistent:
character identity
core memories
slow personality drift
long-term emotional patterns
durable relationship history, if selected

and:

Volatile:
current location
time elapsed
active plots
key objects
recent events
scene-specific memories
current relationship temperature, if starting fresh

That lets the character remain herself without forcing every new test to continue the last setup.

The first reset target was volatile state.

Fresh scenario mode should clear or neutralize things like:

world.location
world.time_elapsed
world.active_plots
world.key_objects
world.recent_events
recent scenario-specific memories
relationship warmth toward the user

Unless the user chooses persistent continuity.

That last part is important.

Sometimes I want the relationship to continue. Sometimes I want a fresh test with the same character but not the same relationship state.

Those are different user intentions.

The UI should make that visible:

Using persistent Soul continuity

or:

Fresh scenario state

The model should not have to guess what kind of session this is.

The user should know.

The engine should know.

The payload should reflect it.

The first fix helped.

The relationship state no longer started completely insane in fresh tests. Instead of carrying huge trust and affection values from old scenes, the new session started closer to neutral.

That mattered immediately.

A cold or threatening opener should not inherit romantic warmth by default. A character facing a dangerous stranger should not behave like she is continuing a vulnerable kitchen scene.

The payload became less contaminated.

The visible chat export still worked.

The payload history still worked.

The app could now prove that the relationship reset was moving in the right direction.

That was progress.

Not complete, but real.

The world snapshot was still not clean enough.

Even after the relationship reset improved, fresh sessions could still carry the default Aurora apartment setup:

Location: Aurora's kitchen counter
Active plots: Establish the first scene
Key objects: Half-empty wine glass; Couch with rumpled blankets; Phone

That meant the fresh session was only half fresh.

The relationship was cleaner, but the setting still dragged the model back into the default scenario.

That explains why a random opener could still become kitchen-counter based.

The user says something broad, the model looks at the payload, and the payload says Aurora is basically in her apartment with wine, couch, and phone.

So the model follows the brief.

Again, not pure model stupidity.

Dirty context.

The fresh session test also exposed another state problem.

Threat scenes were not scaling hard enough.

Trust could drop, but fear stayed too low. A scene could describe Aurora as terrified or cornered, while the numeric state still treated fear like a mild concern.

That mismatch matters.

If the state says fear is low, the next prompt may soften the character too quickly. If affection or desire remain active in a violent threat context, the narrator can start writing emotional tones that feel completely wrong.

A threat should not only reduce trust.

It should push fear hard.

It should suppress romance-adjacent values unless the scene strongly justifies otherwise.

That does not need a giant trauma system yet.

It just needs the state engine to respect severity.

The same tests also showed smaller prompt issues.

Time discipline still failed. The narrator invented concrete durations, like someone being “twenty minutes late,” even when the user or World Log never established that.

That kind of detail sounds natural in prose, but it is dangerous for continuity.

If the system does not know the duration, the narrator should not make one up.

The rule needs to be shorter and harder:

Never invent concrete durations.
Do not say minutes, hours, days, or years passed unless the user or World Log established it.

There was also mild POV leakage.

The narrator could write as if it knew exactly what the user felt, saw, or aimed. Sometimes that is a reasonable inference. Sometimes it crosses into controlling the user.

The better rule is not to make the narrator timid.

The better rule is:

Describe what the character perceives.
Do not claim the user's internal experience or exact action unless the user gave it.

This is where I made a wrong turn.

To stop the narrator from controlling the user's device, a device/prop agency rule got added.

The intention made sense.

If the user shows a phone, the narrator should not decide that Aurora takes it, unlocks it, searches it, and reads everything unless the user allows that.

But the rule was too rigid.

It made the narrator cautious around phones and props in general. Worse, it started pushing against the thing I actually wanted: a character who can act.

Aurora should be allowed to use her own phone.

She should be allowed to reach for things.

She should be allowed to interrupt, step forward, grab for something, refuse, challenge, retreat, escalate, or use her own environment.

The problem is not character action.

The problem is resolving the user's reaction without permission.

That is the real boundary.

Good:

Aurora lunges for the phone, fingers closing toward the edge of the screen. "Give me that."

Bad:

Aurora takes the phone from his hand, unlocks it, scrolls through the messages, and reads everything.

The first one creates pressure.

The second one steals the user's response.

That distinction matters more than a blunt device rule.

This pass exposed a pattern.

When the model behaves badly, the tempting fix is to add another restriction.

Do not touch the phone.

Do not infer objects.

Do not invent time.

Do not move too much.

Do not act too strongly.

But too many rules make the narrator worse.

The model starts writing like it is filling out a compliance form instead of writing a living character.

That is not Mnemosyne.

The engine should make the narrator smarter, not more timid.

The right fix is usually a cleaner boundary, not a bigger cage.

For fresh sessions, the boundary is:

persistent Soul continuity vs fresh scenario state

For action, the boundary is:

character may act, but user reaction stays with the user

For time, the boundary is:

concrete duration requires evidence

Those are cleaner than piling up mechanical restrictions.

Fresh Session V1 improved the worst contamination, but it did not finish the job.

Still rough:

default world snapshot can leak into fresh sessions
default objects can appear without being established
active plots can start stale
threat severity is too weak
time discipline still needs sharper wording
device/prop agency rule overcorrected
model quality may still depend heavily on provider choice

That last one matters too.

If the model is weak at instruction following, continuity, and nuance, the backend can only help so much. A cleaner payload gives the model a better chance, but it does not turn a weak model into a great writer.

Still, the backend had obvious cleanup left.

So the next move was not to blame the model first.

It was to clean the prompt and the brief.

TBD — Fresh Session / Reset Scenario State V1

The implementation checkpoint is clear from the logs, but the exact commit still needs commit archaeology. I do not want to invent a hash.

Fresh sessions got cleaner.

But the next diagnosis was bigger:

Mnemosyne was becoming over-prompted.

The system prompt had accumulated too much mechanical language:

agency rules
device rules
time rules
attribution rules
psychology explanation
hidden-state schema
status reports
context priority
memory instructions
relationship stats
world snapshot
recent memories
recent chat
latest exchange

The model was being briefed, but also lawyered.

That is why the same model could sound more natural in a simpler Janitor-style setup and dumber inside Mnemosyne.

The engine should not bury the narrator under rules.

It should brief the model, preserve continuity, and then let the character act.

So the next pass is Prompt Cleanup V2.

Not more restrictions.

Less machinery in the narrator's face.

Cleaner role.

Cleaner action doctrine.

Shorter prompt.

Better writing.

010 plan

The next devlog should be:

010, Stop Lawyering the Narrator

Subtitle:

Prompt Cleanup V2, action doctrine, and letting the character act again.

This title is better than something bland like “Prompt Cleanup V2” because the actual issue was not just prompt length. It was that Mnemosyne had started over-lawyering the model. Too many rules. Too much machinery. Too much “do not.” The chat log diagnosis says exactly that: the system prompt had accumulated agency rules, device rules, time rules, attribution rules, psychology language, hidden-state schema, status report, context priority, relationship stats, world snapshot, recent memories, recent chat, and latest exchange, making the model behave like it was filling out a form instead of writing a scene. 

Current chat-log position

We are here:

Finished:
009, Starting Fresh Without Erasing the Soul

Current next source:
_11.txt, starting at the “Mnemosyne is currently over-prompted” diagnosis

010 starts from:
Prompt Cleanup V2

009 ended with the device/prop rule overcorrection and the realization that Mnemosyne was burying the narrator under mechanical instructions. The next chat-log section turns that into an actual task: remove the device/prop agency rule, restore proactive character behavior, shorten the prompt, and keep only the necessary narrator/continuity machinery.  

Core thesis

The engine should brief the model, not shackle it.

Or more colloquial:

I built a memory engine and accidentally made the narrator scared to touch the furniture.

That should be the center of 010.

006 was “open the black box.”
007 was “bad outputs need recovery.”
008 was “bugs need receipts.”
009 was “fresh sessions need a reset boundary.”
010 is:

The brief itself got too damn stiff.

What 010 should cover

1. The diagnosis, Mnemosyne was over-prompted

The opening should say the backend was improving, but the writing quality was weirdly getting worse. Same model could sound decent in a simpler Janitor-style setup, but dumb inside Mnemosyne, because Mnemosyne’s prompt was full of mechanical pressure.

Important vibe:

The model was not being briefed anymore.
It was being micromanaged.

The log explicitly compares Mnemosyne’s bloated brief to simpler Janitor-style structure: character card, scenario, style, recent chat, user message. 

2. Device/prop rule was the wrong fix

This should connect directly from 009.

009 says the device rule overcorrected. 010 should expand the lesson:

The problem was never “Aurora must not touch things.”
The problem was “Aurora must not resolve the user’s reaction.”

The corrected doctrine from the log is strong:

The narrator may resolve character-side action and plausible scene motion.
If the action requires the user’s response, resistance, consent, or decision,
end on the attempt, demand, or pressure point.

That is the key design line. 

3. New action doctrine

This is the technical heart of 010.

Add section:

Action and Turn Control

The model should allow the character to:

speak
move
interrupt
refuse
reach
grab for something
retreat
challenge
escalate
use her own environment

But it must stop before deciding the user’s side. The log proposed exactly this compact [ACTION AND TURN CONTROL] section. 

This is also where the devlog can sound more alive. Example:

I do not want Aurora locked in a glass box.
She can lunge. She can interrupt. She can slam the door.
She just cannot decide that the user let it happen.

That has more rhythm and less robotic repetition.

4. Shorter POV and attribution rule

The old prompt had too much “ownership law.” Replace with the short version:

[POV AND ATTRIBUTION]
Write close third-person from the character's perspective.
User facts come from user messages.
Character dialogue and narrator prose are not user statements.

This comes directly from the Prompt Cleanup V2 requirements. 

5. Shorter time rule

Use the compact rule:

[TIME]
Concrete time only comes from user input or World Log.
Avoid invented minutes/hours/days.

This is better than lecturing the model about every failure mode. 

6. Compress psychology

This should be framed carefully.

Do not say psychology does not matter. It does.

The point is that the system prompt does not need a psychology essay every turn. The numeric psyche, needs, and trauma values already live in Character Snapshot. The narrator only needs a compact rule:

Emotional shifts should feel earned.
Micro-shifts are preferred unless the scene strongly justifies a sharper reaction.

That exact reduction is in the log. 

7. Keep hidden state, status, and context order

010 is not a rewrite-the-whole-engine post.

It should say:

I was cleaning the narrator brief, not ripping out the engine.

Keep:

hidden state format
visible status block for now
context compiler
payload inspector
WORLD SNAPSHOT
CHARACTER SNAPSHOT
RELEVANT MEMORIES
RELATIONSHIP
RECENT CHAT, LOWER PRIORITY
LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY

The log explicitly says to preserve hidden state extraction, status block, context compiler, and payload inspector, while cleaning the system prompt order. 

Stop point

010 should stop after Prompt Cleanup V2 and the “brief is mechanically working but still semantically inconsistent” check.

There is a follow-up question in the log: “Is the brief we are feeding the ai consistent and working rn?” The answer says the brief is mechanically readable and structured, with system rules, world snapshot, character snapshot, relevant memories, relationship, lower-priority recent chat, high-priority latest exchange, and current user message. But it still has semantic inconsistency because the World Snapshot can inject the default Aurora kitchen setup into random scenarios. 

That is a good ending for 010:

The prompt was cleaner.
The narrator had more room to act.
But the brief was still only as good as the state inside it.

Then 011 can be about deeper state/brief consistency, Fresh Scenario V2, or whatever the next logs support.

Do not include in 010

Do not fully cover:

- Fresh Scenario V2 implementation
- Threat severity cleanup in detail
- branch timeline work
- memory retrieval
- API settings
- roadmap P0 safety stuff
- alpha planning

Those are later or separate arcs.

010 is a prompt-quality devlog, not a roadmap devlog.

Style target for 010

Since you specifically called out burstiness, colloquial/slang, and varying sentence length, I’d write 010 with more rhythm like this:

Short punch.
Then a longer sentence that actually explains the technical issue.
Then a blunt line.

Not every paragraph needs to end like a verdict.
No more “That mattered” every five seconds.

Voice rules:

- Use some blunt phrasing: “too much crap,” “over-lawyered,” “timid,” “sedated,” “glass box.”
- Keep technical accuracy.
- Mix sentence length hard.
- Let some paragraphs be one line, but not every paragraph.
- Avoid repeating “That is / That was / The problem was.”
- Keep examples concrete.

Proposed structure

# 010, Stop Lawyering the Narrator

## Prompt Cleanup V2, action doctrine, and letting the character act again

Opening:
The logs fixed one problem and exposed another.
Mnemosyne was not just feeding stale state.
It was also over-prompting the narrator.

The app had become too careful.
Too many rules.
Too much machinery.
The model sounded less like a narrator and more like a clerk filling out a form.

What changed:
- removed device/prop agency rule
- added compact Action and Turn Control
- shortened POV/attribution
- shortened time rule
- compressed psychology
- preserved hidden state and status format
- kept context order intact

Why I touched this:
Same model could sound better in simpler RP setups.
Mnemosyne was supposed to improve continuity, not make the character timid.

The wrong fix:
The device rule tried to stop user-agency theft, but it made the character scared of props.
The real issue was not character action.
The issue was resolving user reaction.

Action doctrine:
Aurora can act.
She can reach, challenge, interrupt, retreat, escalate.
But when the user’s response matters, stop on the attempt.

Prompt cleanup:
Less lecture.
More role clarity.
Keep the engine state, but do not shove the whole engine manual into the narrator’s face.

What got better:
The prompt became shorter and more natural.
The narrator had permission to push the scene again.

What stayed rough:
The brief structure works mechanically.
But state can still be semantically wrong.
If World Snapshot says kitchen counter in a random gun scene, the model will still follow it.

Next:
Clean the state inside the brief, not just the wording around it.

Possible opening draft direction

The fresh-session pass fixed one kind of dirt.

Then another kind showed up.

Not stale memory this time. Not exactly.

The prompt itself was getting bloated.

Mnemosyne had started doing the thing I hate in AI systems: trying to solve writing quality by adding more little laws. Do not touch this. Do not infer that. Do not invent time. Do not move too far. Do not act too hard. Do not breathe unless the World Log approves it.

Okay, not literally.

But close enough.

The result was obvious. The narrator got timid. Aurora felt less like a person and more like a system carefully avoiding liability.

That is the tone I’d use for 010.

chatgpt response

# 010, Stop Lawyering the Narrator

## Prompt Cleanup V2, action doctrine, and letting the character act again

The fresh-session pass cleaned one kind of dirt.

Then another kind showed up.

Not stale memory this time.

Not exactly.

The prompt itself was getting bloated.

Mnemosyne had started doing the thing I hate in AI systems: trying to fix writing quality by adding more little laws.

Do not touch this.

Do not infer that.

Do not invent time.

Do not move too far.

Do not act too hard.

Do not breathe unless the World Log approves it.

Okay.

Not literally.

But close enough.

The result was obvious: the narrator got timid. Aurora started feeling less like a person and more like a system carefully avoiding liability.

That was not the point of Mnemosyne.

<div class="section-label">What changed</div>

This pass was Prompt Cleanup V2.

The goal was not to rip out the engine.

The hidden state still mattered. The context compiler still mattered. World Snapshot, Character Snapshot, relevant memories, latest exchange, lower-priority recent chat, all of that still had a job.

The problem was the narrator brief.

Too much crap had accumulated in front of the model:

```txt
agency rules
device rules
time rules
attribution rules
psychology explanation
hidden-state schema
status reports
context priority
memory instructions
relationship stats
world snapshot
recent memories
recent chat
latest exchange

Some of those were necessary.

Together, they were suffocating.

The model was not just being briefed anymore. It was being micromanaged.

So the cleanup target was simple:

Keep the engine.
Clean the narrator's face.

The annoying part was that the same model could sound better in a simpler RP setup.

A basic Janitor-style setup has a pretty simple shape:

character card
scenario
style
recent chat
user message

Not perfect.

Not as durable.

But it gives the model room to write.

Mnemosyne had better machinery, but the writing could come out worse because the narrator was being hammered with too many constraints before every response.

That is a terrible trade.

The whole point was to improve long-form RP continuity, not turn the narrator into a nervous intern who keeps asking legal if it is allowed to pick up a cup.

So the question became:

How do I keep the continuity engine without making the character scared to act?

The device/prop agency rule was the cleanest example of the problem.

It started from a real bug.

The narrator should not decide the user's device behavior. If the user shows a phone, Aurora should not automatically take it, unlock it, scroll through it, and read everything.

Fair.

But the fix came out too blunt.

It started making the narrator cautious around props in general. Phones, objects, movement, small physical actions. The rule was trying to protect user agency, but the side effect was obvious: Aurora got boxed in.

I do not want that.

Aurora is not supposed to be a statue.

She can use her own phone.

She can reach for something.

She can slam a drawer shut.

She can interrupt.

She can step forward.

She can grab for the phone.

She can retreat, challenge, escalate, refuse, push back.

The issue is not character action.

The issue is resolving the user's side without permission.

That is the line.

The replacement rule needed to be shorter and sharper.

Something like:

[ACTION AND TURN CONTROL]
The narrator may resolve character-side action and plausible scene motion.

If an action requires the user's response, resistance, consent, or decision, stop on the attempt, demand, or pressure point.

Do not decide the user's body, thoughts, speech, or compliance.

That is the doctrine.

Aurora can act.

The user gets to answer.

Good:

Aurora lunges for the phone, fingers closing toward the edge of the screen. "Give me that."

Bad:

Aurora takes the phone from his hand, unlocks it, scrolls through the messages, and reads everything.

The first one creates pressure.

The second one steals the outcome.

That distinction is way better than “do not touch devices.”

A character who cannot touch props feels fake.

A narrator who decides the user's response feels worse.

So the rule should hit the actual boundary.

Not the furniture.

The old prompt also had too much ownership law.

Useful idea, bad shape.

I still needed the narrator to respect attribution:

user facts come from user messages
character dialogue is not user dialogue
narrator prose is not user action
the narrator should not invent user thoughts, motives, or speech

But I did not need a whole lecture every turn.

So it got compressed:

[POV AND ATTRIBUTION]
Write close third-person from the character's perspective.

User facts come from user messages.

Character dialogue and narrator prose are not user statements.

Do not invent the user's thoughts, actions, dialogue, or compliance.

Shorter.

Cleaner.

Less courtroom energy.

The model does not need a legal seminar. It needs a clear line.

Same thing with time.

The system had already shown time drift: random minutes, hours, delays, concrete durations that nobody established.

The fix should not be a paragraph of panic.

It should just say the thing:

[TIME]
Concrete time only comes from user input or World Log.

Avoid invented minutes, hours, days, or exact elapsed durations.

Done.

No extra ceremony.

If the model still invents time, I can inspect the payload and tighten the rule later. But the first move should be compact discipline, not another wall of text.

This part needed care.

I do not want to remove the psyche.

That is the Soul.

Trust, fear, shame, affection, openness, attachment, desire, resolve, all of that still matters. The whole project exists because generic RP memory does not handle emotional continuity well enough.

But the narrator prompt does not need to re-explain the psychology foundation every turn.

The state already carries the numbers.

The Character Snapshot already tells the model what matters right now.

So the narrator only needs the writing-level rule:

Emotional shifts should feel earned.

Prefer micro-shifts unless the scene strongly justifies a sharper reaction.

That is enough for the prompt.

The rest belongs in the engine, not in the narrator's face every single generation.

This was cleanup, not demolition.

The hidden state stayed.

The visible status block stayed for now.

The context compiler stayed.

The payload inspector stayed.

The broad payload order still made sense:

SYSTEM RULES
WORLD / STORY SNAPSHOT
CHARACTER SNAPSHOT
RELEVANT MEMORIES
RELATIONSHIP
RECENT CHAT, LOWER PRIORITY
LATEST EXCHANGE, HIGH PRIORITY
CURRENT USER MESSAGE

I was not trying to make Mnemosyne dumb again.

I was trying to stop it from talking over itself.

The engine should prepare the scene.

Then the narrator should narrate.

Not read a 40-point compliance checklist before every line.

The prompt got lighter.

The narrator had more permission to move.

That was the big one.

A good RP narrator cannot be terrified of action. It needs to create pressure, let the character push, and then stop at the point where the user needs to answer.

That is the rhythm I want:

character acts
scene pressure rises
user response stays open

Not:

character thinks about acting
system prevents action
scene goes limp

The cleanup made the narrator feel less sedated.

Still controlled.

Still bounded.

But not trapped in a glass box.

Cleaner wording does not fix dirty state.

This is the part I had to keep in mind.

The prompt can be shorter, smarter, and less annoying, but if the World Snapshot still says:

Location: Aurora's kitchen counter
Key objects: wine glass, couch, phone

inside a random threat scene, the model will still follow the wrong brief.

That is not a prompt wording problem.

That is bad state.

Prompt Cleanup V2 made the narrator easier to work with, but the compiled brief still depends on the quality of the state underneath it.

Cleaner rules help.

Clean state matters more.

I keep running into the same trap from different angles.

A model fails.

The tempting fix is to add a rule.

The rule helps one failure.

Then it creates another failure because the narrator becomes too constrained.

Do that enough times and the prompt turns into sludge.

This pass was me pulling back.

Not every bug deserves another paragraph in the system prompt.

Some bugs need state cleanup.

Some need better payload order.

Some need diagnostics.

Some need a small, sharp rule.

And some are just model quality.

The trick is knowing which one I am looking at before I duct-tape another command onto the narrator.

TBD — Prompt Cleanup V2, action doctrine, and narrator prompt simplification

The implementation checkpoint is clear from the logs, but the exact commit still needs commit archaeology. I do not want to invent a hash.

The narrator brief is cleaner now.

Good.

But the next problem is still sitting underneath it:

The brief can be mechanically clean and semantically wrong.

If the context sections are well ordered but the World Snapshot carries stale scenario data, the model still gets a bad setup. If relationship state is neutral but active plots are old, the scene still bends in the wrong direction. If relevant memories are technically formatted but emotionally irrelevant, the model still wastes attention.

So the next layer is not more prompt cleanup.

It is brief consistency.

The question becomes:

Is the state inside the payload actually true for this scene?

Not just:

Is the payload formatted nicely?

Mnemosyne should not lawyer the narrator.

It should brief the narrator cleanly.

Then get out of the way.
